arXiv:2402.00849v5 [cs.LG] 19 Jul 2025

Burak Varıcı, Emre Acartürk, Karthikeyan Shanmugam, Abhishek Kumar, and Ali Tajer

Score-based Causal Representation Learning:
Linear and General Transformations

Burak Varıcı bvarici@andrew.cmu.edu ^†^†thanks: Equal contribution^†^†thanks: The work was
completed when B.V. was at Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute. Affiliation: Machine Learning
Department Affiliation: Carnegie Mellon University, Pittsburgh, PA 15213, USA    Emre Acartürk^1^1
footnotemark: 1 acarte@rpi.edu Affiliation: Electrical, Computer, and Systems Engineering 
Affiliation: Rensselaer Polytechnic Institute, Troy, NY 12180, USA    Karthikeyan Shanmugam
karthikeyanvs@google.com Affiliation: Google DeepMind India Affiliation: Bengaluru 560043, India   
Abhishek Kumar abhishek.mlwork@gmail.com ^†^†thanks: Contributions of A.K. were done when the
author was at Google Brain. Affiliation: Amazon AGI, USA    Ali Tajer tajer@ecse.rpi.edu 
Affiliation: Electrical, Computer, and Systems Engineering Affiliation: Rensselaer Polytechnic
Institute, Troy, NY 12180, USA

Abstract

This paper addresses intervention-based causal representation learning (CRL) under a general
nonparametric latent causal model and an unknown transformation that maps the latent variables to
the observed variables. Linear and general transformations are investigated. The paper addresses
both the identifiability and achievability aspects. Identifiability refers to determining
algorithm-agnostic conditions that ensure the recovery of the true latent causal variables and the
underlying latent causal graph. Achievability refers to the algorithmic aspects and addresses
designing algorithms that achieve identifiability guarantees. By drawing novel connections between
score functions (i.e., the gradients of the logarithm of density functions) and CRL, this paper
designs a score-based class of algorithms that ensures both identifiability and achievability.
First, the paper focuses on linear transformations and shows that one stochastic hard intervention
per node suffices to guarantee identifiability. It also provides partial identifiability guarantees
for soft interventions, including identifiability up to mixing with parents for general causal
models and perfect recovery of the latent graph for sufficiently nonlinear causal models. Secondly,
it focuses on general transformations and demonstrates that two stochastic hard interventions per
node are sufficient for identifiability. This is achieved by defining a differentiable loss
function whose global optima ensure identifiability for general CRL. Notably, one does not need to
know which pair of interventional environments has the same node intervened. Finally, the
theoretical results are empirically validated via experiments on structured synthetic data and
image data.

^†^†heading: 26 2025 1- 2/24; Revised 3/25 5/25 24-0194^†^†shortheadings: Score-based Causal
Representation Learning: Linear and General Transformations / Varıcı, Acartürk, Shanmugam, Kumar,
and Tajer^†^†editor: Yoshua Bengio

keywords

causal representation learning, causality, interventions

1 Overview

Causal representation learning (CRL) aims to form a causal understanding of the world by learning
appropriate representations that support causal interventions, reasoning, and planning (Schölkopf
et al., 2021). Specifically, CRL considers a data-generating process in which high-level latent
causally-related variables are mapped to low-level, generally high-dimensional observed data
through an unknown transformation. Formally, consider a causal Bayesian network (Pearl, 2009)
encoded by a directed acyclic graph (DAG) 𝒢\mathcal{G} with nn nodes and generating causal random
variables 𝐙≜[Z1,…,Zn]⊤\mathbf{Z}\triangleq[Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}]^{\top}. These random variables are
transformed by an unknown function g:ℝn→ℝdg:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d} to generate the
dd-dimensional observed random variables 𝐗≜[X1,…,Xd]⊤\mathbf{X}\triangleq[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]^{\top}
according to:

 𝐗=g⁡(𝐙).\mathbf{X}=g(\mathbf{Z})\ .   (1)

CRL is the process of using the observed data 𝐗\mathbf{X} and recovering (i) the causal structure 𝒢
\mathcal{G} and (ii) the latent causal variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z}. Achieving these implicitly involves
another objective of recovering the unknown transformation gg as well. Addressing CRL consists of
two central questions:

  • •
   
    Identifiability, which refers to determining the necessary and sufficient conditions under
    which 𝒢\mathcal{G} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z} can be recovered. The scope of identifiability (e.g.,
    perfect or partial) critically depends on the extent of information available about the data,
    the underlying causal structure, and the transformation. The nature of the identifiability
    results can be algorithm-agnostic and non-constructive without specifying how to recover 𝒢\
    mathcal{G} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z}. In particular, this is the case when considering CRL under a
    general transform gg without parametric assumptions. Furthermore, the study of identifiability
    also investigates necessary conditions, e.g., which type of data is required for
    identifiability, regardless of the algorithmic approach.

  • •
   
    Achievability, which complements identifiability and pertains to designing algorithms that can
    recover 𝒢\mathcal{G} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z} while maintaining identifiability guarantees.
    Achievability hinges on forming reliable estimates for the transformation gg.

CRL from interventions.

Identifiability is known to be impossible without additional supervision or sufficient statistical
diversity among the samples of the observed data 𝐗\mathbf{X}. As shown in (Hyvärinen and Pajunen,
1999; Locatello et al., 2019), this is the case even for the simpler settings in which the latent
variables are statistically independent (i.e., graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} has no edges). On the other hand,
using data generated under interventions in addition to the observational data generated by the
underlying latent model creates useful statistical diversity. Specifically, an intervention on a
set of causal variables alters the causal mechanisms that generate those variables. Note that these
causal mechanisms capture the effect of parents on the child variable. Such interventions, even
when imposing sparse changes in the statistical models, create variations in the observed data
sufficient for learning latent causal representations. This has led to CRL via intervention as an
important class of CRL problems, which in its general form has remained an open problem (Schölkopf
et al., 2021). This paper addresses this open problem by drawing a novel connection to score
functions, i.e., the gradients of the logarithm of density functions.

We note that we use the interventions as a weak form of supervision via having access to only the
pair of distributions before and after an intervention. Such supervision can be quite flexible and
bodes well for practical applications in genomics (Tejada-Lapuerta et al., 2025) and robotics (Lee
et al., 2021). For instance, genomics experiments often involve interventions, which can be modeled
as deterministic or stochastic interventions, depending on the experimental mechanism. In robotics,
let us consider the causal variables to be the joint angles of a robotic arm. A stochastic
intervention corresponds to setting the joint angle to take values within a suitable support. We
note that the interventions here can be soft since the feasible support for the joint may still
depend on the other joint angles after the intervention. In this setting, the observations are
simply images of the entire arm captured by a camera positioned at a specific location.

Contributions.

This paper provides both identifiability and achievability results for CRL from interventions under
general latent causal models and general transformations. We establish these results by uncovering
hitherto unknown connections between score functions (i.e., the gradients of the logarithm of
density functions) and CRL. We leverage these connections to design CRL algorithms that serve as
constructive proofs for the identifiability and achievability results. We do not make any
parametric assumption on the latent causal model, i.e., the relationships among elements of 𝐙\
mathbf{Z} take any arbitrary form. For the transformation gg, we consider a diffeomorphism (i.e.,
bijective such that both gg and g−1g^{-1} are continuously differentiable) onto its image. Our
results are categorized into two main groups based on the form of gg: (i) linear transformation,
and (ii) general (nonparametric) transformation. We consider both stochastic hard and soft
interventions, and our contributions are summarized below.

Linear transformations.

We first focus on linear transformations and investigate various extents of results (perfect and
partial) given one intervention per node. In this setting, we consider both hard and soft
interventions.

  • ✔
   
    On identifiability from hard interventions, we show that one hard intervention per node
    suffices to guarantee perfect identifiability (Theorem 16).

  • ✔
   
    On identifiability from soft interventions, we show that one soft intervention per node
    suffices to guarantee identifiability up to ancestors – transitive closure of the latent DAG is
    recovered and latent variables are recovered up to a linear function of their ancestors (
    Theorem 14).

  • ✔
   
    While establishing these results, we also establish partial identifiability of a latent
    variable given an intervention on single node (Theorem 11).

  • ✔
   
    We further tighten the previous results and show that when the latent causal model is
    sufficiently nonlinear, perfect DAG recovery becomes possible using soft interventions.
    Furthermore, we recover a latent representation that is Markov with respect to the latent DAG,
    preserving the true conditional independence relationships (Theorem 18).

  • ✔
   
    On achievability, we design an algorithm referred to as Linear Score-based Causal Latent E
    stimation via Interventions (LSCALE-I), which achieves the identifiability guarantees under
    both soft and hard interventions. LSCALE-I first forms an encoder estimate using correlation
    matrices of the observed score differences and then uses the learned encoder to estimate the
    transitive closure of the latent DAG. Then, in the case of hard interventions, it refines these
    outputs without requiring any conditional independence test.

General transformations.

In this setting, we do not have any restriction on the transformation from the latent space to the
observed space. In this general setting, our contributions are as follows.

  • ✔
   
    On identifiability, we show that observational data and two distinct hard interventions per
    node suffice to guarantee perfect identifiability (Theorem 30). This result generalizes the
    recent results in the literature in two ways. First, we do not require the commonly adopted
    faithfulness assumption on latent causal models. Secondly, we assume the learner does not know
    which pair of environments intervene on the same node. While proving these results, we also
    establish identifiability of a single variable given two hard interventions only for the said
    variable (Theorem 23).

  • ✔
   
    More importantly, on achievability, we design the first provably correct algorithm that
    recovers 𝒢\mathcal{G} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z} perfectly. This algorithm is referred to as Generalized S
    core-based Causal Latent Estimation via Interventions (GSCALE-I). We note that GSCALE-I
    requires only the score functions of observed variables as its inputs and computes those of the
    latent variables by leveraging the Jacobian of the decoders.

  • ✔
   
    We also establish new results that shed light on the role of observational data. Specifically,
    when two interventional environments per node are given in pairs, observational data is only
    needed for recovering the latent DAG (Theorem 26). Furthermore, we show that observational data
    can be dispensed with under a weak faithfulness condition on the latent causal model (Theorem 
    27). Remarkably, our results are tight in terms of the identifiability objectives, which we
    will discuss in Section 3.4.

  • ✔
   
    Finally, we show that extrapolation to unseen combinations of single-node interventions can be
    achieved without performing CRL, just by using score functions of the observed space (Section 
    6.4).

Before describing our novel methodology, we recall the general approach to solving CRL. Recovering
the latent causal variables hinges on finding the inverse of gg based on the observed data 𝐗\mathbf
{X}, which facilitates recovering 𝐙\mathbf{Z} via 𝐙=g−1​(𝐗)\mathbf{Z}=g^{-1}(\mathbf{X}). In other
words, referring to (g−1,g)(g^{-1},g) as the true encoder-decoder pair, we search for an encoder
that takes observed variables back to the true latent space. A valid encoder should necessarily
have an associated decoder to ensure a perfect reconstruction of observed variables from estimated
latent variables. However, infinitely many valid encoder-decoder pairs satisfy the reconstruction
property. The purpose of using interventions is to inject variations into the observed data, which
can help us distinguish among these solutions.

Score-based methodology.

We start by showing that an intervention on a latent node induces changes only in the score
function’s coordinates corresponding to the intervened node and its parents. This is because the
intervention changes only the causal mechanism (i.e., conditional distribution) of the intervened
node, which is a function of the latent node and its parents. Furthermore, when we consider two
hard interventions on the same node, such changes will be limited only to the intervened node
(parents intact). This implies that score changes of the latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} are generally
sparse across different environments. Furthermore, these score changes contain all the information
about the latent causal structure. Motivated by these key properties, we formalize a score-based
CRL framework, based on which we design provably correct distinct algorithms, LSCALE-I and
GSCALE-I, for linear and general transformations, respectively, presented in Section 5 and Section 
6. We briefly describe the key technique in GSCALE-I for general transformation via two
interventions. LSCALE-I involves exploiting the linearity of the transformation to achieve
identifiability using only one intervention per node.

Algorithm sketch for general transformations.

Consider two interventional environments in which the same node is intervened on. As described in
the preceding paragraph, we show that the score functions of the latent variables under these two
environments differ only at the coordinate of the intervened node. Subsequently, the key idea of
the score-based framework is that tracing these sparse changes in the score functions of the latent
variables can guide finding reliable estimates for the inverse of the transformation gg, which in
turn facilitates estimating 𝐙\mathbf{Z}. In particular, we will seek encoders that result in sparse
score variations among the estimated latent variables, thereby matching the properties of the true
latent variables. To this end, we consider a pair of interventional environments for each node and
find an encoder that minimizes the variations in the score function across all pairs. We show that
the encoder obtained via this procedure perfectly recovers 𝒢\mathcal{G} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z}. An
important process in this methodology is projecting the score changes in observed data to the
latent space so that we do not need to estimate latent scores for each possible encoder. We show
that this can be done by multiplying the observed score difference by the Jacobian of the decoder
associated with the encoder. Therefore, recovering ZiZ_{i} is facilitated by solving the following
problem:

 For i-th int. env. pair :min∥𝔼[|Jac. of decoder×(score difference of 𝐗)∥]−𝐞i∥2,\mbox{For
 $i$-th int. env. pair :}\;\;\min\Big\|{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|\text{Jac. of decoder}\times\big   (2)
 (\text{score difference of $\mathbf{X}$}\big)\big\|\Big]-\mathbf{e}_{i}\Big\|^{2}\ ,

in which 𝐞i\mathbf{e}_{i} denotes ii-th standard basis vector, reflecting the sparsity property.
Score differences of observed variables are computed across two environments with the same
intervened node. When we have two interventions for each latent variable, we solve this problem for
i∈[n]i\in[n] simultaneously to recover complete 𝐙\mathbf{Z}, and subsequently, the graph 𝒢\mathcal
{G}. Finally, the minimization is performed over the set of valid encoder-decoder pairs that ensure
perfect reconstruction of 𝐗\mathbf{X}.

Refer to caption
Figure 1: An overview of LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I algorithms. Corresponding to each latent variable
ZiZ_{i}, there are two interventional mechanisms, denoted by red and blue. For each pair of
environments, the score functions of observed variables are fed into the GSCALE-I algorithm. Then,
GSCALE-I uses this input to compute latent score differences for a given encoder and returns the
encoder that minimizes these latent score differences. This encoder is used to compute estimates 𝒢^
\hat{\mathcal{G}} and 𝐙^\hat{\mathbf{Z}}.

Organization.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section 2 provides an overview of the literature
with the main focus on CRL via interventions. Section 3 provides the preliminaries for formulating
the problem and specifies the notations and definitions used throughout the paper. Section 4
establishes the properties of score functions under interventions and presents the key lemmas and
their implications, which will be used in our CRL framework. We present our CRL algorithms and
results for linear transformations in Section 5, and general transformations in Section 6. Proofs
of the building blocks of the results are presented in the main body of the paper, and the rest of
the proofs are provided in the appendices. In Section 7, we empirically assess the performance of
the proposed CRL algorithms for recovering the latent causal variables and the latent causal graph
on both structured synthetic data, biological data, and image data. Section 8 concludes the paper
with a discussion of the results and the future directions.

2 Related Work

In this paper, we study CRL in different interventional environments where the interventions act on
the latent space. We first provide an overview of the literature that investigates CRL from
interventional data, with the main results of the most closely related work summarized in Table 1
and Table 2. Then, we discuss the other relevant lines of work.

Interventional causal representation learning.

The majority of the rapidly growing literature on CRL from interventions focuses on parametric
settings, i.e., a parametric form is assumed for the latent model, the transformation, or both.
Among the related studies, that in (Ahuja et al., 2023) mainly considers polynomial transformations
without restrictions on the latent causal model and shows identifiability under deterministic do
interventions. It also establishes identifiability under general transformations, albeit requiring
a combinatorial number of do interventions. Finally, it shows identifiability under soft
interventions with independent support assumptions on latent variables. The study in (Squires
et al., 2023) considers a linear latent model with a linear mapping to observations and proves
identifiability under hard interventions. It also shows the impossibility of perfect
identifiability under soft interventions and proves identifiability up to ancestors. The study in (
Buchholz et al., 2023) focuses on linear Gaussian latent models and extends the results in (Squires
et al., 2023) to prove identifiability for general transformations. Zhang et al. (2023) consider
polynomial transformations under nonlinearity assumptions on latent models and prove
identifiability up to ancestors under soft interventions. If the latent graph is restricted to
polytrees, they further prove perfect identifiability. Identifying the non-intervened variables
from the intervened variables by using single-node and multi-node soft interventions is studied in
(Ahuja et al., 2024). It also considers a new setting in which the latent DAG can change across
data points and relies on the support invariance of non-intervened variables to identify them from
the rest. The study in (Bing et al., 2024) considers a nonlinear latent model under linear
transformation and uses multi-target do interventions to prove identifiability under certain
sparsity assumptions. Linear transformation and linear non-Gaussian latent models are studied in (
Jin and Syrgkanis, 2024), establishing identifiability up to surrounding parents using soft
interventions. It also establishes sufficient conditions for multi-target soft interventions to
ensure identifiability. The study in (Saengkyongam et al., 2024) takes a different approach and
considers the task of intervention extrapolation. In this formulation, interventions are applied to
exogenous action variables (e.g., instrumental variables) that linearly affect the latent
variables.


Table 1: Parametric Settings. Comparison of the results to prior studies in parametric settings.
Only the main results of the most closely related studies are listed, and standard shared
assumptions are omitted. Formal definitions of identifiability measures are provided in Section 3.

         Work          Transform         Latent             Int. Data         Identifiability
                                         Model         (one env. per node)        result
Squires et al. (2023)  Linear     Linear               Hard                perfect
                       Linear     Linear               Soft                up to ancestors
Ahuja et al. (2023)    Polynomial General              do                  perfect
                       Polynomial Bounded RV           Soft                perfect
Buchholz et al. (2023) General    Lin. Gaussian        Hard                perfect
                       General    Lin. Gaussian        Soft                up to ancestors
Zhang et al. (2023)    Polynomial nonlinear            Soft                up to ancestors
                       Polynomial nonlinear (polytree) Soft                perfect
Theorem 16             Linear     General              Hard                perfect
Theorem 14             Linear     General              Soft                up to ancestors
Theorem 18             Linear     nonlinear            Soft                perfect DAG and
                                                                           mixing w. surrounding

On the fully nonparametric setting, von Kügelgen et al. (2023) provide the most closely related
identifiability results to ours. Specifically, they show that two coupled hard interventions per
node suffice for identifiability under the faithfulness assumption on latent causal models. Our
results have two significant differences: 1) We address achievability via a provably correct
algorithm whereas von Kügelgen et al. (2023) focus mainly on identifiability (e.g., no algorithm
for recovery of the latent variables), 2) we dispense with the restrictive assumptions on
identifiability results, namely, we do not require to know which two environments share the same
intervention target (hence, uncoupled interventions), and do not require faithfulness on the latent
models. While the importance of this relaxation is not fully apparent for single-node
interventions, being able to work without knowing intervention targets is critical for
investigating CRL under more realistic cases of multi-node interventions. Among the other studies
on the nonparametric setting, Jin and Syrgkanis (2024) provide analogous results to (von Kügelgen
et al., 2023) by considering two coupled soft interventions and identifying latent variables up to
mixing with surrounding variables. Jiang and Aragam (2023) consider identifying the latent DAG
without recovering latent variables, where it is shown that a restricted class of DAGs can be
recovered. Finally, some studies employ stronger supervision signals, such as annotations of the
ground-truth causal variables (Shen et al., 2022), or knowledge of the causal graph to recover the
latent variables under hard interventions (Liang et al., 2023).

Multi-view causal representation learning.

A commonly used form of weak supervision in CRL involves multi-view data, non-i.i.d. samples (or
views) are observed, typically generated by the same or closely related realizations of underlying
latent variables. Multi-view scenarios can involve various data settings. Earlier studies have
mostly considered paired data, in which pre- and post-intervention observations are generated from
the same set of latent variables (Locatello et al., 2020; von Kügelgen et al., 2021; Brehmer
et al., 2022). The more generalized and relaxed formulation typically involves partial
observability, where multiple views are concurrently generated by an overlapping subset of latent
variables, such as observing different camera angles of the same scene (Sturma et al., 2023). Yao
et al. (2024) generalizes the multi-view approach via a unified framework, which also allows
partial observability with nonlinear transforms. We also note that Yao et al. (2025) presents an
even more general unifying view to interpret different CRL approaches, including interventional and
multi-view, as special ways of aligning the representations to known data symmetries via leveraging
the invariance principle.

Temporal causal representation learning.

Temporal CRL is particularly motivated by applications in domains where time-series data is readily
available, such as robotics and control systems, which has led to a growing interest in this area.
Some studies in this category assume that the latent causal variables are mutually independent,
meaning there are no instantaneous causal effects between variables at the same time step (Lippe
et al., 2022; Yao et al., 2022; Lachapelle et al., 2024). However, more recent studies have relaxed
this assumption, allowing instantaneous causal effects when sufficient diversity is present in the
observed or interventional data (Lippe et al., 2023a; Lippe et al., 2023b; Li et al., 2024b).

Other approaches.

In some other approaches to CRL, Zhang et al. (2024) consider general mixing functions and general
SCMs under sparsity constraints; Liu et al. (2024) leverage nonlinear ICA principles to work with
nonlinear mixing functions and polynomial latent SCMs; Morioka and Hyvärinen (2024) consider
disjoint groups of observational variables, with general mixing functions and pairwise latent
causal relationships; Welch et al. (2024) use score functions for linear mixing functions and
nonlinear Gaussian SCMs to derive coarser identifiability results; and Li et al. (2024a) study
“domains” and interventions in a combined way for non-Markovian causal systems when the graph is
known.

Identifiable representation learning.

As a special case of CRL, where the latent variables are independent, there is extensive literature
on identifying latent representations. Some representative approaches include leveraging knowledge
of the mechanisms that govern the system’s evolution (Ahuja et al., 2022) and using weak
supervision with auxiliary information (Shu et al., 2020). Nonlinear independent component analysis
(ICA) also uses side information, in the form of structured time series, to exploit temporal
information (Hyvärinen and Morioka, 2017; Hälvä and Hyvärinen, 2020) or knowledge of auxiliary
variables that make latent variables conditionally independent (Khemakhem et al., 2020a; Khemakhem
et al., 2020b; Hyvärinen et al., 2019). Morioka and Hyvärinen (2024) imposes additional constraints
on observational mixing and the causal model to prove identifiability. Kivva et al. (2022) studies
the identifiability of deep generative models without auxiliary information.

Score functions for causal discovery within observed variables.

Score matching has recently gained attention in the causal discovery of observed variables. Rolland
et al. (2022) use score matching to recover nonlinear additive Gaussian noise models. Montagna
et al. (2023b) focus on the same setting, recover the full graph from Jacobian scores, and dispense
with the computationally expensive pruning stage of the algorithm in (Rolland et al., 2022).
Montagna et al. (2023a) empirically demonstrate the robustness of score matching approaches against
the assumption violations in causal discovery. Zhu et al. (2023) establish bounds on the error rate
of score matching-based causal discovery methods. All of these studies are limited to observed
causal variables, whereas in our case, we have a causal model in the latent space.

3 Preliminaries and Definitions

Notations.

For a vector 𝐚∈ℝm\mathbf{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}, the ii-the entry is denoted by aia_{i}. Random
vectors are denoted by bold upper-case letters and their realizations are denoted by bold
lower-case letters, e.g., 𝐗\mathbf{X} and 𝐱\mathbf{x}. Matrices are denoted by bold upper-case
letters, e.g., 𝐀\mathbf{A}, where 𝐀i\mathbf{A}_{i} denotes the ii-th row of 𝐀\mathbf{A} and 𝐀i,j\
mathbf{A}_{i,j} denotes the entry at row ii and column jj. For matrices 𝐀\mathbf{A} and 𝐁\mathbf{B}
with the same shapes, 𝐀≼𝐁\mathbf{A}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{B} denotes component-wise inequality. We
denote the indicator function by 𝟙\mathds{1}, and for a matrix 𝐀∈ℝm×n\mathbf{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\
times n}, we use the convention that 𝟙​{𝐀}∈{0,1}m×n\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{A}\}\in\{0,1\}^{m\times n},
where the entries are specified by [𝟙{𝐀}]i,j=𝟙{𝐀i,j≠0}[\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{A}\}]_{i,j}=\mathds{1}\
{\mathbf{A}_{i,j}\neq 0\}. For a positive integer n∈ℕn\in{\mathbb{N}}, we define [n]≜{1,…,n}[n]\
triangleq\{1,\dots,n\}. The permutation matrix associated with any permutation π\pi of [n][n] is
denoted by 𝐏π\mathbf{P}_{\pi}, i.e., [π1​π2​…​πn]⊤=𝐏π⋅[1  2​…​n]⊤[\pi_{1}\;\;\pi_{2}\;\dots\;\pi_
{n}]^{\top}=\mathbf{P}_{\pi}\cdot[1\;\;2\;\dots\;n]^{\top}. The nn-dimensional identity matrix is
denoted by 𝐈n×n\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}, and the Hadamard product is denoted by ⊙\odot. We use im⁡(f)
{\rm im}(f) to denote the image of the function ff. We let 𝐞i\mathbf{e}_{i} denote the ii-th
standard basis vector of ℝn{\mathbb{R}}^{n}. Given a function f:ℝs→ℝrf:{\mathbb{R}}^{s}\to{\mathbb
{R}}^{r} that has first-order partial derivatives on ℝs{\mathbb{R}}^{s}, we denote the Jacobian of
ff at 𝐳∈ℝs\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s} by Jf​(𝐳)∈ℝr×sJ_{f}(\mathbf{z})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r\times s}
with entries [Jf​(𝐳)]i,j=∂f​(𝐳)i/∂𝐳j[J_{f}(\mathbf{z})]_{i,j}=\partial f(\mathbf{z})_{i}/\partial\
mathbf{z}_{j}.

3.1 Latent Causal Structure

Consider latent causal random variables 𝐙≜[Z1,…,Zn]⊤\mathbf{Z}\triangleq[Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}]^{\top}.
An unknown transformation g:ℝn→ℝdg:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d} generates the observable
random variables 𝐗≜[X1,…,Xd]⊤\mathbf{X}\triangleq[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]^{\top} from the latent
variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} according to:

 𝐗=g⁡(𝐙).\mathbf{X}=g(\mathbf{Z})\ .   (3)

We assume that d≥nd\geq n, and the transformation gg is continuously differentiable and a
diffeomorphism onto its image (otherwise, identifiability is ill-posed). We denote the image of gg
by 𝒳≜im⁡(g)⊆ℝd\mathcal{X}\triangleq{\rm im}(g)\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d}. The probability density
functions (pdfs) of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} and 𝐗\mathbf{X} are denoted by pp and p𝐗p_{\mathbf{X}},
respectively. We assume that pp is absolutely continuous with respect to the nn-dimensional
Lebesgue measure. Subsequently, p𝐗p_{\mathbf{X}}, which is defined on the image manifold im⁡(g){\rm
im}(g), is absolutely continuous with respect to the nn-dimensional Hausdorff measure rather than
dd-dimensional Lebesgue measure^1^1 1 For details of where this has been used, see Section A.2..
The distribution of latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} factorizes with respect to a DAG that consists of
nn nodes and is denoted by 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Node i∈[n]i\in[n] of 𝒢\mathcal{G} represents ZiZ_{i} and
pp factorizes according to:

 p⁡(𝐳)=∏i=1npi​(zi|𝐳pa⁡(i)),p(\mathbf{z})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}  (4)
 (i)})\ ,

where pa⁡(i){\rm{pa}}(i) denotes the set of parents of node ii and pi​(zi|𝐳pa⁡(i))p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\
mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}) is the conditional pdf of ziz_{i} given the variables of its parents. We
use ch⁡(i){\rm{ch}}(i), an⁡(i){\rm{an}}(i), and de⁡(i){\rm{de}}(i) to denote the children,
ancestors, and descendants of node ii, respectively. Accordingly, for each node i∈[n]i\in[n] we
also define

 pa¯​(i)≜pa⁡(i)∪{i},ch¯​(i)≜ch⁡(i)∪{i},an¯​(i)≜an⁡(i)∪{i},and​de¯​(i)≜de⁡(i)∪{i}.\displaystyle
 \overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\triangleq{\rm{pa}}(i)\cup\{i\},\;\;\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(i)\triangleq   (5)
 {\rm{ch}}(i)\cup\{i\},\;\;\overline{{\rm{an}}}(i)\triangleq{\rm{an}}(i)\cup\{i\},\;\mbox{and}
 \;\overline{{\rm{de}}}(i)\triangleq{\rm{de}}(i)\cup\{i\}\ .

We denote the transitive closure and transitive reduction of 𝒢\mathcal{G} by 𝒢tc\mathcal{G}_{\rm
tc} and 𝒢tr\mathcal{G}_{\rm tr}, respectively^2^2 2 Transitive closure of a DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G},
denoted by 𝒢tr\mathcal{G}_{\rm tr}, is a DAG with parents denoted by patr​(i)=an​(i){\rm{pa}}_{\rm
tr}(i)={\rm{an}}(i) for each node ii. The transitive reduction of 𝒢\mathcal{G} is the DAG with the
fewest edges that preserves the same reachability relation as 𝒢\mathcal{G}.. The parental
relationships in these graphs are denoted by patc{\rm{pa}}_{\rm tc} and patr{\rm{pa}}_{\rm tr}, and
other graphical relationships are denoted similarly. Based on the modularity property, a change in
the causal mechanism of node ii does not affect those of the other nodes. We also assume that all
conditional pdfs {pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i)):i∈[n]}\{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}):i\in[n]\} are
continuously differentiable with respect to all variables and p⁡(𝐳)≠0p(\mathbf{z})\neq 0 for all
𝐳∈ℝn\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}. We consider the general structural causal models (SCMs) based on
which for each i∈[n]i\in[n],

 Zi=fi​(𝐙pa⁡(i),Ni),\displaystyle Z_{i}=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)},N_{i})\ ,  (6)

where {fi:i∈[n]}\{f_{i}:i\in[n]\} are general functions that capture the dependence of node ii on
its parents and {Ni:i∈[n]}\{N_{i}:i\in[n]\} account for the exogenous noise terms that we assume to
have pdfs with full support. We specialize some of the results to additive noise SCMs, in which (6)
becomes

 Zi=fi​(𝐙pa⁡(i))+Ni.\displaystyle Z_{i}=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+N_{i}\ .  (7)

Next, we provide several definitions that we will use frequently throughout the paper to formalize
the framework and analyze it.

Definition 0 (Valid Causal Order).

We refer to a permutation (π1,…,πn)(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n}) of [n][n] as a valid causal order^3^3 3 
It is also called topological ordering or topological sort in the literature. if πi∈pa⁡(πj)\pi_{i}\
in{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{j}) indicates that i<ji<j.

In this paper, without loss of generality, we assume that (1,…,n)(1,\dots,n) is a valid causal
order. We also define a graphical notion that will be useful for presenting our results and
analysis on CRL under a linear transformation.

Definition 0 (Surrounded Node).

Node i∈[n]i\in[n] in DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G} is said to be surrounded if there exists another node j∈[n]j\
in[n] such that ch¯​(i)⊆ch​(j)\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(i)\subseteq{\rm{ch}}(j). We denote the set of
nodes that surround i∈[n]i\in[n] by sur⁡(i){\rm{sur}}(i), and the set of all nodes that are
surrounded by 𝒮\mathcal{S}, i.e.,

 sur⁡(i)\       ≜{j∈[n]:j≠i,ch¯(i)⊆ch(j)},and𝒮≜{i∈[n]:sur(i)≠∅}.\displaystyle\triangleq\{j\in
 displaystyle{\ [n]\,:\,j\neq i\;\;,\;\;\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(i)\subseteq{\rm{ch}}(j)\}\ ,\quad  (8)
 rm{sur}}(i)    \mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{S}\triangleq\{i\in[n]\,:\;{\rm{sur}}(i)\neq\emptyset\}
                \ .

The intuition behind surrounded nodes is that, the effect of ii on its children ch⁡(i){\rm{ch}}(i)
can be dominated by the effect of its surrounding node jj.^4^4 4 Surrounded node concept is first
defined by (Varıcı et al., 2023), and later adopted by Jin and Syrgkanis (2024) when considering
soft interventions. Specifically, any effect of ii on a node k∈ch⁡(i)k\in{\rm{ch}}(i) can also be
interpreted as the effect of node jj since k∈ch⁡(j)k\in{\rm{ch}}(j) as well. This effect causes
ambiguities in the recovery of ZiZ_{i} in the case of soft interventions.

and (1,3,2,4)(1,3,2,4), and only surrounded node is 44 with sur⁡(4)={2,3}{\rm{sur}}(4)=\{2,3\}.

{S}=\{2,3\} with sur⁡(2)={1},sur⁡(3)={1,2}{\rm{sur}}(2)=\{1\},{\rm{sur}}(3)=\{1,2\}.

3.2 Score Functions

The score function associated with a pdf is defined as the gradient of its logarithm. The score
function associated with pp is denoted by

 𝒔(𝐳)≜∇𝐳logp(𝐳).{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})\triangleq\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p(\mathbf{z})\ .  (9)

Noting the connection 𝐗=g⁡(𝐙)\mathbf{X}=g(\mathbf{Z}), the density of 𝐗\mathbf{X} under ℰ0\mathcal
{E}^{0}, denoted by p𝐗p_{\mathbf{X}}, is supported on an nn-dimensional manifold 𝒳\mathcal{X}
embedded in ℝd{\mathbb{R}}^{d}. Hence, specifying the score function of 𝐗\mathbf{X} requires
notions from differential geometry. For this purpose, we denote the tangent space of the manifold 𝒳

γ:(−1,1)→𝒳⊆ℝd\gamma\colon(-1,1)\to\mathcal{X}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{d} with γ⁡(0)=𝐱\gamma(0)=\
mathbf{x} and γ′​(0)=𝐯\gamma^{\prime}(0)=\mathbf{v}. Furthermore, given a function f:𝒳→ℝf\colon\

 differential t}(f\circ\gamma)(t)\right|_{t=0}\ ,

for any curve γ\gamma in equivalence class vv. The differential of ff at point 𝐱∈𝒳\mathbf{x}\in\

Note that the differential operator df\differential f is a generalization of the gradient. Hence,

3.3 Intervention Mechanisms

We consider two types of interventions. A soft intervention on node ii (also referred to as
imperfect intervention in literature), changes the conditional distribution pi​(zi|𝐳pa⁡(i))p_{i}(z_
{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}) to a distinct conditional distribution, which we denote by qi​
(zi|𝐳pa⁡(i))q_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}). A soft intervention does not necessarily
remove the functional dependence of an intervened node on its parents but rather alters it to a
different mechanism. A stochastic hard intervention on node ii (also referred to as perfect
intervention) is stricter than a soft intervention and removes the edges incident on ii. A hard
intervention on node changes pi​(zi|𝐳pa⁡(i))p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}) to qi​(zi)q_
{i}(z_{i}) that emphasizes the lack of dependence of ziz_{i} on 𝐳pa⁡(i)\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}.
Finally, we note that in some settings, we assume two hard interventions per node, in which case
the two hard interventional mechanisms for node ii are denoted by two distinct pdfs qi​(zi)q_{i}(z_
{i}) and q~i​(zi)\tilde{q}_{i}(z_{i}).

Interventional environments.

We consider atomic interventional environments in which each environment one node is intervened in,
as is customary in the closely related CRL literature (Squires et al., 2023; Ahuja et al., 2023;
Buchholz et al., 2023). In some settings (linear transformation), we will have one interventional
environment per node and denote the interventional environments by ℰ≜{ℰ1,…,ℰn}\mathcal{E}\triangleq
\{\mathcal{E}^{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}^{n}\}, where we call ℰ\mathcal{E} the atomic environment set.
We denote the node intervened in environment ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} by Im∈[n]I^{m}\in[n]. For other
settings (general transformation), we will have two interventional environments per node and denote
the second atomic environment set by ℰ~={ℰ~1,…,ℰ~n}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}=\{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{1},\
dots,\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{n}\}. Similarly, we denote the intervened node in ℰ~m\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^
{m} by I~m\tilde{I}^{m} for each m∈[n]m\in[n]. We assume that node-environment pairs are
unspecified, i.e., the ordered intervention sets ℐ≜(I1,…,In)\mathcal{I}\triangleq(I^{1},\dots,I^
{n}) and ℐ~≜(I~1,…,I~n)\tilde{\mathcal{I}}\triangleq(\tilde{I}^{1},\dots,\tilde{I}^{n}) are two
unknown permutations of [n][n]. We also adopt the convention that ℰ0\mathcal{E}^{0} is the
observational environment and I0≜∅I^{0}\triangleq\emptyset. Next, we define the notion of coupling
between the environment sets ℰ\mathcal{E} and ℰ~\tilde{\mathcal{E}}.

Definition 0 (Coupled/Uncoupled Environments).

The two environment sets ℰ\mathcal{E} and ℰ~\tilde{\mathcal{E}} are said to be coupled if for the
unknown permutations ℐ\mathcal{I} and ℐ~\tilde{\mathcal{I}} we know that ℐ=ℐ~\mathcal{I}=\tilde{\
mathcal{I}}, i.e., the same node is intervened in environments ℰi\mathcal{E}^{i} and ℰ~i\tilde{\
mathcal{E}}^{i}. The two environment sets are said to be uncoupled if ℐ~\tilde{\mathcal{I}} is an
unknown permutation of ℐ\mathcal{I}.

Next, we define pmp^{m} as the pdf of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} in environment ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m}. Hence, under
soft and hard intervention for each m∈[n]m\in[n], pmp^{m} can be factorized as follows.

Similarly, we define p~m\tilde{p}^{m} as the pdf of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} in ℰ~m\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{m},
which can be factorized similarly to (16) with q~ℓ\tilde{q}_{\ell} replaced with qℓq_{\ell}. Hence,

 𝒔m(𝐳)≜∇𝐳logpm(𝐳),and𝒔~m(𝐳)≜∇𝐳logp~m(𝐳).{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\triangleq\nabla_{\
 mathbf{z}}\log p^{m}(\mathbf{z})\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf     (17)
 {z})\triangleq\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log\tilde{p}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\ .

We denote the score functions of the observed variables 𝐗\mathbf{X} under ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} and ℰ~m
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{m} by 𝒔𝐗m{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}_{\mathbf{X}} and 𝒔~𝐗m\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}_
{\mathbf{X}}, respectively. Note that the score functions change across different environments,
which is induced by the changes in the distribution of 𝐙\mathbf{Z}. Specifically, following (4) and
(15), the latent scores 𝒔⁡(𝐳){\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z}) and 𝒔m​(𝐳){\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf
{z}) are decomposed as

 𝒔⁡(𝐳)\displaystyle{\      =∇𝐳logpℓ(zℓ|𝐳pa⁡(ℓ))+∑i≠ℓ∇𝐳logpi(zi|𝐳pa⁡(i)),\displaystyle=\nabla_
 boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf    {\mathbf{z}}\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\      (18)
 {z})                      ell)})+\sum_{i\neq\ell}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\
                           mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ ,

 mbox{and}\quad{\          {\mathbf{z}}\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\      (19)
 boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\      ell)})+\sum_{i\neq\ell}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\

where ℓ=Im\ell=I^{m}. Hence, 𝒔⁡(𝐳){\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z}) and 𝒔m​(𝐳){\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\
mathbf{z}) differ in only the causal mechanism of the intervened node in environment ℰm\mathcal{E}^
{m}. In Section 4, we investigate these discrepancies between 𝒔{\boldsymbol{s}} and 𝒔m{\boldsymbol
{s}}^{m} (or 𝒔~m\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}) and characterize the relationship between the scores in
the observational and interventional environments.

3.4 Identifiability and Achievability Objectives

The objective of CRL is to use observations 𝐗\mathbf{X} generated by the observational and
interventional environments and estimate the true latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} and causal relations
among them captured by 𝒢\mathcal{G}. The first objective is identifiability, which pertains to
determining algorithm-agnostic sufficient conditions under which 𝐙\mathbf{Z} and 𝒢\mathcal{G} can
be recovered uniquely up to a permutation and element-wise transform, which is the strongest form
of recovery in CRL from interventions as shown in (von Kügelgen et al., 2023). The second objective
is achievability, which refers to designing algorithms that are amenable to practical
implementation and generate provably correct estimates for 𝐙\mathbf{Z} and 𝒢\mathcal{G}, foreseen
by the identifiability guarantees. In this subsection, we provide the definitions needed for
formalizing these objectives.

We denote a generic estimator of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} given 𝐗\mathbf{X} by 𝐙^​(𝐗):ℝd→ℝn\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\
mathbf{X}):{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}. We also consider a generic estimate of 𝒢\mathcal{G}
denoted by 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}}. To assess the fidelity of the estimates 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\
mathbf{X}) and 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} with respect to the ground truth 𝐙\mathbf{Z} and 𝒢\mathcal{G},
we provide the following identifiability measures, which will be achieved when we have a complete
set of atomic interventions.

Definition 0 (Latent Graph Identifiability).

For identifiability of the latent graph, we define:

 1. 1.
   
    Perfect DAG recovery: DAG recovery is said to be perfect if 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} is isomorphic
    to 𝒢\mathcal{G}.

 2. 2.
   
    Transitive closure recovery: DAG recovery is said to maintain transitive closure if 𝒢^\hat{\
    mathcal{G}} and 𝒢\mathcal{G} have the same ancestral relationships, i.e., 𝒢^tr{\hat{\mathcal
    {G}}_{\rm tr}} is isomorphic to 𝒢tr\mathcal{G}_{\rm tr}.

Definition 0 (Latent Variable Identifiability).

For identifiability of all latent variables, we define:

 1. 1.
   
    Componentwise latent recovery: The estimator 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X}) satisfies
    componentwise latent recovery if 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X}) is a componentwise
    diffeomorphism of a permutation of 𝐙\mathbf{Z}, i.e., there exists a permutation π\pi of [n][n]
    and a set diffeomorphisms {ϕi:ℝ→ℝ:i∈[n]}\{\phi_{i}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}:i\in[n]\} such
    that we have

     𝐙^​(𝐗)=(π∘ϕ)​(𝐙),∀𝐳∈ℝn,\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X})=(\pi\circ\phi)(\mathbf   (20)
     {Z})\ ,\qquad\forall\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ ,

    where ϕ⁡(𝐙)≜(ϕ1​(Z1),…,ϕn​(Zn))\phi(\mathbf{Z})\triangleq(\phi_{1}(Z_{1}),\dots,\phi_{n}(Z_
    {n})).

 2. 2.
   
    Scaling consistency: The estimator 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X}) satisfies scaling
    consistency if there exists a permutation π\pi of [n][n] and a constant diagonal matrix 
    𝐂scale∈ℝn×n\mathbf{C}_{\rm scale}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n} such that

     𝐙^​(𝐗)=𝐏π⋅𝐂scale⋅𝐙,∀𝐳∈ℝn.\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{P}_{\pi}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\  (21)
     rm scale}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,\qquad\forall\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ .

 3. 3.
   
    Consistency up to mixing with parents: The estimator 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X})
    satisfies consistency up to mixing with parents if there exists a permutation π\pi of [n][n]
    and a constant matrix 𝐂pa∈ℝn×n\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n} such that

     𝐙^​(𝐗)=𝐏π⋅𝐂pa⋅𝐙,∀𝐳∈ℝn,\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{P}_{\pi}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm   (22)
     pa}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,\qquad\forall\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ ,

    where 𝐂pa\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa} has nonzero diagonal entries and for all j∉pa¯​(i)j\notin\overline
    {{\rm{pa}}}(i), [𝐂pa]i,j=0[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}]_{i,j}=0. Equivalently, Z^πi\hat{Z}_{\pi_{i}} is
    a linear function of {Zj:j∈pa¯​(i)}\{Z_{j}:j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\} for all i∈[n]i\in[n].

 4. 4.
   
    Consistency up to mixing with surrounding parents: The estimator 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf
    {X}) satisfies consistency up to mixing with surrounding parents if there exists a permutation 
    π\pi of [n][n] and a constant matrix 𝐂sur∈ℝn×n\mathbf{C}_{\rm sur}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
    such that

     𝐙^​(𝐗)=𝐏π⋅𝐂sur⋅𝐙,∀𝐳∈ℝn,\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{P}_{\pi}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm  (23)
     sur}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,\qquad\forall\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ ,

    where 𝐂sur\mathbf{C}_{\rm sur} has nonzero diagonal entries and for all j∉sur¯​(i)j\notin\
    overline{{\rm{sur}}}(i), [𝐂sur]i,j=0[\mathbf{C}_{\rm sur}]_{i,j}=0. Equivalently, Z^πi\hat{Z}_
    {\pi_{i}} is a linear function of {Zj:j∈sur¯​(i)}\{Z_{j}:j\in\overline{{\rm{sur}}}(i)\} for all
    i∈[n]i\in[n].

We note that scaling consistency is a special case of component-wise latent recovery in which the
diffeomorphism is restricted to scaling. Next, we provide node-level partial identifiability
definitions which measure the recovery level of a single latent variable. These will be useful to
assess the identifiability guarantees of the algorithms under an incomplete set of interventions.

Definition 0 (Node-level Partial Identifiability).

For identifiability of a single latent variable, we define:

 1. 1.
   
    Partial latent recovery: The estimator 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X}) satisfies partial
    latent recovery for node ii if

     [𝐙^​(𝐗)]i=ϕk​(Zk),[\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X})]_{i}=\phi_{k}(Z_{k})\ ,  (24)

    for some k∈[n]k\in[n] where ϕk:ℝ→ℝ\phi_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}} is a diffeomorphism.

 2. 2.
   
    Partial consistency up to mixing with parents: The estimator 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X})
    satisfies partial consistency up to mixing with parents for node ii if

     [𝐙^​(𝐗)]i=∑j∈pa¯​(k)ck⋅Zk,[\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X})]_{i}=\sum_{j\in\overline{{\rm     (25)
     {pa}}}(k)}c_{k}\cdot Z_{k}\ ,

    for some k∈[n]k\in[n] and constants {cj:j∈pa¯​(k)}\{c_{j}:j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(k)\}.

Tightness of the identifiability definitions.

For general transformations, von Kügelgen et al. (2023, Proposition 3.1) show that identifiability
up to component-wise diffeomorphisms is the best possible result under interventions, without
additional assumptions. For linear transformations, Squires et al. (2023, Proposition 2) and
Buchholz et al. (2023, Remark 2) show that under hard interventions, identifiability up to scaling
consistency and permutation ambiguity is the best achievable outcome without further information on
𝐙\mathbf{Z}. Furthermore, for soft interventions, transitive closure recovery is the best latent
graph identifiability result without additional assumptions on the causal model  (Squires et al.,
2023, Appendix J). Finally, Jin and Syrgkanis (2024, Theorem 3) show that, under some
non-degeneracy assumptions, consistency up to mixing with surrounding parents is the optimal result
for soft interventions. Therefore, given a complete set of atomic interventions, the
identifiability definitions presented in this section represent the ultimate objectives when using
interventions.

3.5 Algorithm-related Definitions

For formalizing the achievability results and designing the associated algorithms, generating the
estimates 𝐙^​(𝐗)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X}) and 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} is facilitated by estimating
the inverse of gg based on the observed data 𝐗\mathbf{X}. Specifically, an estimate of g−1g^{-1},
where g−1g^{-1} denotes the inverse of gg, facilitates recovering 𝐙\mathbf{Z} via 𝐙=g−1​(𝐗)\mathbf
{Z}=g^{-1}(\mathbf{X}) . Throughout the rest of this paper, we refer to g−1g^{-1} as the true
encoder. To formalize the procedures of estimating g−1g^{-1}, we define ℋ\mathcal{H} as the set of
possible valid encoders, i.e., candidates for g−1g^{-1}. A function h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H} can be such
a candidate if it is invertible, that is, there exists an associated decoder h−1h^{-1} such that
(h−1∘h)​(𝐗)=𝐗(h^{-1}\circ h)(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{X}. Hence, the set of valid encoders is specified
by

 ℋ≜{h:𝒳→ℝn:∃h−1:ℝn→ℝdsuch that (h−1∘h)(𝐱)=𝐱,∀𝐱∈𝒳}.\mathcal{H}\triangleq\{h:\mathcal{X}\to{\
 mathbb{R}}^{n}\;\colon\;\exists h^{-1}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\;\;\mbox{such      (26)
 that }\;\;(h^{-1}\circ h)(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{x}\ ,\;\;\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}\}\ .

Next, corresponding to any pair of observation 𝐗\mathbf{X} and valid encoder h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H}, we
define 𝐙^​(𝐗,h)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h) as an auxiliary estimate of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} generated by
applying the valid encoder hh on 𝐗\mathbf{X}, i.e.,

 𝐙^​(𝐗,h)≜h⁡(𝐗)=(h∘g)​(𝐙),∀h∈ℋ.\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h)\triangleq h(\mathbf{X})=(h\circ  (27)
 g)(\mathbf{Z})\ ,\qquad\forall\;h\in\mathcal{H}\ .

The estimate 𝐙^​(𝐗,h)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h) inherits its randomness from 𝐗\mathbf{X}, and
its statistical model is governed by that of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} under the chosen hh. To emphasize the
dependence on hh, we denote the score functions associated with the pdfs of 𝐙^​(𝐗,h)\hat{\mathbf
{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h) under environments ℰ0\mathcal{E}^{0}, ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m}, and ℰ~m\tilde{\mathcal
{E}}^{m}, respectively, by

 𝒔𝐙^​(⋅,h),𝒔𝐙^m​(⋅,h),and𝒔~𝐙^m​(⋅,h).{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\cdot;h)\ ,\quad{\
 boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\cdot;h),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\  (28)
 hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\cdot;h)\ .

We will be addressing both general and linear transformations gg. In the linear transformation
setting, the true linear transformation gg is denoted by the matrix 𝐆∈ℝd×n\mathbf{G}\in{\mathbb{R}}
^{d\times n}. Accordingly, we denote a valid linear encoder by 𝐇∈ℝn×d\mathbf{H}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\
times d}. For a given valid encoder 𝐇\mathbf{H}, the associated valid decoder is given by its
Moore-Penrose inverse, i.e., 𝐇†≜𝐇⊤⋅(𝐇⋅𝐇⊤)−1\mathbf{H}^{{\dagger}}\triangleq\mathbf{H}^{\top}\cdot(\
mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{H}^{\top})^{-1}.

4 Properties of Score Functions under Interventions

Score functions and their variations across different interventional environments play pivotal
roles in our approach to identifying latent representations. In this section, we present the key
properties of the score functions that will be leveraged in Section 5 and Section 6 to construct
identifiability results, along with the corresponding algorithms.

We first investigate score variations across pairs of environments, such as the observational
environment and an interventional one (under both soft and hard atomic interventions) or two
interventional environments, either coupled or uncoupled. The key insight is that an intervention
causes changes in only certain coordinates of the score function, as indicated by the sparse
changes in the decompositions of the score functions in (18) and (19). These sparse changes further
reflect the graph structure in the score differences. For instance, for a single-node intervention
Im={i}I^{m}=\{i\} in mm-th environment, jj-th coordinate of the score difference 𝒔−𝒔m{\boldsymbol
{s}}-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m} becomes

 [𝒔⁡(𝐳)−𝒔m​(𝐳)]j=∂∂zj​log⁡pi​(zi∣𝐳OPENpa⁡(i)))−∂∂zj​log⁡qi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i)),\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}
 (\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}\log    (29)
 p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i))})-\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{j}}\log q_{i}(z_{i}\
 mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ ,

which is zero if j∉pa¯​(i)j\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i). The following lemma formalizes this
property for all relevant cases.

Lemma 0 (Score Changes under Interventions).

Consider the observational environment ℰ0\mathcal{E}^{0} and an interventional environment ℰm\
mathcal{E}^{m} with unknown intervention target ImI^{m}. Then, for any causal model and
intervention type, 𝔼⁡[|𝐬⁡(𝐙)−𝐬m​(𝐙)|i]≠0{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\
boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0 implies that i∈pa¯​(Im)i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}
(I^{m}). For the reverse direction, we have the following results specified for each relevant case.

 1. (i)
   
    Hard interventions: If the intervention in ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} (or ℰ~m\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{m}) is
    hard, then score functions 𝒔{\boldsymbol{s}} and 𝒔m{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m} (or 𝒔~m\tilde{\
    boldsymbol{s}}^{m}) differ in their ii-th coordinate if and only if node ii or one of its
    children is intervened in ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} (or in ℰ~m\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{m}).

     𝔼⁡[|𝒔⁡(𝐙)−𝒔m​(𝐙)|i]≠0\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big| ⇔i∈pa¯(Im),\displaystyle\quad\

     mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0                           {pa}}}(I^{m})\ ,

     {\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0     {pa}}}(\tilde{I}^{m})\ .

 2. (ii)
   
    Soft interventions: If the intervention in ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} is soft and the latent causal
    model is an additive noise model, then score functions 𝒔{\boldsymbol{s}} and 𝒔m{\boldsymbol{s}}
    ^{m} differ in their ii-th coordinate if and only if node ii or one of its children is
    intervened in ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m}.

     𝔼[|𝒔(𝐙)−𝒔m(𝐙)|i]≠0⇔i∈pa¯(Im).{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\

 3. (iii)

     𝔼[|𝒔m(𝐙)−𝒔~m(𝐙)|i]≠0⇔i=Im.{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde   (33)

    {m}\neq\tilde{I}^{m}, and consider additive noise models specified in (7). Given that p⁡(𝐙)p(\

     overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m},\tilde{I}^{m})\ .

Proof: See Section A.1.

Lemma 7 provides the necessary and sufficient conditions for the invariance of the coordinates of
the score functions of latent variables. The core idea of the score-based framework is that tracing
these sparse changes in the score functions of the latent variables guides finding reliable
estimates for the inverse of the transformation gg, which in turn facilitates estimating 𝐙\mathbf
{Z}. Intuitively, we will look for the encoders h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H} such that the variations between
the score estimates 𝒔𝐙^​(𝐳^,h){\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};h), 𝒔𝐙^m​(𝐳^,h)
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};h), and 𝒔~𝐙^m​(𝐳^,h)\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}
_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};h) will be similar to the true score variations given by
Lemma 7. However, the scores of the latent variables are not directly accessible. To circumvent
this, we need to understand the connection between score functions of 𝐗\mathbf{X} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z}.
In the following lemma, we leverage the change of variables formula for injective mappings and
establish this relationship for any injective mapping ff from latent to observed space.

Lemma 0 (Score Difference Transformation).

Consider random vectors 𝐘1,𝐘2∈ℝr\mathbf{Y}_{1},\mathbf{Y}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r} and 𝐖1\mathbf{W}_
{1}, 𝐖2∈ℝs\mathbf{W}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s} that are related through

 𝐘1=f⁡(𝐖1),and𝐘2=f⁡(𝐖2),\mathbf{Y}_{1}=f(\mathbf{W}_{1})\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\qquad\mathbf{Y}_    (35)
 {2}=f(\mathbf{W}_{2})\ ,

such that r≥sr\geq s, probability measures of 𝐖1,𝐖2\mathbf{W}_{1},\mathbf{W}_{2} are absolutely
continuous with respect to the 𝐬{\boldsymbol{s}}-dimensional Lebesgue measure, and f:ℝs→ℝrf:{\
mathbb{R}}^{s}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{r} is an injective and continuously differentiable function. The
difference of the score functions of 𝐘1\mathbf{Y}_{1} and 𝐘2\mathbf{Y}_{2}, and that of 𝐖1\mathbf
{W}_{1} and 𝐖2\mathbf{W}_{2} are related as

 s𝐖1​(𝐰)−s𝐖2​(𝐰)=[Jf​(𝐰)]⊤⋅[s𝐘1​(𝐲)−s𝐘2​(𝐲)],where​𝐲=f⁡(𝐰),s_{\mathbf{W}_{1}}(\mathbf{w})-s_
 {\mathbf{W}_{2}}(\mathbf{w})=\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{\top}\cdot\big[s_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}   (36)
 (\mathbf{y})-s_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})\big]\ ,\quad\mbox{where}\;\;\mathbf{y}=f(\mathbf
 {w})\ ,

where Jf​(𝐰)J_{f}(\mathbf{w}) denotes the Jacobian of ff at point ww. Furthermore, for the reverse
direction, we have

 s𝐘1​(𝐲)−s𝐘2​(𝐲)=[[Jf​(𝐰)]†]⊤⋅[s𝐖1​(𝐰)−s𝐖2​(𝐰)],where​𝐲=f⁡(𝐰).s_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})
 -s_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})=\Big[\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{{\dagger}}\Big]^{\top}\     (37)
 cdot\big[s_{\mathbf{W}_{1}}(\mathbf{w})-s_{\mathbf{W}_{2}}(\mathbf{w})\big]\ ,\quad\mbox
 {where}\;\;\mathbf{y}=f(\mathbf{w})\ .

Proof: See Section A.2.
For CRL under linear transformations in Section 5, we use the following corollary of Lemma 8.

Corollary 0.

In Lemma 8, if ff is a linear transform, that is 𝐘=𝐅⋅𝐖\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{F}\cdot\mathbf{W} for a
full-rank matrix 𝐅∈ℝr×s\mathbf{F}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r\times s}, then the score functions of 𝐘\mathbf
{Y} and 𝐖\mathbf{W} are related through 𝐬𝐖​(𝐰)=𝐅⊤⋅𝐬𝐘​(𝐲){\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{w})=\
mathbf{F}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y}) and 𝐬𝐘​(𝐲)=[𝐅†]⊤⋅𝐬𝐖​(𝐰){\boldsymbol
{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})=\big[\mathbf{F}^{{\dagger}}\big]^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf
{W}}(\mathbf{w}), where 𝐲=𝐅⋅𝐰\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{F}\cdot\mathbf{w}.

For CRL under general transformations in Section 6, we customize Lemma 8 as follows. Consider a
candidate encoder h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H}. Recall that 𝐙^​(𝐗,h)=h⁡(𝐗)=(h∘g)​(𝐙)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf
{X};h)=h(\mathbf{X})=(h\circ g)(\mathbf{Z}). Then, by letting f=(h∘g)f=(h\circ g), Lemma 8 gives

5 CRL under Linear Transformations

In this section, we consider CRL under linear transformation, in which the general transformation
model in (3) becomes:

 𝐗=𝐆⋅𝐙,\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,  (41)

where 𝐆∈ℝd×n\mathbf{G}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times n} is an unknown full-rank matrix mapping the latent
variables to the observed ones. We will present steps for leveraging the properties of the score
functions presented in Section 4 to design an algorithm that identifies the true encoder and
recovers the true causal representations. The algorithm is referred to as Linear Score-based Causal
Latent Estimation via Interventions (LSCALE-I). The theoretical guarantees associated with the
algorithm steps will also be presented, serving as constructive proof of identifiability.

LSCALE-I consists of three stages outlined in Algorithm 1. In the first stage, we will learn an
encoder 𝐇^\hat{\mathbf{H}}, row by row, where each row is an estimate of a row of the true encoder
𝐆†\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}. This encoder estimate will recover each ZiZ_{i} up to mixing with 𝐙pa⁡(i)
\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}. In the second stage, we will use the estimated latent variables 𝐇^\hat{\
mathbf{H}} to obtain the transitive closure of the latent graph 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Finally, as an
optional third stage for hard interventions, we will leverage the independence statements implied
by the hard interventions to refine our encoder estimate. We will demonstrate that this refinement
yields scaling consistency and perfect DAG recovery. We will also discuss the relevance and
distinctions of our results vis-à-vis the results in the existing literature.

Statistical diversity.

Similarly to the existing identifiability results, it is necessary to have some regularity
conditions ^5^5 5 Some examples include generic interventions in (Squires et al., 2023), no pure
shift interventions condition in (Buchholz et al., 2023), and the genericity condition in (von
Kügelgen et al., 2023). on the probability distributions of observational and interventional
environments. We adopt the following assumption for the case of linear transformations.

Assumption 1.

For every possible pair (i,k)(i,k) where i∈[n],k∈pa⁡(i)i\in[n],k\in{\rm{pa}}(i), the following term
cannot be a constant function in 𝐳\mathbf{z},

 ∂∂zk​(log⁡pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))qi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i)))​[∂∂zi​log⁡pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))qi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))]−1.\frac
 {\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\left(\log\frac{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{q_{i}
 (z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\right)\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\log\frac    (42)
 {p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\
 right]^{-1}\ .

Essentially, ∂∂zk​(log⁡pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))qi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i)))\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\big(\log\frac
{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\big)
captures the effect of intervening on node ii on the score associated with node kk. Therefore,
Assumption 1 ensures that an intervention sufficiently differentiates the target variable and its
parents in the score function. We note that this is a very mild assumption and holds for a wide
range of commonly used models (including additive noise models under hard interventions) and is
discussed in more detail in Section 5.5.

Before we explain the details of LSCALE-I and establish identifiability results in the following
subsections, we first present the rationale and derive the partial identifiability result, which
will serve as the building block. In the rest of the paper, for brevity, we denote the score
difference functions for each m∈[n]m\in[n] by

 𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)≜𝒔m​(𝐳)−𝒔⁡(𝐳)and𝒅𝐗m​(𝐱)≜𝒔𝐗m​(𝐱)−𝒔𝐗​(𝐱).{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\
 triangleq{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\  (43)
 boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})\triangleq{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\
 mathbf{x})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})\ .

5.1 Rationale for LSCALE-I and Partial Identifiability

To construct an algorithm that achieves identifiability under linear transformations, we first
analyze the score differences for the linear transformation. Note that for linear transformation 𝐗=
𝐆⋅𝐙\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}, Jacobian J𝐆​(𝐳)J_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{z}) is independent
of 𝐳\mathbf{z} and equal to matrix 𝐆\mathbf{G}. Also, we have 𝐳=𝐆†⋅𝐱\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{G}^{{\

9 we have

 𝒅𝐗m​(𝐱)=[𝐆†]⊤⋅𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳).\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})=[\mathbf{G}  (44)
 ^{{\dagger}}]^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\ .

Lemma 10 crucially implies that we can achieve partial identifiability of a single latent variable
given that we have an environment in which the said variable is intervened.

5.2 Identifiability via Soft Interventions

After obtaining the partial identifiability result from a single interventional environment, our
goal is to construct an algorithm that uses the complete set of interventional environments ℰ=
{ℰ1,…,ℰn}\mathcal{E}=\{\mathcal{E}^{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}^{n}\} with targets ∪m∈[n]Im=[n]\cup_{m\in
[n]}I^{m}=[n] to form estimates of the true encoder 𝐆†\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}} and the latent graph 𝒢
\mathcal{G}.

Encoder estimation.

In Stage L1 of Algorithm 1, we form an encoder estimate 𝐇^\hat{\mathbf{H}} via using the column
spaces of {𝐑𝐗m:m∈[n]}\{\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}:m\in[n]\}. Specifically, for each m∈[n]m\in[n]
we select a vector randomly from col⁡(𝐑𝐗m){\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}) and assign its
transpose to mm-th row of 𝐇^\hat{\mathbf{H}}.

Lemma 0 (Linear – Encoder via Soft Interventions).

Under soft interventions, output 𝐇^\hat{\mathbf{H}} of Algorithm 1 achieves identifiability up to
mixing with parents. Specifically, 𝐙^​(𝐗,𝐇^)=𝐏ℐ⋅𝐂pa⋅𝐙\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf{H}})=
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}\cdot\mathbf{Z} such that diagonal entries of 𝐂pa\
mathbf{C}_{\rm pa} are nonzero and [𝐂pa]i,j=0\big[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}\big]_{i,j}=0 for all j∉pa¯​
(i)j\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i).

Proof: Following Theorem 11 for all m∈[n]m\in[n] immediately implies the desired result.
Specifically, according to (50), [𝐇^m]⊤∈col⁡(𝐑𝐗m)[\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}]^{\top}\in{\rm col}(\mathbf
{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}) satisfies

 [𝐙^​(𝐗,𝐇^)]m=𝐇^m⋅𝐗=cℓ⋅Zℓ+∑i∈pa⁡(ℓ)ci⋅Zi,where​ℓ=Im,[\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf
 {H}})]_{m}=\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}\cdot\mathbf{X}=c_{\ell}\cdot Z_{\ell}+\sum_{i\in{\rm{pa}}(\   (51)
 ell)}c_{i}\cdot Z_{i}\ ,\qquad\mbox{where}\;\ell=I^{m}\ ,

for some constants {ci:i∈pa¯​(ℓ)}\{c_{i}:i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\ell)\} where cℓ≠0c_{\ell}\neq 0
with probability 1. Then, combining all nn identities, we have

 𝐙^​(𝐗,𝐇^)=𝐏ℐ⋅𝐂pa⋅𝐙,\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf{H}})=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\    (52)
 cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,

where 𝐏ℐ\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}} is the permutation matrix of the intervention order (I1,…,In)(I^
{1},\dots,I^{n}), and 𝐂pa\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa} has nonzero diagonal entries and satisfies [𝐂pa]i,j=0\
big[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}\big]_{i,j}=0 for all j∉pa¯​(i)j\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i).  □\square

Latent graph estimation.

Next, we use the encoder estimate 𝐇^\hat{\mathbf{H}} to recover the transitive closure of the
latent graph 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Motivated by Lemma 7(ii), we form 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} with parent sets

 pa^​(m)≜{i≠m:𝔼⁡[|𝒔𝐙^​(𝐙^,𝐇^)−𝒔𝐙^m​(𝐙^,𝐇^)|i]≠0}.\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\triangleq\Big\{i\neq m:{\
 mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{\mathbf{H}})-   (53)
 {\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{\mathbf{H}})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq
 0\Big\}\ .

Using the result that 𝐙^​(𝐗,𝐇^)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf{H}}) recovers 𝐙\mathbf{Z}
up to mixing with parents, we show that the transitive closures of the estimate 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}}
and the true graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} are the same under a graph isomorphism.

Lemma 0 (Linear – Graph via Soft Interventions).

Under Assumption 1 and soft interventions, output 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} of Algorithm 1 recovers the
transitive closure of 𝒢\mathcal{G}.

Proof: Let 𝐙^\hat{\mathbf{Z}} denote 𝐙^​(𝐗,𝐇^)=𝐏ℐ⋅𝐂pa⋅𝐙\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf
{H}})=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}\cdot\mathbf{Z}. Recall that [𝐂pa]i,j=0\big[\
mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}\big]_{i,j}=0 for all j∉pa¯​(i)j\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i), and since nodes
(1,…,n)(1,\dots,n) are topologically ordered, 𝐂pa\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa} is a lower triangular matrix.
By using standard linear algebra for the expansion of an inverse of a lower triangular matrix, we
can show that [𝐂pa−1]i,j=0\big[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}^{-1}\big]_{i,j}=0 for all j∉an¯​(i)j\notin\
overline{{\rm{an}}}(i) (see Lemma 34 in Section B.1 for the detailed steps). Then, using Corollary 
9 for the scores of 𝐙\mathbf{Z}, we have

 𝒅𝐙^m​(𝐙^)\displaystyle{\          =𝐏ℐ⋅[𝐂pa−1]⊤⋅𝒅𝐙m​(𝐙).\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal
 boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}} {I}}\cdot[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}^{-1}]^{\top}\cdot{\           (54)
 ^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})            boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\ .

Next, taking the transpose of 𝐂pa−1\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}^{-1}, we have [𝐂pa−⁣⊤]i,j=0\big[\mathbf{C}_
{\rm pa}^{-\top}\big]_{i,j}=0 for all j∉de¯​(i)j\notin\overline{{\rm{de}}}(i). Combining this
observation with the fact that [𝒅𝐙m]j=0[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}]_{j}=0 for all j∉pa¯​(Im)
j\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m}), we obtain

 [𝒅𝐙^m​(𝐙^)]k=[𝐂pa−⁣⊤]Ik⋅𝒅𝐙m​(𝐙)=∑j∈de¯​(Ik)∩pa¯​(Im)[𝐂pa−⁣⊤]Ik,j⋅[𝒅𝐙m]j\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}
 _{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\big]_{k}=\big[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}^{-\top}\big]_{I^
 {k}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})=\sum_{j\;\in\;\overline{{\rm{de}}}(I^  (55)
 {k})\;\cap\;\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})}\big[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}^{-\top}\big]_{I^{k},j}\cdot
 [{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}]_{j}

Subsequently, 𝔼⁡[|𝒅𝐙^m​(𝐙^)|k]≠0{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\
mathbf{Z}})|_{k}\big]\neq 0 implies that de¯​(Ii)∩pa¯​(Im)≠∅\overline{{\rm{de}}}(I^{i})\cap\

Therefore, the estimated parent set in (53) satisfies pa^​(m)⊆an¯​(Im)\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\subseteq\
overline{{\rm{an}}}(I^{m}), and the transitive closure of 𝒢\mathcal{G} is a supergraph of 𝒢^\hat{\
mathcal{G}} under relabeling of the nodes with permutation ℐ\mathcal{I}. Next, we show that 𝒢^\hat
{\mathcal{G}} contains all the edges in the transitive reduction of 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Let Ik→ImI^{k}\
rightarrow I^{m} be an edge in the transitive reduction of 𝒢\mathcal{G}, i.e., there is no other
directed path between nodes IkI^{k} and ImI^{m} in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. This implies that de¯​(Ik)∩pa¯​
