becomes

 [𝒅𝐙^m​(𝐙^)]k=[𝐂pa−⁣⊤]Ik,Ik⋅[𝒅𝐙m]Ik+[𝐂pa−⁣⊤]Ik,Im⋅[𝒅𝐙m]Im.\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf
 {Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\big]_{k}=\big[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}^{-\top}\big]_{I^{k},I^{k}}\     (56)
 cdot[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}]_{I^{k}}+\big[\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}^{-\top}\big]_{I^
 {k},I^{m}}\cdot[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}]_{I^{m}}\ .

Assumption 1 ensures that [𝒅𝐙^m​(𝐙^)]k\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf
{Z}})\big]_{k} is not constantly zero, which means that 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} contains k→mk\
rightarrow m edge, preserving Ik→ImI^{k}\rightarrow I^{m} in 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Therefore, 𝒢^\hat{\
mathcal{G}} preserves all the edges in the transitive reduction of 𝒢\mathcal{G}, and taking its
transitive closure gives 𝒢tc\mathcal{G}_{\rm tc}, under relabeling of the nodes with permutation ℐ\
mathcal{I}.  □\square

Theorem 14 (Linear – Soft Interventions for General SCMs).

Under Assumption 1 for linear transformations, using observational data and interventional data
from one soft intervention per node suffice to identify (i) the transitive closure of the latent
DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G} and (ii) the latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} with consistency up to mixing with
parents. Specifically, Algorithm 1 achieves these identifiability guarantees.

Proof: Combining Lemma 12 and Lemma 13 gives the statements of the theorem.  □\square

We note that the existing literature on CRL with linear transformations and one soft intervention
requires the latent causal model to be either linear Gaussian (Squires et al., 2023; Buchholz
et al., 2023) or satisfy nonlinearity conditions (Zhang et al., 2023)^6^6 6 The “linear
interventional faithfulness” (Zhang et al., 2023, Assumption 2) implies nonlinearity, which we
elaborate in Section D.3.. In contrast, Theorem 14 achieves identifiability guarantees for soft
interventions without imposing any restrictions (distributional or structural) on the latent causal
model. Furthermore, recovering 𝐙\mathbf{Z} up to mixing with parents improves upon the existing
guarantees of recovering up to mixing with ancestors (Squires et al., 2023; Buchholz et al., 2023).
Finally, we note that 𝒢\mathcal{G} cannot be identified beyond its transitive closure when using
soft interventions without making additional assumptions (Squires et al., 2023, Appendix J).
Therefore, the graph identifiability result in Theorem 14 is tight.

5.3 Identifiability via Hard Interventions

Next, we investigate hard interventions (i.e., perfect interventions) for general latent causal
models. Hard interventions are special cases of soft interventions, in which the intervened node
becomes functionally independent of its parents. Consequently, the identifiability guarantees for
hard interventions are usually stronger, as we show in this section. Our analysis builds on
leveraging the following property, which is exclusive to hard interventions.

Proposition 0.

For the environment ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} in which node ℓ=Im\ell=I^{m} is hard intervened, we have

 Zℓm⟂⟂Zjm,∀j∈nd⁡(ℓ),\displaystyle Z_{\ell}^{m}\perp\!\!\!\perp Z_{j}^{m}\ ,\quad\forall j\in   (57)
 {\rm{nd}}(\ell)\ ,

where nd⁡(ℓ){\rm{nd}}(\ell) is the set of non-descendants of ℓ\ell in 𝒢\mathcal{G}.

The Markov property readily implies this property, that is, each variable in a DAG is independent
of its non-descendants, given its parents. When node ℓ\ell is hard-intervened, it has no parents,
and the statement follows directly. Motivated by this property, we want to ensure that the
estimated latent variables conform to Proposition 15. To this end, we aim to update rows of the
encoder estimate 𝐇^\hat{\mathbf{H}} to remove the effects of 𝐙pa⁡(i)\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)} from
the estimate of ZiZ_{i}. We achieve this objective as follows.

Note that Proposition 15 provides us with random variable pairs that are supposed to be
independent, and the covariance of two independent random variables is necessarily zero. In
Stage L3 of Algorithm 1, for each row 𝐇^m\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}, we consider 𝐙^​(𝐗,𝐇^)\hat{\mathbf
{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf{H}}) in the corresponding environment in which Z^m\hat{Z}_{m} is
intervened, and compute an unmixing vector 𝐮\mathbf{u} via a linear minimum mean square error
(MMSE) estimator,

 𝐮=Cov⁡(Z^m,𝐙^pa^​(m))⋅[Cov⁡(𝐙^pa^​(m))]−1.\mathbf{u}={\rm Cov}(\hat{Z}_{m},\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_  (58)
 {\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)})\cdot\big[{\rm Cov}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)})\big]^{-1}\ .

Then, we update the corresponding row of the encoder as 𝐇^m←𝐇^m−𝐮⋅𝐇^pa^​(m)\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}\
leftarrow\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}-\mathbf{u}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)}. Finally, for the
perfect DAG recovery, we compute the latent score differences under the final encoder estimate and
reconstruct the graph 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} with parent sets

 pa^​(m)≜{i≠m:𝔼⁡[|𝒅𝐙^m​(𝐙^,𝐇^)|i]≠0}.\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\triangleq\Big\{i\neq m:{\mathbb{E}}\Big
 [\big|{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{\mathbf{H}})\big|_{i}\    (59)
 Big]\neq 0\Big\}\ .

Theorem 16 (Linear – Hard Interventions).

Under Assumption 1 for linear transformations, using observational data and interventional data
from one hard intervention per node suffices to identify (i) the latent DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G} perfectly
and (ii) the latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} with scaling consistency. Specifically, Algorithm 1
achieves these identifiability guarantees.

Proof: See Section B.2.

Similar to the restrictions in the existing results for soft interventions, the identifiability
results for hard interventions in the existing literature restrict the latent causal model, e.g.,
to linear causal models (Squires et al., 2023; Buchholz et al., 2023). In contrast, Theorem 16 does
not impose any restriction on the latent causal model and shows that one stochastic hard
intervention per node is sufficient for the identifiability of general latent causal models.

Finally, we comment on the differences between the identifiability results for soft and hard
interventions under missing intervention targets and discuss the identifiability of latent
subgraphs under special cases.

Remark 0.

Let 𝒥⊂[n]\mathcal{J}\subset[n] denote a non-complete set of intervention targets, i.e., 𝒥≠[n]\
mathcal{J}\neq[n]. We note the immediate implications of our results as follows.

 1. 1.
   
    Partial identifiability of latent variables. Theorem 11 immediately implies that for all i∈𝒥i\
    in\mathcal{J} we can recover ZiZ_{i} up to mixing with parents. However, the unmixing procedure
    for hard interventions resolves the mixing with parents sequentially. Specifically, to identify
    ZiZ_{i} up to scaling, we use the fact that the parents of ZiZ_{i} are already identified up to
    scaling. Therefore, even if we have a hard intervention on ZiZ_{i}, we cannot identify ZiZ_{i}
    up to scaling consistency when its parents are not identified up to scaling.

 2. 2.
   
    Ancestrally closed set of interventions. Let 𝒢𝒥\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}} denote the induced
    subgraph of 𝒢\mathcal{G} over the set of nodes 𝒥\mathcal{J}. If 𝒥\mathcal{J} is not ancestrally
    closed, i.e., pa⁡(𝒥)⊈𝒥{\rm{pa}}(\mathcal{J})\not\subseteq\mathcal{J}, then the marginal
    distribution of Z𝒥Z_{\mathcal{J}} is not a causally sufficient model. Conversely, if 𝒥\mathcal
    {J} is an ancestrally closed set, then the marginal of Z𝒥Z_{\mathcal{J}} comes from a causally
    sufficient model with graph 𝒢𝒥\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}. Subsequently, using Stage L2 of
    Algorithm 1 for score differences of 𝐙^𝒥\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathcal{J}}, we obtain the
    transitive closure of 𝒢𝒥\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}. Finally, performing the unmixing procedure
    over the ancestrally closed set of hard interventions, Stage L3 of Algorithm 1 ensures the
    perfect recovery of 𝒢𝒥\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}.

5.4 Identifiability up to Surrounding Parents

For linear transformations, we finally investigate the conditions under which soft interventions
are guaranteed to achieve identifiability results stronger than transitive closure and mixing up to
parents. In particular, we specify one condition on the rank of the score function differences
{𝒅𝐙m:m∈[n]}\{{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}:m\in[n]\}, which is formalized next.

Assumption 2 (Full-rank Score Difference).

For all interventional environments ℰm∈ℰ\mathcal{E}^{m}\in\mathcal{E} we have

 rank⁡(𝐑𝐙m)=|pa¯​(Im)|.\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m})=|\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})|\ .  (60)

For insight into this assumption, it can be readily verified that for linear Gaussian latent models
rank⁡(𝐑𝐙m)≤2\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m})\leq 2 and on the other hand, for sufficiently nonlinear causal
models, rank⁡(𝐑𝐙m)\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m}) is pa¯​(Im)\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m}). This assumption is
stronger than Assumption 1 since it implies that the effects of an intervention on all parents of
the target variable are different. We will provide more discussions on this assumption in Section 
5.5. Under this condition, Algorithm 2 identifies the latent graph and the latent variables with
the following key intuitions.

Latent graph estimation.

Recall that 𝐑𝐗m=[𝐆†]⊤⋅𝐑𝐙m⋅𝐆†\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}=[\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}]^{\top}\cdot\mathbf
{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}\cdot\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}. Since 𝐆†\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}} has full row-rank
nn, Assumption 2 implies that rank⁡(𝐑𝐗m)=|pa¯​(Im)|\rank(\bR_{\bX}^{m})=|\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^
{m})| for all m∈[n]m\in[n], which further implies that Lemma 10 becomes an equality as well, i.e.,

In Algorithm 2, we first obtain a topological order of the true DAG using the graph estimate from

Theorem 18 (Linear – Soft Interventions for Nonlinear SCMs).

Under Assumption 2 for linear transformations, using observational data and interventional data
from one soft intervention per node suffice to identify (i) the latent DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G} perfectly,
and (ii) the latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} with consistency up to mixing with surrounding parents,
and recovered latent variables satisfy Markov property with respect to 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Specifically,
Algorithm 2 achieves these identifiability guarantees.

Proof: Proof of the consistency of latent variables up to mixing with surrounding parents is given
above in complete detail. For the proof of perfect graph recovery and 𝐙^\hat{\mathbf{Z}} satisfies
Markov property with respect to 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}}, see Section B.3.  □\square

Theorem 18 has two important implications. First, the latent DAG can be identified using only soft
interventions under mild nonlinearity assumptions on the latent causal model. To our knowledge,
this is the first result in the literature for fully recovering latent DAG with soft interventions
without restricting the graphical structure, e.g., Zhang et al. (2023) require linear faithfulness
assumption to achieve similar results, which is only shown to hold for nonlinear latent models with
polytree structure. Secondly, the estimated latent variables reveal the true conditional
independence relationships since they satisfy the Markov property with respect to the estimated
latent graph, which is isomorphic to the latent graph. Recalling that the motivation of CRL is to
learn useful representations that preserve causal relationships, our result shows that this can be
achieved without perfect identifiability for a large class of models. Furthermore, Jin and
Syrgkanis (2024, Theorem 3) establish that under some nondegeneracy assumptions, mixing consistency
up to surrounding parents is the best possible result when using single-node soft interventions.
Hence, the results in Theorem 18 are tight for the considered setting.

5.5 Discussion on Assumptions 1 and 2

In this subsection, we elaborate on Assumption 1 and Assumption 2, which are relevant to Theorem 14
and Theorem 18, respectively. Assumption 1 essentially states that score changes in the coordinates
of the intervened node and a parent of the intervened node are linearly independent. This property
holds for (but is not limited to) the widely adopted additive noise models specified in (7) when we
apply hard interventions.

Lemma 0 (Proof in Section D.1).

Assumption 1 is satisfied for additive noise models under hard interventions.

When soft interventions are applied, the transitive closure of 𝒢\mathcal{G} cannot be identified
without making assumptions about the effect of the interventions. Specifically, for linear latent
causal models, Buchholz et al. (2023) prove impossibility results for pure shift interventions and
Squires et al. (2023) show that a genericity condition is necessary for identifying the transitive
closure of the latent DAG. Therefore, Assumption 1 can be interpreted as the counterpart of the
commonly adopted assumptions in the literature on soft interventions adapted to the setting of
general latent causal models. Finally, the next example demonstrates the working of Assumption 1 on
a linear Gaussian latent model.

Example 0.

Consider a linear Gaussian latent model with 𝐙∼𝒩⁡(0,Σ)\mathbf{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma). Score
function of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} is given by 𝐬(𝐳)=−Σ−1⋅𝐳{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})=-\Sigma^{-1}\cdot\
mathbf{z}. Let Zi=𝐰⋅𝐙pa⁡(i)+NiZ_{i}=\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}+N_{i} where Ni∼𝒩⁡
(0,σi2)N_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{i}^{2}) for the node ii. Consider an intervention on node ii
on environment ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} such that Zim=𝐰¯⋅𝐙pa⁡(i)m+N¯iZ^{m}_{i}=\bar{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\
mathbf{Z}^{m}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}+\bar{N}_{i}, where Zm∼𝒩⁡(0,Σ¯)Z_{m}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\Sigma}),
and N¯i∼𝒩⁡(0,σ¯i2)\bar{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}), which yields 𝐝𝐙m(𝐳)=−
(Σ−1−Σ¯−1)⋅𝐳{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})=-(\Sigma^{-1}-\bar{\Sigma}^{-1})\cdot\
mathbf{z}. Then, for a node k∈pa⁡(i)k\in{\rm{pa}}(i), we obtain

 [𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]i\displaystyle  =(1σi2−1σ¯i2)​zi−(𝐰σi2−𝐰¯σ¯i2)​𝐳pa⁡(i),\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}
 [{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\     {\sigma_{i}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right)z_{i}-\left(\  (68)
 mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf   frac{\mathbf{w}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}-\frac{\bar{\mathbf{w}}}{\bar{\
 {z})]_{i}                sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right)\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\ ,
 [𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]k\displaystyle  =−(wkσi2−w¯kσ¯i2)​zi+(wk​𝐰σi2−w¯k​𝐰¯σ¯i2)​𝐳pa⁡(i),\displaystyle=-\
 [{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\     left(\frac{w_{k}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}-\frac{\bar{w}_{k}}{\bar{\sigma}_
 mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf   {i}^{2}}\right)z_{i}+\left(w_{k}\frac{\mathbf{w}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}-\  (69)
 {z})]_{k}                bar{w}_{k}\frac{\bar{\mathbf{w}}}{\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right)\
                          mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\ ,

where wkw_{k} and w¯k\bar{w}_{k} correspond to weights of the parent ZkZ_{k} in observational and
interventional models, respectively. Note that, for Assumption 1 to be violated, there must exists
a constant κ∈ℝ\kappa\in{\mathbb{R}} such that

 κ⋅[𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]i=[𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]k,∀𝐳∈ℝn.\kappa\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\
 big]_{i}=\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{k}\ ,\quad\forall\mathbf    (70)
 {z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ .

However, using (68) and (69), this is possible if and only if wk=w¯kw_{k}=\bar{w}_{k}. Therefore,
if the weight of the node k∈pa⁡(i)k\in{\rm{pa}}(i) changes, Assumption 1 is satisfied for the node
pair (i,k)(i,k).

Given the known result that perfect identifiability is impossible for linear Gaussian models given
soft interventions (Squires et al., 2023), the purpose of Assumption 2 is to get more insight into
the extent of identifiability guarantees under soft interventions. Intuitively, the mentioned
impossibility results for linear Gaussian models are due to the rank deficiency of score
differences for linear models – specifically, we know that rank⁡(𝐑𝐙m)≤2\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m})\leq 2
for linear Gaussian models. In contrast, for sufficiently nonlinear causal models, rank⁡(𝐑𝐙m)\rank
(\bR_{\bZ}^{m}) can be as high as pa¯​(Im)\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m}). Assumption 2 ensures that
this upper bound is satisfied with equality for all nodes. This condition holds for the class of
sufficiently nonlinear models, such as quadratic causal models. In particular, we show that this
condition holds for the two-layer neural networks (NNs) as a function class that can effectively
approximate any continuous function. This result is formalized in the next lemma.

Lemma 0.

Consider the additive model in (7) where fif_{i} is a two-layer NN with sigmoid activation
function, and weight matrices 𝐖i\mathbf{W}^{i} and 𝐖¯i\bar{\mathbf{W}}^{i} for observational and
interventional mechanisms, respectively. If max⁡{rank⁡(𝐖i),rank⁡(𝐖¯i)}=|pa⁡(i)|\max\{\rank(\bW^i)
\,,\,\rank(\bar\bW^i)\}=|{\rm{pa}}(i)| for all i∈[n]i\in[n], then Assumption 2 holds.

We discuss the nonlinearity and the proof of Lemma 21 in Section D.2.

6 CRL under General Transformations

In this setting, we consider general transformations without any parametric assumption for the
transformation gg. In the previous section, we exploited the transformation’s linearity and
recovered the true encoder’s parameters sequentially using one intervention per node. For general
transformations (parametric or nonparametric), however, we cannot use the same parametric approach
and rely on the properties of linear transforms. To rectify these and design the general CRL
algorithm, we use more information in the form of two interventions per node. Specifically, we will
present the steps of leveraging the score functions under two interventions and design an algorithm
that identifies the true encoder and recovers the true causal representations. The algorithm is
referred to as General Score-based Causal Latent Estimation via Interventions (GSCALE-I), which
will be summarized in Algorithm 3. We also present additional results and discuss the role of
various inputs and the distinction of our results compared to the existing literature.

Inputs.

The inputs of GSCALE-I are the observed data from the observational environment, the data from two
interventional environments per node, whether environments are coupled/uncoupled, and a set of
valid encoders ℋ\mathcal{H}. Two sets of interventional environments are denoted by ℰ={ℰ1,…,ℰn}\
mathcal{E}=\{\mathcal{E}^{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}^{n}\} and ℰ~={ℰ~1,…,ℰ~n}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}=\{\tilde
{\mathcal{E}}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{n}\}, as defined in Section 3.3. For these inputs, we
compute the score functions 𝒔𝐗{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}, {𝒔𝐗1,…,𝒔𝐗n}\{{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf
{X}}^{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{n}\} and {𝒔~𝐗1,…,𝒔~𝐗n}\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\
mathbf{X}}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{n}\}. Note that we will use Lemma 8 again
to ensure access to the latent score differences by using these observed score functions.

Statistical diversity.

For being more informative than a single intervention mechanism, we assume that the two
intervention mechanisms per node are sufficiently distinct. This is formalized by defining
interventional discrepancy (Liang et al., 2023) among the causal mechanisms of a latent variable.

Definition 0 (Interventional Discrepancy).

Two intervention mechanisms with pdfs p,q:ℝ→ℝp,q:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}} are said to satisfy
interventional discrepancy if

 ∂∂u​p⁡(u)q⁡(u)≠0,∀u∈ℝ∖𝒯,\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\frac{p(u)}{q(u)}\neq 0\ ,\quad{\forall u  (71)
 \in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\ ,

where 𝒯\mathcal{T} is a null set (i.e., has a zero Lebesgue measure).

This condition ensures that the two distributions are sufficiently distinct, formally expressed as
the partial derivative of their ratio with respect to the intervened variable is nonzero almost
everywhere. For instance, two univariate, distinct Gaussians trivially satisfy this discrepancy
condition. As shown by Liang et al. (2023), even when the latent graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} is known, for
identifiability via one intervention per node, it is necessary to have interventional discrepancy
between observational distribution pip_{i} and interventional distribution qiq_{i}, for all 𝐳pa⁡(i)
∈ℝ|pa⁡(i)|\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}.

6.1 Rationale of GSCALE-I and Partial Identifiability

Similarly to LSCALE-I, analyzing GSCALE-I involves leveraging score differences. However, in
contrast to linear transformations, estimated and true latent score differences are not always
related by a constant matrix for general transformations. To circumvent this issue, we rely on
Lemma 7(iii), i.e., the score difference of coupled interventional environments is one-sparse. As
such, the key idea is to find an encoder h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H} that adheres to this sparsity
structure, and we will show that such an encoder ensures component-wise identifiability of the
latent variables. To formalize these, corresponding to each valid encoder h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H}, we
define the score change matrix 𝐃t​(h)\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h) with entries:

 [𝐃t​(h)]i,m\          ≜𝔼⁡[|𝒔𝐙^m​(𝐙^,h)−𝒔~𝐙^m​(𝐙^,h)|i],\displaystyle\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\
 displaystyle[\mathbf  Big[\,\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)  (72)
 {D}_{\rm t}(h)]_{i,m} -\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\big
                       |_{i}\Big]\ ,

where expectations are under the measures of latent score functions induced by the probability
measure of observational data. We also denote the true score change matrix under true encoder g−1g^
{-1} by 𝐃t≜𝐃t​(g−1)\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\triangleq\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1}) with entries

 [𝐃t]i,m≜𝔼⁡[|𝒔m​(𝐙)−𝒔~m​(𝐙)|i],∀i,m∈[n].[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}]_{i,m}\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\Big
 [\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\  (73)
 ,\quad\forall i,m\in[n]\ .

For clarity in the exposition of the ideas, we consider coupled environments here, i.e., Im=I~mI^
{m}=\tilde{I}^{m} for all m∈[n]m\in[n]. Then, using (33) in Lemma 7 we have

 [𝐃t]i,m≠0⇔i=Im.[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}]_{i,m}\neq 0\;\;\iff\;\;i=I^{m}\ .  (74)

This implies that 𝟙​{𝐃t}\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\} is a permutation matrix, 𝟙​{𝐃t}=𝐏ℐ⊤\mathds
{1}\{\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\top} and

 𝟙{[𝐃t]:,m}=𝐞ℓ,whereℓ=Im,\mathds{1}\{[{\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}}]_{:,m}\}=\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\ ,\quad  (75)
 \mbox{where}\;\ell=I^{m}\ ,

and 𝐞ℓ\mathbf{e}_{\ell} denotes the ℓ\ell-th standard basis vector. Subsequently, the key idea for
identifying the encoder is that the changes between score estimates 𝒔𝐙^m​(𝐳^,h){\boldsymbol{s}}_{\
hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};h) and 𝒔~𝐙^m​(𝐳^,h)\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^
{m}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};h) should match the sparsity structure for those under the true encoder g−1g^
{-1}. In the next result, we leverage the sparsity structure in (75) for two interventions on the
same node to identify an intervened variable, formalized as follows.

Theorem 23 (General – Node-level Identifiability).

Two hard interventions (with interventional discrepancy) on the same target node Im=I~m=ℓI^{m}=\
tilde{I}^{m}=\ell suffice to recover ZℓZ_{\ell} up to a diffeomorphism. In particular, the
following encoder

 h^m=argminh∈ℋ∥[𝐃t(h)]:,m−𝐞m∥2\hat{h}_{m}=\arg\min\limits_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\;\big\|[\mathbf    (76)
 {D}_{\rm t}(h)]_{:,m}-\mathbf{e}_{m}\big\|^{2}

satisfies [𝐙^​(𝐗,h^m)]m=ϕℓ​(Zℓ)\big[\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{h}_{m})\big]_{m}=\phi_{\ell}
(Z_{\ell}) for a diffeomorphism ϕℓ:ℝ→ℝ\phi_{\ell}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}.

We will show shortly that given a complete set of coupled interventions, Theorem 23 will readily
imply the identifiability of all variables (in a similar spirit to going from partial to complete
identifiability in Section 5). Furthermore, the results for uncoupled interventions will also
leverage this key result. Hence, we provide the detailed proof of Theorem 23 as follows.

Proof: We start by providing a direct result of Corollary 9, which will be used in multiple
instances in this section. Consider an encoder h∈ℋh\in\mathcal{H} and an invertible matrix 𝐀∈𝖦𝖫⁡
(n,ℝ)\mathbf{A}\in{\sf GL}(n,{\mathbb{R}}). Then, using Corollary 9, score functions of 𝐙^​(𝐗,h)\

 \cdot h)=\mathbf{A}^{-\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\ .

Recall that score change matrices are defined via the absolute value of the score differences.

 mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)\ ,\quad\forall h\in\mathcal{H}\ .

entries. Thus, using (78), we have 𝐃t​(h∗)=𝐃t−1⋅𝐃t=𝐈n×n\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\rm h^{*})=\mathbf{D}_{\

Next, we show that any solution of (76) recovers Z^ℓ\hat{Z}_{\ell} up to a diffeomorphism.

Consider encoder h^m∈ℋ\hat{h}_{m}\in\mathcal{H} that satisfies [𝐃t(h^m)]:,m=𝐞m[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\

6.2 Complete Identifiability under Coupled Interventions

After achieving the node-level latent variable identifiability from one pair of coupled
interventions, we extend the objective function in Theorem 23 for a complete set of coupled
interventions.

Encoder estimation.

In Stage G1 of Algorithm 3, we combine the objectives in (76) for all m∈[n]m\in[n] and form our
encoder estimate by solving the following optimization problem

 minh∈ℋ⁡‖𝐃t​(h)−𝐈n×n‖F2.\min\limits_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)-\mathbf{I}_   (83)
 {n\times n}\big\|_{\rm F}^{2}\ .

Recall that the valid encoders set ℋ\mathcal{H} consists of invertible encoders over the input
space 𝒳\mathcal{X}. Hence, (83) is readily equivalent to solving the following problem for any λ∈ℝ+
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}:

 minh⁡‖𝐃t​(h)−𝐈n×n‖F2+λ​𝔼​[‖h−1​(h⁡(𝐗))−𝐗‖22].\min\limits_{h}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)-\
 mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\big\|_{\rm F}^{2}\;+\;\lambda\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\|h^{-1}(h(\mathbf  (84)
 {X}))-\mathbf{X}\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big]\ .

Lemma 0.

Given a complete set of coupled interventions that satisfy interventional discrepancy, output h^\
hat{h} of Algorithm 3 satisfies component-wise latent recovery.

Proof: Following Theorem 23 for all m∈[n]m\in[n] immediately implies the desired result. First,
recall that h∗=𝐃t⋅g−1h^{*}=\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\cdot g^{-1} satisfies 𝐃t​(h∗)=𝐈n×n\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}
(h^{*})=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n} and belongs to ℋ\mathcal{H}. Therefore, h∗h^{*} makes the objective
(84) zero. Next, consider a solution h^\hat{h} that makes (84) zero. Since h^\hat{h} makes the
reconstruction loss term zero, i.e., it has a valid inverse h^−1\hat{h}^{-1}, h^\hat{h} belongs to
the set of valid encoders ℋ\mathcal{H}. Then, for all m∈[n]m\in[n], we have [𝐃t(h^)]:,m=𝐞m[\mathbf
{D}_{\rm t}(\hat{h})]_{:,m}=\mathbf{e}_{m}. By Theorem 23, this implies

 𝐙^​(𝐗,h^)=𝐏ℐ⋅ϕ⁡(𝐙),\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{h})=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\phi(\   (85)
 mathbf{Z})\ ,

where ϕ⁡(𝐙)=(ϕ1​(Z1),…,ϕn​(Zn))\phi(\mathbf{Z})=(\phi_{1}(Z_{1}),\dots,\phi_{n}(Z_{n})) is a
componentwise diffeomorphism, which concludes the proof.  □\square

Latent graph estimation.

Next, we use the encoder estimate h^\hat{h} for recovering the latent graph 𝒢\mathcal{G}. In
Stage G2 of Algorithm 3, we use the score differences between observational and interventional
environments and form 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} with parent sets

 pa^​(m)≜{i≠m:𝔼⁡[|𝒔𝐙^​(𝐙^,h^)−𝒔𝐙^m​(𝐙^,h^)|i]≠0},∀m∈[n].\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\triangleq\Big\{i\neq
 m:{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})-{\      (86)
 boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\Big\}\ ,
 \quad\forall m\in[n]\ .

Lemma 0.

Given a complete set of coupled interventions that satisfy interventional discrepancy, Algorithm 3
output 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} and true latent DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G} are related through a graph
isomorphism.

Proof: Denote f=h^∘gf=\hat{h}\circ g, so we have 𝐙^=f⁡(𝐙)\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=f(\mathbf{Z}). Using the

 {Z}};\hat{h})\big|_{i}=\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{i,I^{i}}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol
 {s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{i}\Big|

 i∈pa^​(m)⇔𝔼⁡[[𝒔⁡(𝐙)−𝒔m​(𝐙)]Ii]≠0⇔Ii∈pa⁡(Im),i\in\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\;\;\iff\;\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big
 [\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{i}}\Big]\neq 0   (88)

which concludes the proof that 𝒢\mathcal{G} and 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} are related through a graph

Theorem 26 (General – Coupled Environments).

Using observational data and interventional data from two coupled hard environments for which the
pair (qi,q~i)(q_{i},\tilde{q}_{i}) satisfies interventional discrepancy for all i∈[n]i\in[n],
suffice to identify (i) the latent DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G} perfectly and (ii) the latent variables 𝐙\
mathbf{Z} up to componentwise diffeomorphisms specified in Definition 5. Specifically, Algorithm 3
achieves these identifiability guarantees.

Proof: Combining Lemma 24 and Lemma 25 gives the theorem statements.  □\square

We emphasize that a key contribution of Theorem 26 beyond the identifiability result is that it
provides a well-defined objective function, a minimizer of which provably recovers the latent
variables and the graph up to identifiability guarantees. To our knowledge, this is the first such
result for interventional CRL with general transformations. In comparison, von Kügelgen et al.
(2023) also use two hard interventions per node to prove identifiability, albeit without providing
a provably correct algorithm. Furthermore, the result in (von Kügelgen et al., 2023) requires that
the estimated latent distribution is faithful to the associated candidate graph for all h∈ℋh\in\
mathcal{H}. Although a faithfulness assumption does not compromise the identifiability result, it
is a strong requirement to verify and poses challenges to devise recovery algorithms. In contrast,
we only require observational data, which is generally accessible in practice.

Next, we shed light on the role of observational data. Lemma 24 requires only interventional data
for identifying the encoder, whereas Lemma 25 uses observational data. We further tighten this
result by showing that for DAG recovery, the observational data becomes unnecessary when we have
additive noise models and a weak faithfulness condition holds.

Theorem 27 (No Observational Data).

Using interventional data from two coupled hard environments for which the pair (qi,q~i)(q_{i},\
tilde{q}_{i}) satisfies interventional discrepancy for all i∈[n]i\in[n], suffices to identify the
latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} up to component-wise diffeomorphisms. If the latent causal model has
additive noise, p⁡(𝐙)p(\mathbf{Z}) is twice differentiable and satisfies the adjacency-faithfulness
with respect to 𝒢\mathcal{G}, then the latent DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G} is also identifiable.

Proof sketch: The recovery of latent variables follow from Lemma 24. For the recovery of latent
DAG, we leverage Lemma 7(iv) under the additive noise assumption. Specifically, using the score
difference between environments ℰi\mathcal{E}^{i} and ℰ~j\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{j}, we obtain pa¯​
(i,j)\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j) for all i,j∈[n],i≠ji,j\in[n],i\neq j. Finally, we perform at most nn
conditional independence tests to identify all parent sets {pa⁡(i):i∈[n]}\{{\rm{pa}}(i):i\in[n]\}.
See Section C.1 for the complete proof.

6.3 Complete Identifiability under Uncoupled Interventions

In this setting, we consider the case where the interventional environments corresponding to the
same nodes are not specified in pairs. That is, not only is it unknown which node is intervened in
an environment, but the learner also does not know which two environments intervene on the same
node. Hence, additionally, we need to determine the correct coupling between the interventional
environment sets ℰ\mathcal{E} and ℰ~\tilde{\mathcal{E}} as well. However, this is not a
straightforward objective since we cannot readily determine the intervention target in an
environment. To address this issue, we solve the problems of finding the correct coupling and
optimizing the estimated score variations together. Let σ\sigma denote the permutation that takes
ℐ~=(I~1,…,I~n)\tilde{\mathcal{I}}=(\tilde{I}^{1},\dots,\tilde{I}^{n}) to ℐ=(I1,…,In)\mathcal{I}=(I^
{1},\dots,I^{n}), i.e., σ⁡(I~m)=Im\sigma(\tilde{I}^{m})=I^{m} for all m∈[n]m\in[n]. We will modify
the objective function in (84) to solve for permutation π\pi as an estimate of σ\sigma, in addition
to solving for encoder hh. To this end, we first modify the score change matrix in (72) and define
𝐃t​(h,π)\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h;\pi) as

 [𝐃t​(h,π)]i,m≜𝔼⁡[|𝒔𝐙^m​(𝐙^,h)−𝒔~𝐙^πm​(𝐙^,h)|i],[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h;\pi)]_{i,m}\triangleq{\
 mathbb{E}}\Big[\,\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)-\tilde{\    (89)
 boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\pi_{m}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\big|_{i}\Big]\ ,

Unlike the case of coupled interventions, we will also leverage the observational data to identify
the encoder. As such, we also define the score change matrices for ℰ\mathcal{E} and ℰ~\tilde{\
mathcal{E}} with respect to ℰ\mathcal{E} with entries

 [𝐃⁡(h)]i,m\           ≜𝔼⁡[|𝒔𝐙^​(𝐙^,h)−𝒔𝐙^m​(𝐙^,h)|i],\displaystyle\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\Big
 displaystyle[\mathbf  [\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)-{\        (90)
 {D}(h)]_{i,m}         boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\big|_{i}\
                       Big]\ ,
 [𝐃~​(h)]i,m\          ≜𝔼⁡[|𝒔𝐙^​(𝐙^,h)−𝒔~𝐙^m​(𝐙^,h)|i].\displaystyle\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\
 displaystyle[\tilde{\ Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)-\      (91)
 mathbf{D}}(h)]_{i,m}  tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\big|_
                       {i}\Big]\ .

Encoder and coupling estimation.

We modify the objective in (84) and solve the following constrained optimization problem for any
λ∈ℝ+\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}:

                                 _{n\times n}\ .

Note that for the true encoder g−1g^{-1}, both 𝐃⁡(g−1)\mathbf{D}(g^{-1}) and 𝐃~​(g−1)\tilde{\mathbf
{D}}(g^{-1}) capture the graph structure via Lemma 7(i), albeit under different permutations.
Hence, this equivalence of graph structures for the learned encoder is ensured by the first
constraint of 𝟙​{𝐃⁡(h)}=𝐏π⋅𝟙​{𝐃~​(h)}\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h)\}=\mathbf{P}_{\pi}\cdot\mathds{1}\{\
tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h)\}. Also, the acyclicity of the graphs implied by the score differences is
ensured by the second constraint, that is 𝐃⁡(h)\mathbf{D}(h) does not contain 2-cycles. To prove
that solving this problem leads to the identifiability of the latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z}, we
first show that there exists a global minimizer of this problem.

Lemma 0 (Existence).

Encoder h∗=[𝐃t​(g−1,σ)]⊤⋅g−1h^{*}=\big[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)\big]^{\top}\cdot g^{-1}
and permutation π∗=σ\pi^{*}=\sigma minimize the objective in (92).

Proof sketch: See Section C.2 for the complete proof. The main intuition is that π∗=σ\pi^{*}=\sigma
makes the problem more similar to the case of coupled interventions. The proof then follows by
using the results of the coupled interventions to show that 𝐃t​(g−1,σ)\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\
sigma) is a scaled permutation matrix, using (77) to derive the relationship between the score
differences under h^∗\hat{h}^{*} and the true encoder g−1g^{-1}, and using the sparsity structure
of 𝐃⁡(g−1)\mathbf{D}(g^{-1}) to show that the constraints are satisfied.

Next, we show that the objective in (92) can attain its minimum value zero only for the correct
coupling.

Lemma 0 (Feasibility).

The optimization problem in (92) attains its minimum value zero only for the correct coupling π=σ\
pi=\sigma.

Proof sketch: See Section C.3 for the complete proof. The main intuition is that the constraints
make it impossible to achieve 𝐃t​(h,π)=𝐈n×n\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h;\pi)=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n} for an
incorrect coupling π≠σ\pi\neq\sigma. We prove it by contradiction. We assume that h∗h^{*} is a
solution, hence, 𝐃t​(h∗)=𝐈n×n\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h^{*})=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}, and 𝟙​{𝐃​(h)}=𝟙​{𝐃~​
(h)}\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h)\}=\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h)\}. Then, by scrutinizing the
eldest node in 𝒢\mathcal{G} with mismatched interventional environments, we show that 𝐃⁡(h∗)⊙[𝐃⁡
(h∗)]⊤\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\odot[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})]^{\top} cannot be a diagonal matrix, which
contradicts the premise that h∗h^{*} is a feasible solution of (84).

Finally, we denote the minimizer encoder of (92) by h^\hat{h} and form the graph estimate similarly
to the coupled intervention case via (86). Combining these results, we present our strongest result
for general transformations.

Theorem 30 (General – Uncoupled Environments).

Using observational data and interventional data from two uncoupled hard environments for which
each pair in (pi,qi,q~i)(p_{i},q_{i},\tilde{q}_{i}) satisfies interventional discrepancy for all i∈
[n]i\in[n], suffice to identify (i) the latent DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G} perfectly and (ii) the latent
variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} up to component-wise diffeomorphisms.

Proof: Lemma 29 shows that minimizers of the objective in (92) necessarily have π=σ\pi=\sigma.
Under this correct coupling, any minimizer of the constrained optimization problem in (92) that
attains the minimum value zero is also a minimizer of the unconstrained optimization problem in (84
). Finally, Lemma 24 and Lemma 28 show that such a shared minimizer exists, that is h∗=[𝐃t​(g−1)]
⊤⋅g−1h^{*}=[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1})]^{\top}\cdot g^{-1}. Hence, by Theorem 26, minimizer h^\hat
{h} of (92) satisfies component-wise latent recovery. Similarly, using h^\hat{h}, proof of the
perfect graph recovery follows from Lemma 25.  □\square

Theorem 30 shows that using observational data enables us to resolve any mismatch between the
uncoupled environment sets and shows identifiability in the setting of uncoupled environments. This
generalizes the identifiability result of von Kügelgen et al. (2023), which requires coupled
environments.

Comparison to Theorem 26.

We note that Theorem 30 requires slightly stronger interventional discrepancy conditions than
Theorem 26. In particular, when environments are coupled, we only need (qi,q~i)(q_{i},\tilde{q}_
{i}) to satisfy the interventional discrepancy. On the other hand, to find the correct coupling
while performing CRL, Theorem 30 requires each of the pairs {(qi,q~i),(pi,qi),(pi,q~i)}\{(q_{i},\
tilde{q}_{i}),(p_{i},q_{i}),(p_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})\} to satisfy interventional discrepancy.

Remark 0.

For the nonparametric identifiability results, having an oracle that solves the functional
optimization problems (84) and (92) is sufficient. Solving these two problems in their most general
form requires calculus of variations. These two problems, however, for any desired parameterized
family of functions ℋ\mathcal{H} (e.g., linear, polynomial, and neural networks), reduce to
parametric optimization problems.

6.4 Intervention Extrapolation without CRL

So far, we have studied learning the latent causal representations using the data from single-node
interventional environments. A related research problem is extrapolating to unseen combinations of
interventions. Namely, given a set of interventions ℐ={I1,…,Ik}\mathcal{I}=\{I^{1},\dots,I^{k}\},
it is desired to emulate the data from an unseen combination of these interventions, e.g.,
accessing multi-node interventional data using only the single-node interventional data. This is
especially important in domains where interventions can be costly or not viable, e.g., not all
combinations of different drugs can be clinically tested. Causal representation learning literature
has also taken an interest in this problem. Specifically, Zhang et al. (2023, Theorem 3) show for
polynomial transformations that given interventions ℐ={I1,…,Ik}\mathcal{I}=\{I^{1},\dots,I^{k}\},
one can sample from any intervention I⊆ℐI\subseteq\mathcal{I} after learning the latent
representations. We argue that extrapolation to unseen combinations of interventions can be
achieved on the observed space without performing CRL for the general transformations.

Consider two single-node interventional environments, ℰ1\mathcal{E}^{1} and ℰ2\mathcal{E}^{2}.
Without loss of generality, suppose that I1={1}I^{1}=\{1\} and I2={2}I^{2}=\{2\}. Also consider the
observational environment ℰ0\mathcal{E}^{0} with I0=∅I^{0}=\emptyset and the unseen double-node
interventional environment ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} with Im={1,2}I^{m}=\{1,2\}. First, using the score
function decompositions in (18) and (19), we have

                    =∇𝐳logp1(z1∣𝐳pa⁡(1))+∇𝐳logp2(z2∣𝐳pa⁡(2))+∑k=3n∇𝐳logpk(zk∣𝐳pa⁡(k)),\

This means that, given score functions of observed data from environments ℰ0\mathcal{E}^{0}, ℰ1\
mathcal{E}^{1}, and ℰ2\mathcal{E}^{2}, we can obtain the score function of the unseen
interventional environment with Im={I1,I2}I^{m}=\{I^{1},I^{2}\}, and subsequently generate data
from this synthetic environment (using techniques like Langevin sampling (Welling and Teh, 2011)
that uses score function as the drift vector field). Note that this process does not require
learning the latent causal representations and can be applied to obtain the observed score function
𝒔𝐗m{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m} for any intervention Im⊂ℐI^{m}\subset\mathcal{I}.

Finally, we note that concurrent work by Jain et al. (2024) makes a similar observation for the
extrapolation of two single-node interventions without performing CRL. In particular, (Jain et al.,
2024) focuses on detecting pairwise interactions between two biological perturbations (i.e.,
interventions), investigates the conditions under which two perturbations are separable, e.g.,
distinct single-node interventions, and designs statistical tests for the separability of two
perturbations. If the two perturbations are confirmed to be separable, our results indicate that we
can use their score functions to generate synthetic environments that emulate combined perturbation
effects.

7 Empirical Studies

In this section, we provide empirical assessments of our theoretical guarantees. Specifically, we
empirically evaluate the performance of the LSCALE-I (Section 7.1) and GSCALE-I (Section 7.2)
algorithms for recovering the latent causal variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} and the latent DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G}
on synthetic data. In Section 7.3, we compare the performance of LSCALE-I to that of the existing
algorithms in the closely related literature on both synthetic and biological data. Next, we also
apply GSCALE-I on image data to demonstrate the potential of our approach in realistic
high-dimensional datasets (Section 7.5). Finally, we note that any desired score estimator can be
modularly incorporated into our algorithms. In Section 7.6, we assess our performance’s sensitivity
to the estimators’ quality. ^7^7 7 The codebase for the algorithms and simulations is available at:
https://github.com/acarturk-e/score-based-crl. Additional results and further implementation
details are deferred to Appendix E.

Evaluation metrics.

The objectives are recovering the graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} and the latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z}. We use
the following metrics to evaluate the accuracy of LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I in recovering these
(depending on the specifics of the transformations and interventions, we will also have more
specific metrics). For each metric, we will report the mean and standard error over multiple runs.

  • •
   
    Structural Hamming distance: For assessing the recovery of the latent DAG, we report structural
    Hamming distance (SHD) between the estimate 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} and true DAG 𝒢\mathcal{G}. This
    captures the number of edge operations (add, delete, flip) needed to transform 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal
    {G}} to 𝒢\mathcal{G}.

  • •
   
    Mean correlation coefficient: For the recovery of the latent variables, we use the mean
    correlation coefficient (MCC), which was introduced in (Khemakhem et al., 2020b) and commonly
    used as a standard metric in CRL. Specifically, MCC measures the linear correlation between the
    estimated and ground-truth latent variables. Since the recovery of latent variables is up to
    permutations, it is reported for the best matching permutation between the components of 𝐙^\hat
    {\mathbf{Z}} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z}, i.e.,

     MCC⁡(𝐙,𝐙^)≜maxπ⁡1n​∑i∈[n]corr⁡(Zi,Z^π⁡(i)).{\rm MCC}(\mathbf{Z},\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\       (101)
     triangleq\max_{\pi}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}{\rm corr}(Z_{i},\hat{Z}_{\pi(i)})\ .

Score functions.

LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I algorithms, in their first steps, compute estimates of the score differences
in the observational environment. The designs of our algorithms are agnostic to how this is
performed, i.e., any reliable method for estimating the score differences can be adopted and
incorporated into our algorithms in a modular way. In our experiments, we adopt two score
estimators necessary for describing the different aspects of our results.

  • •
   
    Perfect score oracle for identifiability: Identifiability, by definition, refers to the
    possibility of recovering the causal graph and latent variables under all idealized assumptions
    for the data. Assessing the identifiability guarantees formalized in Theorems 14–30 requires
    using perfect estimates for the score differences. Hence, we adopt a perfect score oracle for
    evaluating identifiability. Specifically, we use a perfect score oracle that computes the score
    difference inputs in LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I by leveraging Lemma 8 and using the ground truth
    score functions 𝒔,𝒔m{\boldsymbol{s}},{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m} and 𝒔~m\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}
    (see Section E.1 for details).

  • •
   
    Data-driven score estimates: For evaluating the accuracy of our algorithms in practice, we need
    real score estimates, which are inevitably noisy. For this purpose, when the pdf p𝐗p_{\mathbf
    {X}} has a parametric form and the score function 𝒔𝐗{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}} has a
    closed-form expression, then we can estimate the parameters to form an estimated score
    function. For instance, when 𝐗\mathbf{X} follows a linear Gaussian distribution, then we have
    𝒔𝐗(𝐱)=−Θ⋅𝐱{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})=-\Theta\cdot\mathbf{x} in which Θ\Theta is
    the precision matrix of 𝐗\mathbf{X} and can be estimated from samples of 𝐗\mathbf{X}. In other
    cases in which 𝒔𝐗{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}} does not have a known closed-form, we adopt
    nonparametric score estimators. In particular, we use sliced score matching with variance
    reduction (SSM-VR) for score estimation due to its efficiency and accuracy for downstream
    tasks (Song et al., 2020). We also introduce a classification-based score difference estimation
    method, inspired by Gutmann and Hyvärinen (2012, Section 2.1). The key observation is that
    given two distributions pp and qq, the optimal minimum cross-entropy classifier for
    distinguishing the samples from two distributions is the log density ratio function log⁡p/q\log
    p/q. The difference between the score functions of these distributions, ∇log⁡p−∇log⁡q=∇log⁡p/q\
    nabla\log p-\nabla\log q=\nabla\log p/q is exactly the gradient of the learned function, which
    enables us to estimate score differences using a classifier directly.

7.1 LSCALE-I Algorithm for Linear Transformations

Data generation.

To generate 𝒢\mathcal{G}, we use Erdős-Rényi model with density 0.50.5 and n∈{5,8}n\in\{5,8\}
nodes, which is generally the size of the latent graphs considered in CRL literature. We consider
the observed dimension d=100d=100 and generate 100 latent graphs. For the causal mechanisms, we
adopt both linear and nonlinear models:

 1. 1.
   
    Linear causal model: We adopt the linear Gaussian model with

     Zi=𝐀i⋅𝐙+Ni,∀i∈[n],Z_{i}=\mathbf{A}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{Z}+N_{i}\ ,\qquad\forall i\in[n]\ ,  (102)

    where 𝐀i∈ℝ1×n\mathbf{A}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1\times n} are the rows of the weight matrix 𝐀\
    mathbf{A} in which 𝐀i,j≠0\mathbf{A}_{i,j}\neq 0 if and only j∈pa⁡(i)j\in{\rm{pa}}(i). The
    nonzero edge weights are sampled from Unif⁡(±[0.5,1.5]){\rm Unif}(\pm[0.5,1.5]), and the noise
    terms are zero-mean Gaussian variables with variances σi2\sigma_{i}^{2} sampled from Unif⁡
    ([0.5,1.5]){\rm Unif}([0.5,1.5]). For node ii, a hard intervention is given by Zi=N¯iZ_{i}=\bar
    {N}_{i} where N¯i∼𝒩⁡(0,σi24)\bar{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{4}), and a soft
    intervention is given by Zi=𝐀¯i2⋅𝐙+NiZ_{i}=\frac{\bar{\mathbf{A}}_{i}}{2}\cdot\mathbf{Z}+N_{i}.

 2. 2.
   
    Quadratic causal model: We adopt an additive noise model with

     Zi=𝐙pa⁡(i)⊤⋅𝐐i⋅𝐙pa⁡(i)+Ni,\displaystyle Z_{i}=\sqrt{\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{\top}\    (103)
     cdot\mathbf{Q}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}}+N_{i}\ ,

    where {𝐐i:i∈[n]}\{\mathbf{Q}_{i}:i\in[n]\} are positive-definite matrices, and the noise terms
    are zero-mean Gaussian variables with variances σi2\sigma_{i}^{2} sampled randomly from Unif⁡
    ([0.5,1.5]){\rm Unif}([0.5,1.5]). For node ii, a hard intervention is given by Zi=N¯iZ_{i}=\bar
    {N}_{i} where N¯i∼𝒩⁡(0,5⋅σi2)\bar{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,5\cdot\sigma_{i}^{2}) to ensure a
    non-negligible change. A soft intervention is given by

     Zi=12​𝐙pa⁡(i)⊤⋅𝐐i⋅𝐙pa⁡(i)+N¯i.Z_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{\top}\cdot\
     mathbf{Q}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}}+\bar{N}_{i}\ .

 3. 3.
   
    Multi-layer perceptron (MLP) causal model: We adopt an additive noise model with Zi=fi​(𝐙pa⁡
    (i))+NiZ_{i}=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+N_{i} where fif_{i} is parameterized as a
    randomly initialized two-layer MLP, with hidden dimension 32 and ReLU activation function. For
    node ii, a hard intervention is given by Zi=N¯iZ_{i}=\bar{N}_{i} where 𝒩⁡(0,5⋅σi2)\mathcal{N}
    (0,5\cdot\sigma_{i}^{2}), and a soft intervention is given by Zi=12​fi​(𝐙pa⁡(i))+N¯iZ_{i}=\frac
    {1}{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+\bar{N}_{i}.

For each graph, we sample nsn_{\rm s} independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) samples of 𝐙
\mathbf{Z} from each environment. We consider ns∈{5000,10000,50000}n_{\rm s}\in\{5000,10000,50000\}
to investigate the effect of the number of samples on the performance of LSCALE-I. The observed
variables 𝐗\mathbf{X} are generated according to 𝐗=𝐆⋅𝐙\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}, in
which 𝐆∈ℝd×n\mathbf{G}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times n} is a randomly sampled full-rank matrix.

7.1.1 Hard Interventions

Theorem 16 ensures scaling consistency and perfect DAG recovery under hard interventions for linear
transformations. As such, we assess the recovery of the latent DAG by the SHD between the estimate
𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} and true graph 𝒢\mathcal{G}. For latent variable recovery, we report MCC
between 𝐙^\hat{\mathbf{Z}} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z}. Note that 𝐙^=(𝐇⋅𝐆)⋅𝐙\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=(\mathbf{H}\cdot\
mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{Z} implies that the effective transform recovery can also be measured by the
closeness of 𝐇⋅𝐆\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G} to the identity matrix. Therefore, in addition to MCC, we
also report the normalized effective transform error, defined as

 ℓscale≜‖𝐇⋅𝐆−𝐈n×n‖2.\ell_{\rm scale}\triangleq\|\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{I}_{n\      (104)
 times n}\|_{2}\ .

Table 3 shows the performance of the LSCALE-I algorithm using perfect scores and noisy scores under
hard interventions on linear causal models. The first observation is that we achieve excellent
performance in latent variable recovery, as demonstrated by a perfect MCC and nearly zero effective
transform error ℓscale\ell_{\rm scale}, even when using noisy score estimates. For graph recovery,
SHD between the estimated and true latent graphs reduces to less than 0.1 even when using noisy
scores given enough samples, e.g., ns=50000n_{s}=50000 in Table 3. Another key observation is that
the results remain consistent while the dimension of observed variables increases from d=25d=25 to
d=200d=200, as shown in Table 4. This confirms our analysis that the performance of LSCALE-I is
agnostic to the dimension of the observations. Hence, we suffice by using d=100d=100 for the rest
of the experiments.

Causal models with more complex score functions.

Next, we test the performance of LSCALE-I on quadratic and MLP causal models specified earlier. A
difference in this setting compared to linear causal models is that we estimate the score functions
of the observed variables using SSM-VR (Song et al., 2020) as p𝐗p_{\mathbf{X}} is not amenable to
parameter estimation. For quadratic causal models, Table 5 shows that using perfect scores,
LSCALE-I performs nearly perfectly. Under noisy scores, an MCC of 0.93 indicates a strong
performance for the latent variable recovery. The graph recovery performance suffers more from
noisy score estimates, yet it remains reasonable with an approximate SHD of 2.622.62 (where the
expected number of true edges is 55). Table 6 shows a similar trend, where perfect scores ensure
perfect performance, whereas noisy score estimates lead to a degradation in graph recovery
performance.

Effect of noisy score estimates on graph recovery under noisy scores.

Table 5 and Table 6 show that graph recovery performance suffers more than the latent variable
recovery under noisy score estimates. This discrepancy is due to the difficulty of applying (59)
under noisy scores. Specifically, we observe that the nonzero entries in true score differences
(entries of 𝒅m{\boldsymbol{d}}^{m}) can be small, especially for MLP causal models. As such, under
noisy score estimates, it becomes more difficult to successfully threshold the empirical score
difference quantities to identify the edges of the latent graph. In Section 7.6, we provide further
empirical evaluations on the effect of score estimation quality on the performance of LSCALE-I.

7.1.2 Soft Interventions

To evaluate soft interventions, we first consider linear causal models. In this setting, Theorem 14
ensures the recovery of the latent variables and the latent DAG up to ancestors when using soft
interventions. So, we report the SHD between the transitive closure of the estimate 𝒢^tc\hat{\
mathcal{G}}_{\rm tc} and that of the true graph 𝒢tc\mathcal{G}_{\rm tc}. Recall that recovery of
latent variables for generic soft interventions is guaranteed up to mixing with parents. To measure
the accuracy of 𝐙^=𝐇⋅𝐆⋅𝐙\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z} with respect to
this guarantee, we define 𝐋pa\mathbf{L}_{\rm pa} with entries [𝐋pa]i,j=𝟙{j∈pa¯(i)}[\mathbf{L}_{\rm
pa}]_{i,j}=\mathds{1}\{j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\}, and define ℓpa\ell_{\rm pa} by

 ℓpa≜‖𝐇⋅𝐆⊙(𝟏n×n−𝐋pa)‖2,\ell_{\rm pa}\triangleq\|\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}\odot(\boldsymbol    (105)
 {1}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{L}_{\rm pa})\|_{2}\ ,

which effectively measures the effect of incorrect mixing in estimated latent variables. Table 7
shows that by using perfect scores from as few as ns=5000n_{\rm s}=5000 samples, we meet the
theoretical identifiability guarantees, implied by zero values of SHD and incorrect mixing norm ℓpa
\ell_{\rm pa}. Similar to the case of hard interventions, we observe that increasing the number of
samples improves the performance under noisy scores. For instance, given ns=50000n_{\rm s}=50000
samples, the average SHD between the transitive closures 𝒢^tc\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm tc} and 𝒢tc\
mathcal{G}_{\rm tc} is approximately 0.50.5 for n=8n=8, where graph density being 0.50.5 implies
that the expected number of edges is 1414. Also, the latent variable recovery becomes near perfect,
indicated by MCC of 0.980.98 and near-zero incorrect mixing norm ℓpa\ell_{\rm pa}.

Next, we consider quadratic causal models. In this case, Assumption 2 is satisfied, and Theorem 18
ensures the perfect recovery of the latent DAG and the recovery of the latent variables up to
surrounding variables. Hence, we report SHD between true 𝒢\mathcal{G} and estimate G^\hat{G}, and
the incorrect mixing norm for this setting given by

 ℓsur≜‖𝐇⋅𝐆⊙(𝟏n×n−𝐋sur)‖2,\ell_{\rm sur}\triangleq\|\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}\odot(\           (106)
 boldsymbol{1}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{L}_{\rm sur})\|_{2}\ ,

where 𝐋sur\mathbf{L}_{\rm sur} is defined with entries [𝐋pa]i,j=𝟙{j∈sur¯(i)}[\mathbf{L}_{\rm pa}]_
{i,j}=\mathds{1}\{j\in\overline{{\rm{sur}}}(i)\}. Table 8 shows that when using perfect scores,
LSCALE-I performs nearly perfectly, verifying the results of Theorem 18. Similarly to the case of
hard interventions, the performance of LSCALE-I suffers under noisy score estimations, while it
remains reasonable, e.g., an SHD of 2.792.79 with respect to the true graph when using only soft
interventions.

7.2 GSCALE-I Algorithm for General Transformations

Next, we focus on nonlinear transformations to showcase the settings for which the existing
interventional CRL literature lacks provably correct algorithms and provides only identifiability
results under nonlinear transformations.

Choice of nonlinearity.

In this section, we consider M-layer perceptrons with tanh\tanh activation as our class of
nonlinear transformations. Specifically, we consider functions of the form

 𝐗=g(𝐙)=(tanh∘𝐀M∘⋯tanh∘𝐀1)(𝐙),\mathbf{X}=g(\mathbf{Z})=(\tanh\circ\mathbf{A}^{M}\circ\cdots\  (107)
 tanh\circ\mathbf{A}^{1})(\mathbf{Z})\ ,

in which tanh\tanh is applied element-wise, and parameters {𝐀1,…,𝐀M}\{\mathbf{A}^{1},\dots,\mathbf
{A}^{M}\} are compatible, randomly sampled full column rank matrices. We consider two settings of
increasing complexity: 1-layer MLP, and 3-layer MLP (for nonparametric transformations, see
experiments on images in Section 7.5).

7.2.1 Single-layer MLP

First, consider the single-layer perceptron as the nonlinear transformation gg, given by

 𝐗=g⁡(𝐙)=tanh⁡(𝐆⋅𝐙),\mathbf{X}=g(\mathbf{Z})=\tanh(\bG\cdot\bZ)\ ,  (108)

where 𝐆∈ℝd×n\mathbf{G}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times n}. Because it is a relatively simple nonlinear
transformation, this setting enables us to evaluate the performance of GSCALE-I more effectively.
Specifically, leveraging (108), we parameterize valid encoders hh with parameter 𝐇∈ℝn×d\mathbf{H}\
in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}, which gives

 𝐙^​(𝐗,h)\displaystyle\hat  =h⁡(𝐗)=𝐇⋅arctanh⁡(𝐗),\displaystyle=h(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{H}\cdot  (109)
 {\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h) \rm arctanh(\mathbf{X})\ ,
 𝐗^\displaystyle\hat{\      =h−1​(𝐙^​(𝐗,h))=tanh⁡(𝐇†⋅𝐙^​(𝐗,h)),\displaystyle=h^{-1}(\hat{\
 mathbf{X}}                 mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h))=\tanh(\bH^{\dag} \cdot\hat\bZ(\bX;h))\  (110)
                            ,
 𝐙^\displaystyle\hat{\      =𝐇⋅𝐆⋅𝐙.\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ .  (111)
 mathbf{Z}}

This is equivalent to reducing the original nonparametric functional estimation problem to a
finite-dimensional parameter estimation problem. This enables stable training and large-scale
testing. Furthermore, this parametrization enables us to compute the ground truth score functions
of the observed variables, allowing us to directly assess the effect of score estimation errors on 
GSCALE-I performance.

Data generation.

In this setting, we focus on quadratic causal models. To generate 𝒢\mathcal{G} we use the
Erdős-Rényi model with density 0.50.5 and n∈{5,8}n\in\{5,8\} nodes. For observational causal
mechanisms, we use Equation (103) and the corresponding parameterization. For the two hard
interventions on node ii, ZiZ_{i} is set to Nq,i∼𝒩⁡(0,σq,i2)N_{q,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{q,i}^
{2}) and Nq~,i∼𝒩⁡(0,σq~,i2)N_{\tilde{q},i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\tilde{q},i}^{2}), where we set
σq,i2=σi2/4\sigma_{q,i}^{2}=\sigma_{i}^{2}/4 and σq~,i2=4⋅σi2\sigma_{\tilde{q},i}^{2}=4\cdot\sigma_
{i}^{2}. Similarly to LSCALE-I experiments, we consider a target dimension of d=100d=100 and
generate 20 latent graphs, with ns=30000n_{\rm s}=30000 samples per environment for each graph.

Results.

Recall that for general transformations, we can guarantee Z^i=ϕi​(Zi)\hat{Z}_{i}=\phi_{i}(Z_{i})
for a diffeomorphism ϕi\phi_{i}. However, MCC(Zi,ϕ⁡(Zi))(Z_{i},\phi(Z_{i})) can largely deviate
from 1 for a nonlinear ϕi\phi_{i}. For instance, for Zi∼𝒩⁡(0,1)Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1) and ϕ⁡(Zi)
=Zi3+0.1​Zi\phi(Z_{i})=Z_{i}^{3}+0.1Z_{i}, we have MCC(Zi,ϕ⁡(Zi))≈0.786(Z_{i},\phi(Z_{i}))\approx
0.786. On the other hand, when we use the parameterization in (109), the only element-wise
diffeomorphism between 𝐙\mathbf{Z} and 𝐙^\hat{\mathbf{Z}} is an element-wise scaling. Therefore,
MCC remains a perfectly informative metric. Table 9 shows that we can almost perfectly recover the
latent variables (indicated by MCC of 11) latent DAG for n=5n=5 nodes by using perfect scores.
Furthermore, increasing the latent dimension to n=8n=8 has no significant impact on performance.


Table 9: GSCALE-I for a quadratic causal model with two coupled hard interventions per node. Noisy
scores are obtained using SSM-VR with nscore=30000n_{\rm score}=30000 samples.

                 expected             perfect scores                        noisy scores
                   num.
          nsn_{\ edges in                SHD⁡(𝒢,𝒢^){\rm SHD}(\                SHD⁡(𝒢,𝒢^){\rm SHD}(\
nn   dd   rm s}  𝒢\mathcal      MCC        mathcal{G},\hat{\         MCC        mathcal{G},\hat{\
                    {G}                       mathcal{G}})                         mathcal{G}})
55 100100 200200 55        1.00±0.001.00 0.00±0.000.00\pm 0.00  0.85±0.020.85 4.50±0.384.50\pm 0.38

88 100100 500500 1414      0.95±0.010.95 1.50±0.271.50\pm 0.27  0.75±0.020.75 12.9±0.4412.9\pm 0.44
                           \pm 0.01                             \pm 0.02

The effect of the quality of score estimation.

When using noisy scores, the performance of GSCALE-I degrades significantly. For instance, MCC goes
down to approximately 0.850.85 for n=5n=5 and 0.750.75 for n=8n=8. This degradation is similar to
what happens when using LSCALE-I on quadratic causal models in Table 8. It is noteworthy that the
transition from perfect to noisy scores is remarkably smoother when using LSCALE-I on linear causal
models (Table 3 and Table 7). This discrepancy is attributed to the distinct score estimation
procedures adopted in the two experimental settings. Specifically, linear Gaussian latent models
allow us to directly estimate the parameters of the closed-form score function 𝒔𝐗{\boldsymbol{s}}_
{\mathbf{X}}. However, when using a quadratic latent model, we rely on a nonparametric score
estimation via SSM-VR (Song et al., 2020). This comparison between two experiment settings and
results underscores that the performance gap between the theoretical guarantees and practical
results can be significantly mitigated through advances in general score estimation techniques. In
Section 7.6, we provide a further empirical evaluation of how the quality of the score estimation
affects the final performance.

7.2.2 Multi-layer nonlinearity

Next, we consider general MLPs by setting the number of layers to 3, which is in line with the MLP
depths considered in CRL literature (Liang et al., 2023). In this setting, we do not use any
knowledge of the transformation parametrization and aim to learn a generic NN-based encoder-decoder
pair h,h−1h,h^{-1} parameterized by θenc,θdec\theta_{\rm enc},\theta_{\rm dec}. In essence, this
enables us to test our algorithm’s performance on a standard procedure of function approximation
via neural networks.

Data generation.

In this setting, we focus on linear causal models. To generate 𝒢\mathcal{G}, we use the Erdős-Rényi
model with density 0.50.5 and n=5n=5 nodes. For observational causal mechanisms, we use Equation (
102) and its associated parameterization. For the two hard interventions on node ii, ZiZ_{i} is set
to Nq,i∼𝒩⁡(0,σq,i2)N_{q,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{q,i}^{2}) and Nq~,i∼𝒩⁡(0,σq~,i2)N_{\tilde{q},i}
\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\tilde{q},i}^{2}), where we set σq,i2=σi2/4\sigma_{q,i}^{2}=\sigma_{i}^
{2}/4 and σq~,i2=σi2⋅4\sigma_{\tilde{q},i}^{2}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\cdot 4. Due to the increased
computational complexity of training, we consider target dimension value d=5d=5, and we generate 5
latent graphs and ns=10000n_{\rm s}=10000 samples per graph.

Results.

GSCALE-I algorithm ensures latent variables recovery up to element-wise transformations. As a
proxy, we report its linear analogue, i.e., MCC. We summarize the results of these experiments in
Table 10.


Table 10: MCC across different runs in MLP transform + MLP causal model setting

          Runs             Mean (std. error)
0.64 0.54 0.43 0.58 0.54 0.54±0.030.54\pm 0.03

In this setting, we obtain a smaller average MCC rate (0.540.54) compared to the 1-layer
nonlinearity experiments in Table 9. Performance degradation can be attributed to two factors:
First, making the transformation more complex and making it more difficult to construct any kind of
autoencoder. In contrast to the parametric single-layer MLP setting in the previous section,
fitting a generic NN has considerably higher sample complexity. Second, our methodology requires
the estimation of score functions with relatively good accuracy. However, complex distributions can
push forward distributions into very complex and, similarly, difficult-to-estimate landscapes,
which can limit the accuracy of our algorithms.

7.3 Comparison with the Existing CRL Studies

In this section, we compare the performance of LSCALE-I with those of the approaches designed for
comparable settings in  (Squires et al., 2023; Zhang et al., 2023).

7.3.1 Comparison with linear causal models on synthetic data

A closely related study in the linear transformation setting is (Squires et al., 2023). As
discussed in Section 5, (Squires et al., 2023) presents identifiability guarantees and provides
algorithms for linear transformations with linear causal models. For a fair comparison, we perform
comparisons for linear Gaussian SEMs under the parameterization detailed in Section 7.1.
Experiments with synthetic linear causal models in (Squires et al., 2023) mostly focus on
recovering the parameters of the true encoder 𝐆†\mathbf{G}^{\dagger}. Hence, in Table 11, we report
performance comparisons in terms of the latent variable recovery metric ℓscale\ell_{\rm scale}.
Table 11 shows that LSCALE-I achieves close to perfect accuracy with as few as ns=5000n_{s}=5000
samples, whereas the algorithm of Squires et al. (2023) requires significantly more samples (more
than ns=50,000n_{s}=50,000) to achieve a reasonable performance.


Table 11: Comparison of LSCALE-I with the algorithm of Squires et al. (2023) for a linear causal
model with one hard intervention per node.

7.3.2 Comparison with DiscrepancyVAE on synthetic data

In this section, we compare LSCALE-I with the DiscrepancyVAE algorithm of (Zhang et al., 2023) on
the setting described in Section 7.1. We consider 10 runs of the quadratic causal model setting
with soft interventions, latent dimension n=5n=5, observed dimension d=100d=100, and 5000050000
samples per environment. We observe that the average SHD of DiscrepancyVAE on this dataset is
3.1±0.73.1\pm 0.7, which is comparable to LSCALE-I performance of 2.79±0.182.79\pm 0.18 reported in
Table 8.

To provide a visual comparison, we consider a single run and plot the ground truth latent graph (
Figure 3(a)), the estimate generated by LSCALE-I (Figure 3(b)), and the estimate generated by
DiscrepancyVAE (Figure 3(c)). The differences between the latter and the ground truth graph are
shown in these figures by red (added) and dashed (deleted) edges. We refer further comparison
between these methods to Section E.3.


(a) Ground truth


(b) LSCALE-I


(c) DiscrepancyVAE


Figure 3: Latent graph recovery comparison. Extra edges are marked in red, and dashed lines mark
the missing edges.

7.3.3 Comparison with DiscrepancyVAE on biological data

In this section, we apply our algorithms to the Perturb-seq dataset of Norman et al. (2019), which
is also used in Zhang et al. (2023). Following the same pre-processing steps, there are 8,907
unperturbed and 99,590 perturbed cells. Each cell (sample) is denoted by a sparse vector of
dimension 5000, which represents the expression levels of select genes. The perturbed cells are
generated by CRISPR activation (Gilbert et al., 2014) on one or two target genes out of 105 genes.
Hence, this is modeled by a 5000-dimensional observable variable 𝐗\mathbf{X} and a 105-dimensional
latent variable 𝐙\mathbf{Z} representing the perturbed genes. In this dataset, the ground truth
latent graph and variables are unknown, with the exception of limited gene interactions that have
been experimentally verified (Norman et al., 2019). For graph estimation, instead of working with a
latent dimension of 105, Zhang et al. (2023) first learns groups of perturbation targets subject to
a regularity constraint and then learns a latent graph among these groups. Since the methodology of
LSCALE-I does not naturally extend to learning such groups of latent nodes, we use the perturbation
groupings reported by Zhang et al. (2023) as our super-nodes and run LSCALE-I to learn a latent
graph over them. We list representative perturbation targets from each super-node in  Table 12.

The most important observation is that LSCALE-I and DiscrepancyVAE both estimate a causal edge from
DUSP9 to the MAPK1/ETS2 group, which is a gene activation relation that is demonstrated
experimentally (Norman et al., 2019). Due to the lack of ground truth for other graph edges,
comparing the algorithms for recovering other edges is not informative. Nevertheless, we provide
the estimated latent graph from LSCALE-I (Figure 4(a)) and DiscrepancyVAE (Figure 4(b)) for
completeness. This verifies that our score-based methodology can recover causal genomics relations.


Table 12: Representative target genes from super nodes.

Super node Representative
  index        gene(s)
    0      OSR2
    1      TBX2
    2      DUSP9
    3      MAPK1, ETS2
    4      COL2A1
    5      SET
    6      KLF1


(a) LSCALE-I


(b) DiscrepancyVAE
