red.

7.4 Intervention Extrapolation on Biological Data

In Section 6.4, we have demonstrated that it is possible to sample from unseen combinations of
given interventions via only learning the score functions of observed variables. We verify this on
the Perturb-seq data discussed in Section 7.3 through the following steps:

 1. 1.
   
    Collect observed variables’ data from observational distribution p𝐗p_{\mathbf{X}}, two
    single-node interventions p𝐗1p_{\mathbf{X}}^{1} and p𝐗2p_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}, and double-node
    intervention p𝐱{1,2}p_{\mathbf{x}}^{\{1,2\}}.

 2. 2.
   
    Compute score functions 𝒔𝐗{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}, 𝒔𝐗1{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1},
    and 𝒔𝐗2{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}.

 3. 3.
   
    Perform score function extrapolation for double-node intervention via (100): 𝒔~𝐗{1,2}=
    𝒔𝐗1+𝒔𝐗2−𝒔𝐗\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\{1,2\}}={\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}+{\
    boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}.

 4. 4.
   
    Single-node setting: Generate samples from 𝒔𝐗1{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1} and 𝒔𝐗2{\
    boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2} using Langevin dynamics (Welling and Teh, 2011), compare them
    to the original samples from p𝐗1p_{\mathbf{X}}^{1} and p𝐗2p_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}.

 5. 5.
   
    Double-node intervention extrapolation: Generate samples from 𝒔~𝐗{1,2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\
    mathbf{X}}^{\{1,2\}} using Langevin dynamics, compare them to the original samples from p𝐗{1,2}
    p_{\mathbf{X}}^{\{1,2\}}.

We note that Zhang et al. (2023) also report experiments on the same dataset for the single-node
data generation and double-node intervention extrapolation settings. However, as discussed in
Section 6.4, they perform these tasks using the VAE model for CRL, whereas our approach shows that
learning the latent causal representations is unnecessary. Similarly to Zhang et al. (2023), we use
the maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) (Gretton et al., 2012) to measure performance quantitatively. In
Table 13, we show that our approach performs at least as well as DiscrepancyVAE on the Perturb-seq
dataset. Finally, in Section E.4, we illustrate the simulated interventional distributions via UMAP
clustering, which visually supports the performance implied by Table 13. These results validate the
theoretical analysis that we do not need to learn latent variables to perform intervention
extrapolation.


Table 13: MMD evaluation for single-node and double-node intervention extrapolation of our
score-based approach and DiscrepancyVAE (Zhang et al., 2023) on Perturb-seq dataset.

                      Single-node               Double-node
 Score-based   0.057±0.0130.057\pm 0.013 0.208±0.0360.208\pm 0.036
DiscrepancyVAE ≥0.15\geq 0.15            ≥0.2\geq 0.2

7.5 Experiments on Image Data

In this section, we perform experiments on CRL where the general transformation is image rendering,
a highly nonlinear transformation, by applying the GSCALE-I algorithm to synthetic image data.

Image data generation.

For image-based experiments, we follow the setup of the closely related studies in (Ahuja et al.,
2023; Buchholz et al., 2023). Specifically, we consider images of the form in Figure 6, which are
generated as follows. The pairs of latent variables (Z2​i−1,Z2​i)(Z_{2i-1},Z_{2i}) describe the
coordinates of the ii-th ball’s center in a 64×64×364\times 64\times 3 RGB image. We use two and
three balls in our experiments, which corresponds to n∈{4,6}n\in\{4,6\} latent variables. We sample
the latent graph 𝒢\mathcal{G} from Erdős–Rényi model for nn nodes and set the expected number of
edges to 2​n2n. Given this graph 𝒢\mathcal{G}, we adopt a truncated linear Gaussian latent causal
model. The details of the linear model are the same as the Section 7.1, with the addition of a
second set of interventions represented by the causal mechanisms q~i\tilde{q}_{i}, by setting Zi=
N~iZ_{i}=\tilde{N}_{i}, where N~i∼𝒩⁡(0,σi24)\tilde{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}
{4}). Latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} are sampled from the distribution defined by this linear model,
but samples with any coordinate absolute value greater than 11 are discarded to truncate the
distribution in the box [−1,1]n[-1,1]^{n}. For each latent sample 𝐙\mathbf{Z}, we render an image
of the balls centered at the specified coordinates (shifted and scaled to fit entirely within
rendered image boundaries) with a radius of 8 pixels and use these as our observed data 𝐗\mathbf
{X}. Balls are color-coded, filled with three different colors, and the background is white. For
both two and three balls, we generate 5 graphs and 1000010000 samples from each graph under each
environment.

Refer to caption
Figure 5: Learning the latent variables from images. Autoencoder-1 is trained to minimize only the
reconstruction loss. Autoencoder-2 is trained to minimize the score-based loss in (83) while using
the reconstruction loss as regularization.

Candidate encoder and training.

For learning the latent variables, we construct a two-step autoencoder, depicted in Figure 5.
Specifically, in step one, we train an autoencoder on the observational image dataset with a
bottleneck dimension of 64 using only a reconstruction objective. In step two, we train another
autoencoder on the 64-dimensional output of the first encoder with the bottleneck dimension of nn
(the latent dimension) using both reconstruction loss and the score-based loss described earlier in
Section 7.2. Architecture details and training schedule are given in Table 17. Finally, we repeat
the training of the second autoencoder 5 times for each dataset.

Score difference estimation.

The main loss function for the second autoencoder of our setup is ‖𝐃t​(h)−𝐈n×n‖1,1\norm{\bD_{\rm t}
(h)-\bI_{n \times n}}_{1,1}, which is equivalent to the score-based loss in (83). For computing 𝐃t​
(h)\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h) for an encoder hh, we need to compute the score differences of image
datasets. To this end, we adopt a binary classifier-based log density ratio (LDR) estimator and use
the gradients of these learned LDRs as our score difference functions, as described earlier.
Specifically, we parameterize the LDR at any image through a CNN-based model, details of which are
given in Table 18. The output of this model is used to compute class probabilities, which are used
to minimize cross-entropy to train the model. We train one LDR model for each pair of hard
interventions on the same node and one for the observational-interventional environment pairs for
one set of hard interventions.

Refer to caption
Figure 6: Sample images (top row) versus their reconstructions (bottom row).

Results.

GSCALE-I ensures perfect graph recovery and latent variables recovery up to permutation and
element-wise scaling. Similarly to related work (Ahuja et al., 2023; Buchholz et al., 2023), we
report MCC between 𝐙^\hat{\mathbf{Z}} and 𝐙\mathbf{Z} as a metric of latent variables recovery,
shown in Table 14.


Table 14: MCC comparison in image experiments (over 5 runs).

Algorithm              SCM       # balls # int. / node int. type mean (std. error)
GSCALE-I               linear    2       2             hard      0.80±0.030.80\pm 0.03
GSCALE-I               linear    3       2             hard      0.76±0.080.76\pm 0.08
GSCALE-I               nonlinear 2       2             hard      0.93±0.020.93\pm 0.02
GSCALE-I               linear    2       1             hard      0.79±0.030.79\pm 0.03
GSCALE-I               nonlinear 2       1             hard      0.92±0.020.92\pm 0.02
Ahuja et al. (2023)    linear    2       1             do        0.13±0.030.13\pm 0.03
Ahuja et al. (2023)    linear    2       3             do        0.73±0.030.73\pm 0.03
Ahuja et al. (2023)    linear    2       5             do        0.83±0.030.83\pm 0.03
Buchholz et al. (2023) linear    2       1             hard      0.87±0.030.87\pm 0.03
Buchholz et al. (2023) linear    5       1             hard      0.94±0.010.94\pm 0.01

During training, we observed that MCC highly correlates with the reconstruction performance, i.e.,
when reconstruction errors in both autoencoders converge to small values, latent variable recovery
is also successful. We note that the reconstruction does not have to be perfect. For instance, the
samples in Figure 6 are from a run with 3 balls with an MCC of 0.98. Conversely, for the runs with
non-convergent reconstruction loss in either of the autoencoder training steps, latent variable
recovery also struggled. Hence, as long as we can ensure reasonable reconstruction performance when
we are minimizing the score-based loss, our algorithm manages to disentangle the true causal
variables embedded in the latent domain. We report the results from related work in Table 14 and
discuss them as follows. Overall, we achieve a better performance than that of Ahuja et al. (2023),
and slightly weaker than that of Buchholz et al. (2023) for linear SCMs. This can perhaps be
explained by the versatility of our approach, which works for general SCMs, whereas the algorithm
of Buchholz et al. (2023) is designed to work exclusively for linear SCMs, both theoretically and
due to its loss function design. We demonstrate in Table 14 that our algorithm performs even better
when the latent SCM is nonlinear (specifically, quadratic) than when it is linear, thereby
verifying its versatility. Finally, we also test our algorithm when using one intervention per
node, using the score differences between interventional and observational environments instead of
two interventional environments. While two interventions per node are required for theoretical
guarantees, our experiments show that using one intervention per node generally leads to
competitive performance, as illustrated in Table 14.

Gap between the theory and practice in CRL.

We highlight that the nature of the results presented in this paper is theoretical. We have
established unknown identifiability guarantees in several settings. Furthermore, we have designed
algorithms by defining a differentiable loss function whose global optima achieve identifiability
for general CRL and established that the algorithms generate provably correct representations. The
experiments presented on various synthesized and real datasets demonstrate the potential of the
score-based framework for complex nonlinear transforms, such as image rendering. We achieve this
goal by showing reasonable performance in latent variable recovery. That being said, the considered
image dataset and other synthetic datasets are still relatively simple compared to real-world
problems, and a gap remains between theoretical guarantees and practical applications. In other
words, the theory developed is only a necessary step for guaranteed practical performance. From our
perspective, closing this gap and obtaining sufficient conditions for practical performance
requires improvements in two aspects. First, using better score difference estimators (which we
empirically investigate in Section 7.6) would enable a more effective application of our
score-based CRL framework. Second, designing more suitable architectures for leveraging the
invariance properties of causal models can help scale up CRL applications.

7.6 Sensitivity to Score Estimation Noise

As discussed earlier, score estimators can be modularly incorporated into our algorithm. For cases
where p𝐗p_{\mathbf{X}} is not amenable to parameter estimation, we can use the noisy score
estimates generated by SSM-VR as achievable baselines, as presented in this section. In this
subsection, we evaluate the potential performance improvement that can be achieved as the score
estimates become more accurate. To this end, we test the LSCALE-I algorithm under varying levels of
score estimation noise. Specifically, we run the LSCALE-I algorithm using the scores generated by
the following model:

 𝒔^𝐗​(𝐱,σ2)=𝒔𝐗​(𝐱)⋅(1+Ξ),whereΞ∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2⋅𝐈d×d).\hat{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x};\
 sigma^{2})={\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\big(1+\Xi\big)\ ,\quad\mbox        (112)
 {where}\quad\Xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d\times d})\ .

We consider hard interventions on graph size of n=5n=5, set d=25d=25, and vary the value of σ2\
sigma^{2} within [10−4,10−2][10^{-4},10^{-2}]. We repeat the experiments 100 times with ns=10000n_
{\rm s}=10000 samples from each environment. We plot mean normalized 𝐙\mathbf{Z} error, defined as

 ℓnorm​(𝐙,𝐙^)≜‖𝐙−𝐙^‖2‖𝐙‖2.\ell_{\rm norm}(\mathbf{Z},\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\triangleq\frac{\|\     (113)
 mathbf{Z}-\hat{\mathbf{Z}}\|_{2}}{\norm{\bZ}_{2}}\ .

and mean SHD in Figure 7 with respect to the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). It is clear that when the
score estimation error is small, indicated by a high SNR value, the LSCALE-I algorithm demonstrates
a strong performance in recovering both latent causal variables and the latent graph. We also note
that the baseline results with noisy scores computed via SSM-VR in Table 5 yield similar success at
graph recovery at the SNR of approximately 2525 dB and latent recovery at the SRM of approximately
88 dB. The curves also confirm our observations in Section 7.1 and Section 7.2 that graph recovery
is more sensitive to score estimation errors. The trend of the curves in Figure 7 indicates that
our algorithm would greatly benefit from a better score estimator.


(a) Normalized ℓ2\ell_{2} loss versus SNR


(b) SHD versus SNR


Figure 7: The performance of LSCALE-I under noisy scores with varying SNR for n=5n=5 and d=25d=25.
The dashed vertical lines correspond to SNR values that correspond to the performance attained by
SSM-VR.

8 Discussion and Concluding Remarks

In this paper, we have proposed a score function-based CRL framework that uses stochastic
interventions to learn latent causal representations and the latent causal graph underlying them.
In this framework, by uncovering novel connections between score functions and CRL, we have
established identifiability results for linear and general transformations without restricting the
latent causal models, and designed LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I algorithms that achieve these
identifiability guarantees. There are several exciting directions for future work.

As discussed in Section 3.4 after defining the identifiability objectives, and emphasized when
presenting the corresponding results through the paper, our results are tight given a complete set
of atomic interventions. A natural direction for future work is relaxing the atomic intervention
requirement. In this aspect, the paper (Varıcı et al., 2024) extends the score-based framework for
linear transformations into the setting of unknown multi-node interventions by using combinations
of multi-node score differences. Given a sufficiently diverse multi-node intervention set, similar
identifiability results to those in Section 5 are shown. That being said, establishing the
necessary conditions for multi-node interventions and the case of general transformations remains
an open problem. Next, we note that our LSCALE-I algorithm for linear transformations is agnostic
to the intervention type and latent causal model, meaning that it can be used for both soft and
hard interventions and different causal models. Designing similar universal CRL algorithms that can
handle general transformations under different sizes and types of interventional environments is
the ultimate goal for studying the identifiability of CRL from interventions. A missing component
of existing CRL literature is the finite-sample analysis. Probabilistic identifiability results for
a given number of interventional data samples can be useful, especially in applications where
performing interventions is costly. In this direction, the recent paper (Acartürk et al., 2024)
establishes finite sample guarantees for CRL under linear transformations. Extending such analysis
to general transformations can provide insights for bridging the gap between theory and practice in
CRL.

This work was supported in part by IBM through the IBM-Rensselaer Future of Computing Research and
the National Science Foundation under Award ECCS-193310.

Appendix A Proofs of Score Function Properties and Transformations

We start by providing the following facts that will be used repeatedly in the proofs.

Proposition 0.

Consider two continuous functions f,g:ℝn→ℝf,g:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}} with full support.
Then, for any α>0\alpha>0,

 ∃𝐳∈ℝnf(𝐳)≠g(𝐳)⇔𝔼[|f(𝐙)−g(𝐙)|α]≠0.\exists\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;\;f(\mathbf{z})\neq
 g(\mathbf{z})\quad\iff\quad{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\big|^{\alpha}\  (114)
 Big]\neq 0\ .

Specifically, for α=1\alpha=1, we have

 ∃𝐳∈ℝnf(𝐳)≠g(𝐳)⇔𝔼[|f(𝐙)−g(𝐙)|]≠0.\exists\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;\;f(\mathbf{z})\neq g
 (\mathbf{z})\quad\iff\quad{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\big|\Big]\neq 0  (115)
 \ .

Proof: If there exists 𝐳∈ℝn\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n} such that f⁡(𝐳)≠g⁡(𝐳)f(\mathbf{z})\neq g(\
mathbf{z}), then f⁡(𝐳)−g⁡(𝐳)f(\mathbf{z})-g(\mathbf{z}) is nonzero over a nonzero-measure set due
to continuity. Then, 𝔼⁡[|f⁡(𝐙)−g⁡(𝐙)|α]≠0{\mathbb{E}}\big[|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})|^{\alpha}\
big]\neq 0 since pp (pdf of 𝐙\mathbf{Z}) has full support. On the other direction, if f⁡(𝐳)=g⁡(𝐳)f
(\mathbf{z})=g(\mathbf{z}) for all 𝐳∈ℝn\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}, then 𝔼⁡[|f⁡(𝐙)−g⁡(𝐙)|α]=0{\
mathbb{E}}\big[|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})|^{\alpha}\big]=0. This means that 𝔼⁡[|f⁡(𝐙)−g⁡(𝐙)|α]≠0
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})|^{\alpha}\big]\neq 0 implies that there exists 𝐳∈ℝn\
mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n} such that f⁡(𝐳)≠g⁡(𝐳)f(\mathbf{z})\neq g(\mathbf{z}).

A.1 Proof of Lemma 7

Our score-based methodology builds on the changes in score functions under interventions. For
proving Lemma 7, we start by showing that 𝔼⁡[|𝒔⁡(𝐙)−𝒔m​(𝐙)|i]≠0⟹i∈pa¯​(Im){\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\
boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\implies i\in\
overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m}), which holds true regardless of the causal model and the intervention
type.

Proof of 𝔼⁡[|𝒔⁡(𝐙)−𝒔m​(𝐙)|i]≠0⟹i∈pa¯​(Im){\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\

For the reverse direction, we will use the following intermediate result which formalizes the
weakest possible requirement for a meaningful intervention and shows that it is a property of (i)
hard interventions under any causal model, and (ii) additive noise model under either soft or hard
interventions.

Lemma 0 (Interventional Regularity).

Causal mechanisms pip_{i} and qiq_{i} of node ii are said to satisfy interventional regularity if

 ∃𝐳∈ℝn​such that∂∂zk​qi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))≠0,∀k∈pa⁡(i).\displaystyle\exists\mathbf
 {z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;\;\mbox{such that}\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\frac{q_{i}    (119)
 (z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\neq 0\ ,\
 qquad\forall k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)\ .

Then, pip_{i} and qiq_{i} satisfy interventional regularity if at least one of the following
conditions is true:

 1. 1.
   
    The intervention is hard, i.e., qi​(zi|𝐳pa⁡(i))=qi​(zi)q_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}
    (i)})=q_{i}(z_{i}).

 2. 2.
   
    The causal model is an additive noise model in which the pdfs of the noise variables are
    analytic.

Proof: See Section A.3.

Case (i) and Case (ii).

𝔼⁡[|𝒔⁡(𝐙)−𝒔m​(𝐙)|i]≠0⟹i∈pa¯​(Im){\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}
^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\implies i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m}) is already shown
above. For the reverse direction, we give the proof for soft interventions on additive noise
models, Case (ii). We will use interventional regularity since Lemma 33 shows that it is satisfied
for additive noise models. The proof for hard interventions, Case (i), follows from similar
arguments since interventional regularity is also satisfied for hard interventions by Lemma 33.

    Then, taking the derivative of the right-hand side of (133) with respect to zℓz_{\ell}, we
    obtain

     0=∂2log⁡pℓ​(zℓ|𝐳pa⁡(ℓ))∂zi​∂zℓ,\displaystyle 0=\dfrac{\partial^{2}\log p_{\ell}(z_{\     (136)
     ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{\ell}}\ ,

    which is again a realization of (129) for k=ik=i, which we proved to be impossible.

Hence, we showed that [𝒔m​(𝐳)−𝒔~m​(𝐳)]i\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}
^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i} cannot be zero for all 𝐳\mathbf{z} values. Then, by Proposition 32 we
have 𝔼⁡[|𝒔m​(𝐙)−𝒔~m​(𝐙)|i]≠0{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol
{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})|_{i}\big]\neq 0, and the proof is concluded.

A.2 Proof of Lemma 8

Let us recall the setting. Consider random vectors 𝐘1,𝐘2∈ℝr\mathbf{Y}_{1},\mathbf{Y}_{2}\in{\mathbb
{R}}^{r} and 𝐖1\mathbf{W}_{1}, 𝐖2∈ℝs\mathbf{W}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s} that are related through 𝐘1=

an injective and continuously differentiable function.

Proof of Corollary 9

For a given linear transform 𝐅\mathbf{F}, we have Jf​(𝐰)=𝐅J_{f}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{F}, which is
independent of 𝐰\mathbf{w}. Then, in the proof of Lemma 8, (143) reduces to 𝒔𝐖​(𝐰)=𝐅⊤⋅𝒔𝐘​(𝐲){\
boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{F}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf
{y}). Finally, we note that the score difference of 𝐘\mathbf{Y} can be similarly written in terms
of the score difference of 𝐖\mathbf{W}.

                                 \mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\in\Omega\times\Phi\ .

Note that the RHS of (166) is not a function of nin_{i}. Then, taking the derivative of both sides
with respect to nin_{i}, we get

 rp′​(ni)rp​(ni)=rq′​(ni+δ⁡(𝐳pa⁡(i)))rq​(ni+δ⁡(𝐳pa⁡(i))).\frac{r_{p}^{\prime}(n_{i})}{r_{p}
 (n_{i})}=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}{r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta   (167)
 (\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}\ .

In the next step, we show that δ\delta is not a constant function. We prove this by contradiction.
Suppose that δ⁡(𝐳pa⁡(i))=δ∗\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=\delta^{*} is a constant function.
Then, the gradients of fif_{i} and f¯i\bar{f}_{i} are equal. From (166), this implies that

 rp​(ni)=rq​(ni+δ∗),∀ni∈Ω.r_{p}(n_{i})=r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})\ ,\quad\forall n_{i}\in\Omega  (168)
 \ .

Since rp​(ni)r_{p}(n_{i}) and rq​(ni+δ∗)r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*}) are analytic functions that agree
on an open interval of ℝ{\mathbb{R}}, they are equal for all ni∈ℝn_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}. This implies
that pN​(ni)=η⋅qN​(ni+δ∗)p_{N}(n_{i})=\eta\cdot q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*}) for some constant η∈ℝ\eta\
in{\mathbb{R}}. Since pNp_{N} and qNq_{N} are pdfs, η=1\eta=1 is the only choice that maintains
pNp_{N} and qNq_{N} are pdfs. Therefore, pN​(ni)=qN​(ni+δ∗)p_{N}(n_{i})=q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*}).
However, using (159), this implies that pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))=qi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_
{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}), which contradicts the premise that an
intervention changes the causal mechanism of target node ii. Therefore, δ\delta is a continuous,
non-constant function, and its image over 𝐳pa⁡(i)∈Φ\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in\Phi includes an
open interval Θ⊆ℝ\Theta\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}. With this result in mind, we return to (167).
Consider a fixed realization ni=ni∗n_{i}=n_{i}^{*} and denote the value of the left-hand side (LHS)
for ni∗n_{i}^{*} by α\alpha. By defining u≜δ⁡(𝐳pa⁡(i))u\triangleq\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}),
we get

 α\            =rq′​(ni∗+u)rq​(ni∗+u),∀u∈Θ.\displaystyle=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}
 displaystyle\ {r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}\ ,\qquad\forall u\in\Theta\ .                             (169)
 alpha

This is only possible if rqr_{q} is an exponential function, i.e., rq​(u)=k1​exp⁡(α​u)r_{q}(u)=k_
{1}\exp(\alpha u) over interval u∈Θu\in\Theta. Since rqr_{q} is an analytic function, it is,
therefore, exponential over entire ℝ{\mathbb{R}}. Then, the associated pdf must have the form qN​
(u)=k2​exp⁡((k1/α)​exp⁡(α​u))q_{N}(u)=k_{2}\exp((k_1/\alpha) \exp(\alpha u)). However, the integral
of this function over the entire domain ℝ{\mathbb{R}} diverges. Hence, it is not a valid pdf,
rendering a contradiction. Hence, the additive noise model satisfies interventional regularity.

Appendix B Proofs of the Results for Linear Transformations

B.1 Auxiliary Results

First, we provide a linear algebraic property, which will be used in the proofs.

Lemma 0.

Consider the latent causal graph 𝒢\mathcal{G}, and a matrix 𝐋∈ℝn×n\mathbf{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\
times n}.

 1. 1.
   
    Let 𝐋pa\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}} and 𝐋an\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}} be binary matrices that denote the
    parental and ancestral relationships in 𝒢\mathcal{G}, respectively, i.e.,

     [𝐋pa]i,j≜{1,ifj∈pa¯​(i)0,otherwise,and[𝐋an]i,j≜{1,ifj∈an¯​(i)0,otherwise.\displaystyle
     [\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}]_{i,j}\triangleq\begin{cases}1\ ,&\textnormal{if}\quad j\in\
     overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\\ 0\ ,&\textnormal{otherwise}\end{cases}\ ,\;\;\mbox{and}\quad[\  (170)
     mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}]_{i,j}\triangleq\begin{cases}1\ ,&\textnormal{if}\quad j\in\
     overline{{\rm{an}}}(i)\\ 0\ ,&\textnormal{otherwise}\end{cases}\ .

    Then, if 𝐈n×n≼𝟙​{𝐋}≼𝐋pa\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{L}\}\preccurlyeq\
    mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}, we also have 𝐈n×n≼𝟙​{𝐋−1}≼𝐋an\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds
    {1}\{\mathbf{L}^{-1}\}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}.

 2. 2.
   
    Let 𝐋sur\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}} be a binary matrix that denotes the surrounding relationships
    in 𝒢\mathcal{G}, i.e.,

     [𝐋sur]i,j\            ≜{1,ifch¯​(i)⊆ch¯​(j),0,otherwise.\displaystyle\triangleq\begin
     displaystyle[\mathbf  {cases}1\ ,&\textnormal{if}\quad\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(i)\subseteq\  (171)
     {L}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_    overline{{\rm{ch}}}(j)\ ,\\ 0\ ,&\textnormal{otherwise}\end
     {i,j}                 {cases}\ .

    Then, if 𝐈n×n≼𝟙​{𝐋}≼𝐋sur\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{L}\}\preccurlyeq\
    mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}, we also have 𝐈n×n≼𝟙​{𝐋−1}≼𝐋sur\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds
    {1}\{\mathbf{L}^{-1}\}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}.

matrix 𝐋\mathbf{L} such that 𝐈n×n≼𝟙​{𝐋}≼𝐋pa\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{L}
\}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}. Matrix 𝐋\mathbf{L} can be decomposed as

 𝐋=𝐄⋅(𝐈n×n+𝚲),\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{E}\cdot(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})\ ,  (172)

in which 𝐄\mathbf{E} is a diagonal matrix and 𝚲\mathbf{\Lambda} is a strictly lower triangular

Subsequently, since 𝐄\mathbf{E} is a diagonal matrix, we have

 𝐋−1=(𝐈n×n+𝚲)−1⋅𝐄−1,and𝟙​{𝐋−1}=𝟙​{(𝐈n×n+𝚲)−1}.\mathbf{L}^{-1}=(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\
 mathbf{\Lambda})^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{E}^{-1}\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{L}^{-1}  (173)
 \}=\mathds{1}\big\{(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})^{-1}\big\}\ .

Note that 𝚲\mathbf{\Lambda} is a strictly lower triangular n×nn\times n matrix, which implies that
𝚲n\mathbf{\Lambda}^{n} is a zero matrix. Therefore, the inverse of (𝐈n×n+𝚲)(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+

 {n-1}\ .

\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}.

B.2 Proof of Theorem 16

{1},\dots,I^{n}), 𝐋\mathbf{L} has nonzero diagonal entries and satisfies 𝐋i,j=0\mathbf{L}_{i,j}=0

B.3 Proof of Theorem 18

Latent graph estimation.

We continue from the intuitions provided in Section 5.4 and prove the perfect recovery of the
latent graph as follows. First, recall that output 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} of Algorithm 1 for soft
interventions satisfies that transitive closures of 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} and 𝒢\mathcal{G} are the
same under permutation (I1,…,In)(I^{1},\dots,I^{n}). Since (1,…,n)(1,\dots,n) is assumed to be a
topological order of 𝒢\mathcal{G}, ρ≜(Iπ1,…,Iπn)\rho\triangleq(I^{\pi_{1}},\dots,I^{\pi_{n}}) is
also a topological order of 𝒢\mathcal{G}. Then, for any k=πuk=\pi_{u} and t=πv∈{π1,…,πu−1}t=\pi_{v}
\in\{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{u-1}\}, (64) becomes

 dim(col(𝐑𝐗t)∩col(𝐑𝐗k))=|pa(ρu)∩pa(ρv)|+𝟙{ρv∈pa(ρu)}.\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf
 {X}}^{t})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{k})\big)=|{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\cap{\rm{pa}}   (194)
 (\rho_{v})|+\mathds{1}\big\{\rho_{v}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\big\}\ .

By induction, we will prove that the parent sets generated by Algorithm 2 satisfy

 πu∈pa^​(πv)⇔ρu∈pa⁡(ρv).\pi_{u}\in\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{v})\;\iff\;\rho_{u}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_    (195)
 {v})\ .

At the base case, consider k=π2k=\pi_{2}. The only possible parent is t=π1t=\pi_{1}. Since ρ1\rho_
{1} is a root node in 𝒢\mathcal{G}, (194) implies

 dim(col(𝐑𝐗π1)∩col(𝐑𝐗π2))=𝟙{ρ1∈pa(ρ2)}.\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{1}})
 \cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{2}})\big)=\mathds{1}\{\rho_{1}\in{\rm{pa}}(\     (196)
 rho_{2})\}\ .

Since pa^​(π1)=pa^​(π2)=∅\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{1})=\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{2})=\emptyset at this step, the
algorithm adds π1\pi_{1} to pa^​(π2)\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{2}) if and only if ρ1∈pa⁡(ρ2)\rho_{1}\in{\rm
{pa}}(\rho_{2}), and (195) is satisfied for k=π2k=\pi_{2}. Next, as the induction step, assume that
the algorithm output pa^​(k)\hat{\rm{pa}}(k) satisfies (195) for k∈{π1,…,πu−1}k\in\{\pi_{1},\dots,\
pi_{u-1}\}. We prove that k=πuk=\pi_{u} also satisfies the condition by induction again. At the
base case, consider t=π1t=\pi_{1}. Similar to the previous base case, (194) implies

 dim(col(𝐑𝐗π1)∩col(𝐑𝐗πu))=𝟙{ρ1∈pa(ρu)},\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{1}})
 \cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{u}})\big)=\mathds{1}\{\rho_{1}\in{\rm{pa}}(\     (197)
 rho_{u})\}\ ,

and since pa^​(πu)\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{u}) does not contain π1\pi_{1} yet, the algorithm adds π1\pi_
{1} to pa^​(πu)\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{u}) if and only if ρ1∈pa⁡(ρu)\rho_{1}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u}).
Next, as the induction step, assume that for πj∈{π1,…,πv−1}\pi_{j}\in\{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{v-1}\}
where v<uv<u, the algorithm correctly identified the parents, i.e., πj∈pa^​(πu)\pi_{j}\in\hat{\rm
{pa}}(\pi_{u}) if and only if ρj∈pa⁡(ρu)\rho_{j}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u}). Consider k=πuk=\pi_{u} and
t=πvt=\pi_{v}, for which (194) implies

 dim(col(𝐑𝐗πv)∩col(𝐑𝐗πu))=|pa(ρu)∩pa(ρv)|+𝟙{ρv∈pa(ρu)}.\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\
 mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{v}})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{u}})\big)=|{\rm{pa}}(\rho_  (198)
 {u})\cap{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{v})|+\mathds{1}\{\rho_{v}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\}\ .

By the induction hypothesis, we know that |pa^​(πu)∩pa^​(πv)|=|pa⁡(ρu)∩pa⁡(ρv)||\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_
{u})\cap\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{v})|=|{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\cap{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{v})|. Therefore, the
algorithm adds πv\pi_{v} to pa^​(πv)\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{v}) if and only if ρv∈pa⁡(ρu)\rho_{v}\in{\rm
{pa}}(\rho_{u}). Therefore, by the inner induction, pa^​(k)\hat{\rm{pa}}(k) satisfies (195). Then,
by the outer induction, (195) is satisfied for all parent set estimates, which concludes the proof
of perfect graph recovery.  □\square

Markov property of consistency up to mixing with surrounding parents.

Appendix C Proofs of the Results for General Transformations

The proof of Theorem 26 is already given in Section 6.2. In this section, we first prove Theorem 27
, that is, identifiability for coupled interventions without observational environment. Then, we
prove the results in Section 6.3 related to the uncoupled interventions.

    {i\}}\{j\}=\emptyset since there are at least two root nodes excluding ii. Then, ℬi=pa¯​(i)\
    mathcal{B}_{i}=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i) for all i∈[n]i\in[n] and we are done.

 2. 2.
   
    |ℬ|=2|\mathcal{B}|=2: The two nodes in ℬ\mathcal{B} are root nodes. If there were at least
    three root nodes, we would have at least three nodes in ℬ\mathcal{B}. Hence, the two nodes in ℬ
    \mathcal{B} are the only root nodes. Subsequently, every i∉ℬi\notin\mathcal{B} is also not in 𝒦
    \mathcal{K} and we have

     pa¯​(i)⊆ℬi⊆⋂j∈𝒦pa¯​(i,j)=pa¯​(i)∪{⋂j∈𝒦{j}}=pa¯​(i).\displaystyle\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)
     \subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i}\subseteq\bigcap_{j\in\mathcal{K}}\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)=\    (214)

     {pa}}}(i)\ .

    Hence, ℬi=pa¯​(i)\mathcal{B}_{i}=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i) for every non-root node ii and we
    already have the two root nodes in ℬ\mathcal{B}, which completes the graph recovery.

 3. 3.

    pairs, we identify all root nodes as follows. Note that a hard intervention on node ii makes
    ZiZ_{i} independent of all of its non-descendants, and all conditional independence relations
    are preserved under componentwise diffeomorphisms such as ff. Then, using the
    adjacency-faithfulness assumption, we infer that

        then j→ij\rightarrow i and jj is a root node.

    This implies that we can determine whether ii and jj nodes are root nodes by using at most two
    independence tests. Hence, we identify all root nodes by using at most nn independence tests.
    We also know that there are at most two root nodes. If we have two root nodes, then ℬi=pa¯​(i)\
    mathcal{B}_{i}=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i) for all non-root nodes, and the graph is recovered. If
    we have only one root node ii, then for any j≠ij\neq i we have

    overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j) due to adjacency-faithfulness. Otherwise, we conclude that i∈pa¯​(j)i\in
    \overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j). Hence, an additional (n−1)(n-1) conditional independence tests ensure
    the recovery of all pa¯​(j)\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j) sets, and the graph recovery is complete.

C.3 Proof of Lemma 29

We will prove the desired result by contradiction. Suppose that (h∗,π)(h^{*},\pi) is a solution to

Appendix D Analysis of the Assumptions

D.1 Analysis of Assumption 1

In this subsection, we prove Lemma 19 statement, i.e., Assumption 1 is satisfied for additive noise
models under hard interventions, as follows. Consider a hard interventional environment ℰm\mathcal
{E}^{m} and let Im=iI^{m}=i be the intervened node. Recall (29), which becomes

 [𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]k=[𝒔⁡(𝐳)−𝒔m​(𝐳)]k=∂∂zk​log⁡pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i)),[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\
 mathbf{z})]_{k}=\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{k}  (244)
 =\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ ,

for the hard intervention on node ii and parent node k∈pa⁡(i)k\in{\rm{pa}}(i). Then, the ratio in
Assumption 1 is given by

 [∇𝐳logp(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))]k[∇𝐳logp(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))]i−[∇𝐳logq(zi)]i=[𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]k[𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]i.\frac{[\
 nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})]_{k}}{[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log

 {[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}}{[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\
 mathbf{z})]_{i}}\ .

We will prove the desired result by contradiction. Assume that there exists a nonzero constant c∈ℝc
\in{\mathbb{R}} such that [𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]k=c⋅[𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]i[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_
{k}=c\cdot[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i} for all 𝐳∈ℝn\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}
^{n}.

Additive noise model.

The additive noise model for node ii is given by Zi=fi​(𝐙pa⁡(i))+NiZ_{i}=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm
{pa}}(i)})+N_{i} as specified in (7). When node ii is hard intervened, ZiZ_{i} is generated

the interventional model. Then, denoting the pdfs of NiN_{i} and N¯i\bar{N}_{i} by pNp_{N} and qNq_

 pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))=pN​(zi−fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))),andqi​(zi)=qN​(zi).\displaystyle p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\

 quad q_{i}(z_{i})=q_{N}(z_{i})\ .

 [𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]k[𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]i=−∂fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))∂zk⋅rp(ni)rp​(ni)−rq​(ni+fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))).\displaystyle\
 frac{[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}}{[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^    (248)

 {r_{p}(n_{i})-r_{q}(n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}\ .

 c⋅∂fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))∂zk+1=rq​(ni+fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i)))rp​(ni).c\cdot\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\
 rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}+1=\frac{r_{q}(n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}{r_{p}   (249)
 (n_{i})}\ .

We scrutinize (249) in two cases.

Case 1: ∂fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))∂zk\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}} is

to be constant, denoted by δ∗\delta^{*}, for all 𝐳pa⁡(i)∈ℝ|pa⁡(i)|\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in{\

=r_{p}(n_{i}) for all ni∈ℝn_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}. This implies that pN​(ni)=η∗​qN​(ni+δ∗)p_{N}(n_{i})
=\eta^{*}q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*}) for some constant η∗\eta^{*}. Since pNp_{N} and qNq_{N} are pdfs,
the only choice is η∗=1\eta^{*}=1 and pN​(ni)=qN​(ni+δ∗)p_{N}(n_{i})=q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*}), then
pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))=qi​(zi)p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=q_{i}(z_{i}), which contradicts
the premise that an intervention changes the causal mechanism of the target node ii.

Case 2: ∂fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))∂zk\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}} is not
constant.

In this case, note that LHS of (249) is not a function of nin_{i}. Then, taking the derivative of
both sides with respect to nin_{i} and rearranging, we obtain

 rp′​(ni)rp​(ni)=rq′​(ni+fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i)))rq​(ni+fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))),∀(ni,𝐳pa⁡(i))∈ℝ×ℝ|pa⁡(i)|.\frac
 {r_{p}^{\prime}(n_{i})}{r_{p}(n_{i})}=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm      (250)
 {pa}}(i)}))}{r_{q}(n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}\ ,\quad\forall(n_{i},\mathbf{z}
 _{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ .

Next, consider a fixed realization ni=ni∗n_{i}=n_{i}^{*}, and denote the value of LHS by α\alpha.
Since fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}) is continuous and not constant, its image
contains an open interval Θ⊆ℝ\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}. Denoting u≜fi​(𝐳pa⁡(i))u\triangleq f_{i}(\
mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}), we have

 α=rq′​(ni∗+u)rq​(ni∗+u),∀u∈Θ.\alpha=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}{r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}\  (251)
 ,\quad\forall u\in\Theta\ .

The only solution to this equality is that rq​(ni∗+u)r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u) is an exponential function,
rq​(u)=k1​exp⁡(α​u)r_{q}(u)=k_{1}\exp(\alpha u) over interval u∈Θu\in\Theta. Since rqr_{q} is an
analytic function that equals to an exponential function over an interval, it is exponential over
entire ℝ\mathbb{R}. This implies that the pdf qN​(u)q_{N}(u) is of the form qN​(u)=k2​exp⁡((k1/α)
​exp⁡(α​u))q_{N}(u)=k_{2}\exp((k_1/\alpha) \exp(\alpha u)). However, this cannot be a valid pdf
since its integral over ℝ{\mathbb{R}} diverges. Then, [𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]k=c⋅[𝒅𝐙m​(𝐳)]i[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\
mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}=c\cdot[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i} cannot be
true, which concludes the proof.

D.2 Analysis of Assumption 2

In this section, we establish the necessary and sufficient conditions under which Assumption 2
holds for additive models. Furthermore, we show that a large class of nonlinear models in the
latent space satisfy these conditions, including the two-layer neural networks. We have focused on
such NNs since they effectively approximate continuous functions (Cybenko, 1989). Readily, the
necessary and sufficient conditions can be investigated for other choices of nonlinear functions.

D.2.1 Interpreting Assumption 2

The implication of Assumption 2 is that the effect of an intervention is not lost in any linear
combination of the varying coordinates of the scores. To formalize this, for each node i∈[n]i\in
[n], we define

 𝒞i≜{𝐜∈ℝn:∃j∈pa¯​(i)​such that ​cj≠0},\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i}\triangleq\{\mathbf{c}\in
 {\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;:\;\exists j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\;\;\mbox{such that }\;c_{j}\neq 0   (252)
 \}\ ,

Let Im=iI^{m}=i. Note that 𝐑𝐙m\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m} is positive semi-definite and [𝐑𝐙m]j,k=0
[\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}]_{j,k}=0 for all (j,k)∉pa¯​(i)×pa¯​(i)(j,k)\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}
(i)\times\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i). Therefore, for any 𝐜∈𝒞i\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i},

 𝐜⊤⋅𝐑𝐙m⋅𝐜=𝐄⁡[(𝐜⊤⋅𝒅𝐙m)⋅(𝐜⊤⋅𝒅𝐙m)⊤]≠0,\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}\cdot\
 mathbf{c}=\mathbf{E}\big[(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m})\cdot(\    (253)
 mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m})^{\top}\big]\neq 0\ ,

since 𝐑𝐙m\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m} has rank |pa¯​(i)||\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)|. The reverse
direction also holds true, i.e., if 𝐑𝐙m\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m} has rank less than |pa¯​(i)||\
overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)|, then there exists 𝐜​𝒞i\mathbf{c}\mathcal{C}_{i} which makes 𝐜⊤⋅𝐑𝐙m⋅𝐜=0\
mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}\cdot\mathbf{c}=0. Therefore, Assumption 2 is
equivalent to the following statement:

 𝐄⁡[|𝐜⊤⋅𝒅𝐙m|]≠0,∀𝐜∈𝒞i.\mathbf{E}\big[|\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^    (254)
 {m}|\big]\neq 0\ ,\quad\forall\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\ .

Necessary and sufficient conditions.

Consider the additive noise model for node ii

 Zi\displaystyle Z_   =fi​(𝐙pa⁡(i))+Ni,\displaystyle=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+N_{i}\  (255)

as specified in (7). When node ii is soft intervened, ZiZ_{i} is generated according to

 {i}                \bar{N}_{i}\ ,

in which f¯i\bar{f}_{i} and N¯i\bar{N}_{i} specify the interventional mechanism for node ii. The
following lemma characterizes the necessary and sufficient conditions under which (254)
(equivalently, Assumption 2) is satisfied. In this subsection, we use φ\varphi as the shorthand for
𝐳pa⁡(i)\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}.

Lemma 0.

For each node i∈[n]i\in[n] consider the following two set of equations for c∈ℝnc\in{\mathbb{R}}^

following observations.

 1. 1.
   
    If fif_{i} and f¯i\bar{f}_{i} are linear functions, the vector spaces generated by uu and u¯\
    bar{u} have dimensions 1. Subsequently, we always have rank⁡(𝐕)≤2\rank(\bV)\leq 2, rendering an
    underdetermined system when |pa⁡(i)|≥2|{\rm{pa}}(i)|\geq 2. Hence, when the maximum degree of 𝒢
    \mathcal{G} is at least 2, a linear causal model does not satisfy Assumption 2.

 2. 2.
   
    If fif_{i} and f¯i\bar{f}_{i} are quadratic with full-rank matrices, i.e., fi​(φ)=φ⊤​𝐀​φf_{i}(\

    \{\varphi^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}:t\in[w]\} for which rank⁡(𝐕)=|pa⁡(i)|+1\rank(\bV)
    =|{\rm{pa}}(i)|+1 and the system in (260) admits only the trivial solutions ai=0a_{i}=0 and 𝐛=𝟎
    \mathbf{b}=\boldsymbol{0}. Hence, quadratic causal models satisfy Assumption 2.

 3. 3.
   
    If fif_{i} and f¯i\bar{f}_{i} are two-layer NNs with a sufficiently large number of hidden
    neurons, they also render a fully determined system, and as a result, they satisfy Assumption 2
    .

Sufficient condition.

We show that if (D.2.2) admit solutions 𝐜\mathbf{c} only in ℝn∖𝒞i{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\mathcal

We show that 𝐜∗∈𝒞i\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i} is also a solution to (D.2.2), contradicting the
premise. In order to show that 𝐜∗∈𝒞i\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i} is also a solution to (D.2.2),
suppose, by contradiction, that (280) holds, and there exists φ∗∈ℝ|pa⁡(i)|\varphi^{*}\in{\mathbb
{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|} corresponding to which

 (𝐜∗)⊤⋅∇𝐳f¯i​(φ∗)−ci∗≠0.\displaystyle(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_  (281)
 {i}(\varphi^{*})-c^{*}_{i}\neq 0\ .

Note that fif_{i} is a continuously differentiable function and also a function of zjz_{j} for all
j∈pa⁡(i)j\in{\rm{pa}}(i). Hence, there exists an open set Φ⊆ℝ|pa⁡(i)|\Phi\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{|{\rm
{pa}}(i)|} for φ\varphi for which (𝐜∗)⊤⋅∇𝐳fi​(φ∗)−ci∗(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf
{z}}f_{i}(\varphi^{*})-c^{*}_{i} is nonzero everywhere in Φ\Phi. Likewise, rpr_{p} cannot
constantly be zero over all possible intervals Ω\Omega. This is because otherwise, it would have to
necessarily be a constant zero function (since it is analytic), which is an invalid score function.
Hence, there exists an open interval Ω⊆ℝ\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R} over which rp​(ni)r_{p}(n_{i}) is
nonzero for all ni∈Ωn_{i}\in\Omega. Subsequently, the left-hand side of (280) is nonzero over the
Cartesian product (ni,φ)∈Ω×Φ(n_{i},\varphi)\in\Omega\times\Phi. This means that if (280) is true,
then both functions on its right-hand side must also be nonzero over Ω×Φ\Omega\times\Phi. Hence, by
rearranging the terms in (280) we have

 {r_{q}\big(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi)\big)}{r_{p}(n_{i})}=\frac{c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\     (282)
 top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)}{c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\
 mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)}\ ,\qquad\forall(n_{i},\varphi)\in\Omega\times\Phi\ .

In two steps, we show that (282) cannot be valid.

Step 1. First, we show that function δ\delta cannot be a constant over Φ\Phi (interval specified
above). Suppose the contrary and assume that δ⁡(φ)=δ∗\delta(\varphi)=\delta^{*} for all φ∈Φ\varphi\
in\Phi. Hence, the gradient of δ\delta is zero. Using the definition of δ\delta, this implies that

 ∇𝐳fi​(φ)=∇𝐳f¯i​(φ),∀φ∈Φ.\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\  (283)
 bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)\ ,\quad\forall\varphi\in\Phi\ .

Then, by leveraging (282), we conclude that rp​(ni)=rq​(ni+δ∗)r_{p}(n_{i})=r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
for all ni∈Ωn_{i}\in\Omega. Since rp​(ni)r_{p}(n_{i}) and rq​(ni+δ∗)r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*}) are
analytic functions that agree on an open interval of ℝ\mathbb{R}, they are equal for all ni∈ℝn_{i}\
in\mathbb{R} as well. This implies that pN​(ni)=η⋅qN​(ni+δ∗)p_{N}(n_{i})=\eta\cdot q_{N}(n_{i}+\
delta^{*}) for some constant η\eta. Since pNp_{N} and qNq_{N} are pdfs, the only choice is η=1\eta=
1 and pN​(ni)=qN​(ni+δ∗)p_{N}(n_{i})=q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*}), then pi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))=qi​(zi∣𝐳pa⁡(i))

contradicts the premise that observational and interventional mechanisms are distinct.

Step 2. Finally, we will show that (282) cannot be true when δ\delta is not a constant function
over Φ\Phi. Note that the right-hand side of (282) is not a function of nin_{i}. Then, taking the
derivative of both sides with respect to nin_{i} and rearranging, we obtain

 rp′​(ni)rp​(ni)\displaystyle =rq′​(ni+δ⁡(φ))rq​(ni+δ⁡(φ)),∀(ni,φ)∈Ω×Φ.\displaystyle=\frac

 {r_{p}(n_{i})}               varphi))}\ ,\quad\forall(n_{i},\varphi)\in\Omega\times\Phi\ .

Next, consider a fixed realization ni=ni∗n_{i}=n_{i}^{*}, and denote the value of LHS by α\alpha.

 (n_{i}^{*}+u)}\ ,\quad\forall u\in\Theta\ .

The only solution to this equality is that rq​(ni∗+u)r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u) is an exponential function,
rq​(u)=k1​exp⁡(α​u)r_{q}(u)=k_{1}\exp(\alpha u) over interval u∈Θu\in\Theta. Since rqr_{q} is an

there exists 𝐜∗∈𝒞i\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i} and φ∈ℝ|pa⁡(i)|\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm
{pa}}(i)|} corresponding to which (281) holds is invalid, concluding that for all φ∈ℝ|pa⁡(i)|\

{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=c_{i}. Proving the counterpart identity 𝐜⊤⋅∇𝐳fi​(φ)=ci\mathbf{c}^{\top}\

 {ci∗−(𝐜∗)⊤⋅∇𝐳fi​(φ)=0ci∗−(𝐜∗)⊤⋅∇𝐳f¯i​(φ)=0,∀φ∈ℝ|pa⁡(i)|,\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c^

 forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ ,

which means that we have found a solution to (D.2.2) that is not in ℝn∖𝒞i{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\

Necessary condition.

D.2.3 Proof of Lemma 21

Approach.

We will use the same argument as at the beginning of the proof of Lemma 35. Specifically, we will
show that for any node ii and two-layer NNs fif_{i} and f¯i\bar{f}_{i} with weight matrices 𝐖i\
mathbf{W}^{i} and 𝐖¯i\bar{\mathbf{W}}^{i}, the following continuum of equations admit their
solutions 𝐜\mathbf{c} in ℝn∖𝒞i{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{i}.

 {ci−𝐜⊤⋅∇𝐳f¯i​(φ)=0ci−𝐜⊤⋅∇𝐳fi​(φ)=0,∀φ∈ℝ|pa⁡(i)|,\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c_{i}-\
 mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=0\\ \\ c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\
 nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=0\end{array}\right.\ ,\qquad\qquad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^
 {|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ ,

in which shorthand φ\varphi is used for 𝐳pa⁡(i)\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}. Hence, by invoking Lemma 
35, Assumption 2 holds.

Definitions.

Define di≜|pa⁡(i)|d_{i}\triangleq|{\rm{pa}}(i)|. Since max⁡{rank⁡(𝐖i),rank⁡(𝐖¯i)}=di\max\{\rank(\bW
^i),\rank(\bar\bW^i)\}=d_{i}, without loss of generality, suppose that 𝐖i∈ℝwi×di\mathbf{W}^{i}\in{\
mathbb{R}}^{w_{i}\times d_{i}} has rank did_{i}. The rest of the proof follows similarly for the
case of rank⁡(𝐖¯i)=di\rank(\bar\bW^i)=d_{i}. We use shorthand {𝐖,w}\{\mathbf{W},w\} for {𝐖i,wi}\{\
mathbf{W}^{i},w_{i}\} when it is obvious from context.

Parameterization.

Note that fif_{i} can be represented by different parameterizations, some containing more hidden
nodes than others. Without loss of generality, let ww be the fewest number of nodes that can
represent fif_{i}. This implies that the entries of 𝝂\boldsymbol{\nu} are nonzero. Otherwise, if νi
=0\nu_{i}=0, we can remove ii-th hidden node and still have the same fif_{i}. Similarly, the rows
of 𝐖\mathbf{W} are distinct. Otherwise, if there exist rows 𝐖i=𝐖j\mathbf{W}_{i}=\mathbf{W}_{j} for
distinct i,j∈[w]i,j\in[w], removing jj-th hidden node and using (νi+νj)(\nu_{i}+\nu_{j}) in place
of νi\nu_{i} results the same function as fif_{i} with (w−1)(w-1) hidden nodes. Similarly, we have
𝐖i≠𝟎\mathbf{W}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{0} for all i∈[w]i\in[w]. Otherwise, we have

 νi⋅σ⁡(𝐖i⋅φ)+ν0=(ν0+νi2),\nu_{i}\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{i}\cdot\varphi)+\nu_{0}=(\nu_{0}+\    (295)
 frac{\nu_{i}}{2})\ ,

and by removing ii-th hidden node and using (ν0+νi2)(\nu_{0}+\frac{\nu_{i}}{2}) instead of ν0\nu_
{0}, we reach the same function as fif_{i} with (w−1)(w-1) hidden nodes. Finally, we have 𝐖i+𝐖j≠𝟎\
mathbf{W}_{i}+\mathbf{W}_{j}\neq\boldsymbol{0}. Otherwise, we have

 νi⋅σ⁡(𝐖i​φ)+νj​σ​(𝐖j⋅φ)+ν0=(νi−νj)⋅σ⁡(𝐖i⋅φ)+(ν0+νj),\nu_{i}\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{i}\
 varphi)+\nu_{j}\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{j}\cdot\varphi)+\nu_{0}=(\nu_{i}-\nu_{j})\cdot\sigma(\     (296)
 mathbf{W}_{i}\cdot\varphi)+(\nu_{0}+\nu_{j})\ ,

and by removing jj-th hidden node and using (νi−νj)(\nu_{i}-\nu_{j}) instead of νi\nu_{i} and
(ν0+νj)(\nu_{0}+\nu_{j}) instead of ν0\nu_{0}, we reach the same function as fif_{i} with (w−1)
(w-1) hidden nodes. In summary, we have

 𝐖i≠𝟎,νi≠0,∀i∈[w],and𝐖i±𝐖j≠𝟎,∀i,j∈[w]:i≠j.\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{0}\ ,\;
 \;\nu_{i}\neq 0\ ,\quad\forall i\in[w]\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbf{W}_{i}\pm\mathbf{W}_    (297)
 {j}\neq\boldsymbol{0}\ ,\quad\forall i,j\in[w]:i\neq j\ .

We will show that there does not exist 𝐜∈𝒞i\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i} such that 𝐜⊤⋅∇𝐳fi​(φ)=ci\

{d_{i}}. Assume the contrary, and assume there exist 𝐜∗∈𝒞i\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i} such
that

 (𝐜∗)⊤⋅∇𝐳fi​(φ)=ci∗,∀φ∈ℝdi.\displaystyle(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}  (298)

such that

 (𝐛∗)⊤⋅∇φfi​(ϕ)=ci∗,∀φ∈ℝdi.\displaystyle(\mathbf{b}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\varphi}f_{i}(\   (299)
 phi)=c_{i}^{*}\ ,\quad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}\ .

Based on (263), the gradient of fi​(φ)f_{i}(\varphi) is

 ∇φfi​(φ)=𝐖⊤⋅diag⁡(𝝂)⋅σ˙​(W⋅φ),\displaystyle\nabla_{\varphi}f_{i}(\varphi)=\mathbf{W}^{\top}  (300)
 \cdot{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\dot{\sigma}(W\cdot\varphi)\ ,

where diag⁡(𝝂){\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu}) is the diagonal matrix with 𝝂\boldsymbol{\nu} as its
diagonal elements, and σ˙\dot{\sigma} is the derivative of the sigmoid function, applied
element-wise to its argument. Hence, based on (298), the contradiction premise is equivalent to
having a nonzero 𝐛∗∈ℝdi\mathbf{b}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}} such that

 [σ˙​(𝐖⋅φ)]⊤⋅diag⁡(𝝂)⋅𝐖⋅𝐛∗=ci,∀φ∈ℝdi.\displaystyle\big[\dot{\sigma}(\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi)\
 big]^{\top}\cdot{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{*}=c_{i}\ ,\     (301)
 quad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}\ .

We note that since 𝐖\mathbf{W} is full-rank and νi\nu_{i} is nonzero for all i∈[w]i\in[w], the
matrix diag⁡(𝝂)⋅𝐖{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W} is full-rank as well and it has a
trivial null space. Subsequently, 𝐛∗∈ℝdi\mathbf{b}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}} is nonzero if and
only if (diag⁡(𝝂)⋅𝐖⋅𝐛∗)∈ℝw({\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{*})\in{\
mathbb{R}}^{w} is nonzero. We will use the following lemma to show that (301) cannot be true. This
establishes that the contradiction premise is not correct, and completes the proof.

Lemma 0.

Let 𝐮∈ℝp\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p} have nonzero entries with distinct absolute values, i.e.,
ui≠0u_{i}\neq 0 and |ui|≠|uj||u_{i}|\neq|u_{j}| for all i≠ji\neq j, and α∈ℝ\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}} be
a constant. Then, for every nonzero vector 𝐜∈ℝp\mathbf{c}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}, there exists α∈ℝ\
alpha\in{\mathbb{R}} such that [σ˙​(α​𝐮)]⊤⋅𝐜≠a[\dot{\sigma}(\alpha\mathbf{u})]^{\top}\cdot\mathbf
{c}\neq a.

Proof: See Section D.2.4.

Let us define ξ≜𝐖⋅φ\xi\triangleq\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi. We will show that there exists φ∗∈ℝdi\
varphi^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}} such that ξ=𝐖⋅φ∗\xi=\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi^{*} satisfies the
conditions in Lemma 36. Then, using Lemma 36 with the choice of 𝐮=𝐖⋅φ∗\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{W}\cdot\
varphi^{*}, d=diag⁡(𝝂)⋅𝐖⋅𝐛∗d={\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{*}, and a=
cia=c_{i}, we find that there exists α∈ℝ\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}} such that

 [σ˙​(α⋅𝐖⋅φ∗)]⊤⋅diag⁡(𝝂)⋅𝐖⋅𝐛∗≠ci.\displaystyle\big[\dot{\sigma}(\alpha\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\
 varphi^{*})\big]^{\top}\cdot{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{*}\  (302)
 neq c_{i}\ .

Hence, (301) is false since it is violated for φ=α​φ∗\varphi=\alpha\varphi^{*} and the proof is
completed. We show the existence of such φ∗\varphi^{*} as follows. We first construct the set of φ\
varphi values for which conditions of Lemma 36 on ww are not satisfied. The set in question is the
union of the following cases: (i) ξi=𝐖i⋅φ=0\xi_{i}=\mathbf{W}_{i}\cdot\varphi=0 for some i∈[w]i\in
[w], (ii) |ξi|=|ξj||\xi_{i}|=|\xi_{j}| for some distinct i,j∈[w]i,j\in[w], or equivalently, (𝐖i±𝐖j)
​φ=0(\mathbf{W}_{i}\pm\mathbf{W}_{j})\varphi=0. Note that 𝐖i≠𝟎\mathbf{W}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{0} and
𝐖i±𝐖j≠𝟎\mathbf{W}_{i}\pm\mathbf{W}_{j}\neq\boldsymbol{0} by (297). For a nonzero vector 𝐲∈ℝdi\
mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}, the set {φ∈ℝdi:𝐲⊤​φ=0}\{\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}\ :\ \
mathbf{y}^{\top}\varphi=0\} is a (di−1)(d_{i}-1)-dimensional subspace of ℝdi{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}.
Then, there is ww number of (di−1)(d_{i}-1)-dimensional subspaces that fall under case (i), and w⁡
(w−1)w(w-1) number of (di−1)(d_{i}-1)-dimensional subspaces that fall under case (ii). Therefore,
there are w2w^{2} lower-dimensional subspaces for which the conditions of Lemma 36 do not hold.
However, ℝdi{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}} cannot be covered by a finite number of lower-dimensional
subspaces of itself. Therefore, there exists φ∗∈ℝdi\varphi^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}} such that ξ=
𝐖⋅φ∗\xi=\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi^{*} satisfies the conditions of Lemma 36, and the proof is
completed.

D.2.4 Proof of Lemma 36

Assume the contrary and suppose that there exists a nonzero 𝐜\mathbf{c} and α\alpha for a given 𝐮\
mathbf{u}. Define the function gu​(α)≜𝐜⊤⋅σ˙​(α​𝐮)g_{u}(\alpha)\triangleq\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\dot
{\sigma}(\alpha\mathbf{u}). Note that

 σ˙​(x)=11+e−x+ex\displaystyle\dot{\sigma}(x)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-x}+e^{x}}  (303)

is an even analytic function, and its Taylor series expansion at 00 has the domain of convergence
{x∈ℝ:|x|<π2}\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:|x|<\frac{\pi}{2}\}. Thus, for all α∈(−π2​maxi​|ui|,π2​maxi​|ui|)\
alpha\in(-\frac{\pi}{2\max_{i}|u_{i}|},\frac{\pi}{2\max_{i}|u_{i}|}),

 a=gu​(α)\      =∑j=1pcj⋅σ˙​(α​uj)=∑i=0∞γi​∑j=1pcj​(α​uj)2​i=∑i=0∞(γi​∑j=1pcj​uj2​i)​α2​i.\
 displaystyle a displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}c_{j}\cdot\dot{\sigma}(\alpha u_{j})=\sum_{i=0}^   (304)

                (\gamma_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{p}c_{j}u_{j}^{2i})\alpha^{2i}\ .

Note that gu​(α)g_{u}(\alpha) is a constant function of α∈(−π2​maxi​|ui|,π2​maxi​|ui|)\alpha\in(-\
frac{\pi}{2\max_{i}|u_{i}|},\frac{\pi}{2\max_{i}|u_{i}|}). Thus, its Taylor coefficients, i.e.,
(γi​∑j=1pcj​uj2​i)(\gamma_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{p}c_{j}u_{j}^{2i}), are zero for all i∈ℕ+i\in{\mathbb{N}}^
{+}. However, the coefficients of even powers in Taylor expansion of σ˙\dot{\sigma}, i.e., γi\
gamma_{i}’s, are nonzero (Weisstein, 2002). Therefore, we have ∑i=1p(cj​uj2​i)=0\sum_{i=1}^{p}(c_
{j}u_{j}^{2i})=0 for all i∈ℕ+i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}. Next, construct the following system of linear
equations,

 [u12u14…u12​w…up2up4…up2​w]​[c1cp]=𝟎.\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}u_{1}^{2}&u_{1}^{4}&\dots&

This is equivalent to

 diag⁡([u12,…,up2]⊤)⋅[1u12…u12​(p−1)…1up2…up2​(p−1)]⏟Vandermonde⋅[c1cp]=𝟎.\displaystyle{\rm

 end{bmatrix}=\boldsymbol{0}\ .

Note that the Vandermonde matrix in (306) has determinant ∏1≤i≤j<p(ui2−uj2)\prod_{1\leq i\leq j<p}
(u_{i}^{2}-u_{j}^{2}), which is nonzero since |ui|≠|uj||u_{i}|\neq|u_{j}| for i≠ji\neq j.
Multiplying an invertible matrix with a diagonal invertible matrix generates another invertible
matrix, and 𝐜\mathbf{c} must be a zero vector which is a contradiction. Hence, there does not exist
such 𝐜\mathbf{c} for which 𝐜⊤⋅σ˙​(α​𝐮)\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\dot{\sigma}(\alpha\mathbf{u}) is
constant for every α∈ℝ\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}.

D.3 Assumptions of (Zhang et al., 2023)

We clarify that Assumption 2 of Zhang et al. (2023), which is referred to as “linear interventional
faithfulness”, requires nonlinear SCMs. Subsequently, both of their main results (Theorem 1 and
Theorem 2) require this assumption. First, note that Zhang et al. (2023, p.6) implicitly explain
that their results are for nonlinear SCMs: “In general, we show in Appendix B that a large class of
nonlinear SCMs and soft interventions satisfy this assumption.” Next, we show that this assumption
is violated for linear additive noise models as follows. First, we quote their assumption:

Linear interventional faithfulness

(Zhang et al., 2023, Assumption 2): Intervention II on node ii satisfies linear interventional
faithfulness if for every j∈ch⁡(i)∪{i}j\in{\rm{ch}}(i)\cup\{i\} such that pa⁡(j)∩de⁡(i)=∅{\rm{pa}}
(j)\cap{\rm{de}}(i)=\emptyset, it holds that ℙ⁡(Zj+𝐜⊤​𝐙S)≠ℙI​(Zj+𝐜⊤​𝐙S)\mathbb{P}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^
{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S})\neq\mathbb{P}^{I}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S}) for all constant
vectors 𝐜∈ℝ|S|\mathbf{c}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|S|} where S=[n]∖de⁡(i)S=[n]\setminus{\rm{de}}(i).

Linear SCMs violate linear interventional faithfulness.

Next, we show that linear SCMs violate the above linear interventional faithfulness assumption.
Consider an intervention on node ii, and let j∈ch⁡(i)j\in{\rm{ch}}(i) such that pa⁡(i)∩de⁡(i)=∅{\rm
{pa}}(i)\cap{\rm{de}}(i)=\emptyset. Let

 Zj=𝐰⊤​𝐙pa⁡(j)+Nj,Z_{j}=\mathbf{w}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)}+N_{j}\ ,  (307)

where 𝐰∈ℝ|pa⁡(j)|\mathbf{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(j)|} denotes the weight vector. Note that we
have pa⁡(j)⊆S=[n]∖de⁡(i){\rm{pa}}(j)\subseteq S=[n]\setminus{\rm{de}}(i) since pa⁡(i)∩de⁡(i)=∅{\rm
{pa}}(i)\cap{\rm{de}}(i)=\emptyset. Consider vector 𝐜∈ℝ|S|\mathbf{c}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|S|} such that
𝐜pa⁡(j)=−𝐖\mathbf{c}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)}=-\mathbf{W} and 𝐜S∖pa⁡(j)=𝟎\mathbf{c}_{S\setminus{\rm{pa}}(j)}=
\boldsymbol{0} where 𝐜pa⁡(j)\mathbf{c}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)} denotes the entries corresponding to nodes in
pa⁡(j){\rm{pa}}(j). Then, we have

 Zj+𝐜⊤​𝐙S=Nj,Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S}=N_{j}\ ,  (308)

which remains invariant after an intervention on node ii and yields ℙ⁡(Zj+𝐜⊤​𝐙S)=ℙI​(Zj+𝐜⊤​𝐙S)\
mathbb{P}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S})=\mathbb{P}^{I}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_
{S}). Therefore, linear SCMs violate linear interventional faithfulness.

Appendix E Empirical Evaluations: Details and Additional Results

E.1 Implementation Details of LSCALE-I Algorithm

Preprocessing: Dimensionality reduction.

Since 𝐗=𝐆⋅𝐙\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}, we can compute im⁡(𝐆){\rm im}(\mathbf{G}) using nn
random samples of 𝐗\mathbf{X} almost surely. Specifically, im⁡(𝐆){\rm im}(\mathbf{G}) equals the
column space of the sample covariance matrix of nn samples of 𝐗\mathbf{X}. In LSCALE-I, as a
preprocessing step, we compute this subspace and express samples of 𝐗\mathbf{X} and 𝐬𝐗\mathbf{s}_{\
mathbf{X}} in this basis. This procedure effectively reduces the dimension of 𝐗\mathbf{X} and 𝐬𝐗\
mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}} from dd to nn. Then, we perform steps of LSCALE-I using this nn dimensional
observed data.

Implementation details of Algorithm 1.

In Stage L1 of Algorithm 1, choosing any 𝐡∈col⁡(𝐑𝐗m)\mathbf{h}\in{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^
{m}) suffices. To make our algorithm deterministic, we choose the top eigenvector of col⁡(𝐑𝐗m){\rm
col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}) that corresponds to the largest eigenvalue. In Stage L2, we use a
nonzero threshold λ𝒢\lambda_{\mathcal{G}}, i.e.,

 pa^​(m)≜{i≠m:𝔼⁡[|𝒅𝐙^m​(𝐙^,𝐇^)|i]≥λ𝒢}.\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\triangleq\Big\{i\neq m:{\mathbb{E}}\
 Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{\mathbf{H}})\big|_    (309)
 {i}\Big]\geq\lambda_{\mathcal{G}}\Big\}\ .

In Table 16, we list all λ𝒢\lambda_{\mathcal{G}} values used in the experiments.


Table 16: Choice of thresholding parameter λ𝒢\lambda_{\mathcal{G}} for latent graph estimation.

Experiment perfect scores  noisy scores
 Table 3   0.001           0.1
 Table 6   0.001           0.05
 Table 7   0.0001          0.001
 Table 8   0.001           0.1
 Table 9   0.01            0.5

Implementation details of Algorithm 2.

The latent graph estimation part of Algorithm 2 involves rank tests, i.e., determining the
dimension of the intersection of column spaces. Therefore, we also need a threshold, denoted by
λeigv\lambda_{\rm eigv} to determine the numerical ranks in practice. In experiments reported in
Table 8, we set λeigv=0.01\lambda_{\rm eigv}=0.01.

Using the algorithms of related work.

For the comparisons in Section 7.3, we used the shared sources codes of Squires et al. (2023)^8^8 8
https://github.com/csquires/linear-causal-disentanglement-via-interventions and Zhang et al. (2023)
^9^9 9 https://github.com/uhlerlab/discrepancy_vae.

E.2 Implementation Details of GSCALE-I Algorithm

We perform experiments for the coupled interventions setting, and solve the following optimization
problem in Step G2 of GSCALE-I,

 minh⁡‖𝐃t​(h)−𝐈n×n‖1,1+λ​𝔼​[‖h−1​(h⁡(𝐗))−𝐗‖22].\min_{h}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)-\mathbf
 {I}_{n\times n}\big\|_{1,1}+\lambda{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\|h^{-1}(h(\mathbf{X}))-\mathbf{X}\  (310)
 big\|_{2}^{2}\Big]\ .

where λ>0\lambda>0 is a regularization parameter to ensure injectivity. In the following, first, we
show why this problem is equivalent to solving (83). Then, we describe the computation of the
ground truth score differences for 𝐗\mathbf{X} and discuss other implementation details.

Implementation steps.

We use nsn_{\rm s} samples from the observational environment to compute empirical expectations.
Since encoder hh is parameterized by 𝐇\mathbf{H}, we use gradient descent to learn this matrix. To
do so, we minimize the loss described above in (310). We denote the final parameter estimate by 𝐇∗\
mathbf{H}^{*} and the encoder parameterized by 𝐇∗\mathbf{H}^{*} by h∗h^{*}.

In the simulation results reported in Section 7.2, we set λ=1\lambda=1 and solve (310) using
RMSprop optimizer with learning rate 10−310^{-3} for 3×1043\times 10^{4} steps for n=5n=5 and
4×1044\times 10^{4} steps for n=8n=8. We also use early stopping when the training converges before
the maximum number of steps. Recall that the latent graph estimate 𝒢^\hat{\mathcal{G}} is
constructed using 𝟙​{𝐃⁡(h∗)}\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\}. Similarly to (309), We use a threshold
λ𝒢\lambda_{\mathcal{G}} to obtain the graph from the upper triangular part of 𝐃⁡(h∗)\mathbf{D}(h^
{*}).

Architecture details and hyperparameter selection for image experiments.

In Table 17 and Table 18, we list the architecture details and training hyperparameters used in the
image experiments in Section 7.5. Specifically, Table 17 specifies the autoencoder architecture we
used to estimate the latent variables, and Table 18 details the classification-based score
difference network we trained. Finally, Table 19 lists the first level CNN-based autoencoder we
used for image experiments with 2 balls.

E.3 Experimental Methodology Comparison with DiscrepancyVAE

One major difference between the LSCALE-I algorithm and the publicly available DiscrepancyVAE
implementation is that DiscrepancyVAE tries to learn the intervention effects as an encoding of the
intervention labels. Specifically, for each intervention target, the VAE model learns a vector of
latent intervention targets (a neural network with softmax activation) to denote which latent node
is intervened on. In practice, learning an intervention encoding runs the risk of learning
encodings where different interventions affect the same latent node, consequently, having nodes
that are not affected by any intervention encoding. We demonstrate this phenomenon for
DiscrepancyVAE in Figure 8, plotting the learned intervention embeddings for each single-node
intervention. We observe that despite having exhaustive single-node interventions, the intervention
embedding network fails to learn exhaustive intervention targets/effects. Therefore, the learned
graph is not immediately interpretable as a DAG on nn causal latent variables since the learned
intervention targets (i.e., node labels) are not surjective.

In contrast, in LSCALE-I, the effect of an intervention is estimated using score function
differences. While the distinction may seem small, this approach ensures that the estimated graph’s
nodes are always interpretable since score differences between observational and interventional
environments directly correspond to the intervention targets.

Refer to caption
Figure 8: Intervention encoding learned in the sample run in Figure 3.

interventions that Zhang et al. (2023) plots in their double-node intervention extrapolation
results that have more than 10001000 samples for both single and double-node
environments.sec:discrvae-invert-enc-problem

We see across intervention pairs, score-based intervention extrapolation method leads to a sampling
pattern that is relatively compliant with the actual double node interventional data. However, we
observe that in some pairs, intervention effects are overestimated (particularly for CEBPE+SPI and
ETS2+MAPK1).

E.5 Details of Score Functions Used in the Experiments

Score function of the linear Gaussian model.

For a Gaussian random vector 𝐘∼𝒩⁡(μ,Σ)\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma), the score function of
𝐘\mathbf{Y} is given by

 𝐬𝐘(𝐲)=−Σ−1⋅(𝐲−μ).\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})=-\Sigma^{-1}\cdot(\mathbf{y}-\mu)\ .  (311)

Using the linear model specified in (102), in environment ℰ0\mathcal{E}^{0},

 𝐙=(𝐈n×n−𝐀)−1⋅Ni.\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{A})^{-1}\cdot N_{i}\ .  (312)

Since NiN_{i} is a zero-mean Gaussian random vector and (𝐈n×n−𝐀)−1(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}-\mathbf
{A})^{-1} is a full rank matrix, 𝐙\mathbf{Z} is a zero-mean Gaussian random vector. Hence,

 𝐬(𝐳)=−[Cov(𝐙)]−1⋅𝐳.\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{z})=-\big[{\rm Cov}(\mathbf{Z})\big]^{-1}\cdot\mathbf  (313)
 {z}\ .

Note that the covariance of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} has the following form

 Cov⁡(𝐙)=(𝐈n×n−𝐀)−1⋅diag⁡([σ12,…,σn2]⊤)⋅((𝐈n×n−𝐀)−1)⊤.{\rm Cov}(\mathbf{Z})=(\mathbf{I}_{n\
 times n}-\mathbf{A})^{-1}\cdot{\rm diag}\big([\sigma_{1}^{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}^{2}]^{\top}\   (314)
 big)\cdot\big((\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{A})^{-1}\big)^{\top}\ .

Similarly, the score function of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} in ℰm\mathcal{E}^{m} is given by

 𝐬m(z)=−[Cov(𝐙m)]−1⋅𝐳,∀m∈[n],\mathbf{s}^{m}(z)=-\big[{\rm Cov}(\mathbf{Z}^{m})\big]^{-1}\     (315)
 cdot\mathbf{z}\ ,\qquad\forall m\in[n]\ ,

in which 𝐙m\mathbf{Z}^{m} denotes the latent variables 𝐙\mathbf{Z} in environment ℰm\mathcal{E}^
{m}. Finally, by setting f=𝐆f=\mathbf{G}, Corollary 9 specifies the score differences of 𝐗\mathbf
{X} in terms of 𝐙\mathbf{Z} under different environment pairs as

 𝐬𝐗​(𝐱)−𝐬𝐗m​(𝐱)=(𝐆†)⊤⋅[𝐬⁡(𝐳)−𝐬m​(𝐳)].\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf
 {X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})=\big(\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}\big)^{\top}\cdot\big[\mathbf{s}(\mathbf     (316)
 {z})-\mathbf{s}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]\ .

Score estimation for a linear Gaussian model.

For all environments ℰm∈ℰ\mathcal{E}^{m}\in\mathcal{E}, given nsn_{\rm s} i.i.d. samples of 𝐗\
mathbf{X}, we first compute the sample covariance matrix denoted by Σ^m\hat{\Sigma}^{m}. Then, we
compute the sample precision matrix as

 Θ^m≜(Σ^m)†,\hat{\Theta}^{m}\triangleq(\hat{\Sigma}^{m})^{{\dagger}}\ ,  (317)

which leads to the score function estimate given by

 𝐬^𝐗m(𝐱)≜−Θ^m⋅𝐱.\hat{\mathbf{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})\triangleq-\hat{\Theta}^{m}\     (318)
 cdot\mathbf{x}\ .

Experimental support, please view the build logs for errors. Generated by L A T E xml [LOGO] .

Instructions for reporting errors

We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility
and mobile support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and
rendering, choose any of the methods listed below:

  • Click the "Report Issue" ( ) button, located in the page header.

Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.

Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and
reporting rendering errors we may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML
versions for all readers, because disability should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank
you for your continued support in championing open access for all.

Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML
maintain a list of packages that need conversion, and welcome developer contributions.

We gratefully acknowledge support from our major funders, member institutions, , and all
contributors.
